## What's in the document

- **Purpose:** a chat assistant for a Customer Service Representative (CSR). It covers sign-in, account lookup, explaining why a customer's user can or can't use a feature, plan changes, temporary grants that expire on their own, supervisor approvals, change history, and conversations that survive a reload. Both job posts are on Cloudflare's IAM team, and the posting with the assignment lists RBAC/ABAC, policy engines like Cedar, token lifecycle, and Workers/Durable Objects. So the proposal is aimed at IAM correctness more than chat polish.
- **Guiding principle:** *"The LLM proposes; the policy engine decides."* The model only chooses actions. A fixed set of rules decides whether each action is allowed, based on the signed-in CSR. Every change needs the CSR's confirmation. This is also the answer to the obvious question for AI in IAM: what stops the model from granting something it shouldn't, for example because of text planted in a customer record?
- **Mapping to the assignment's four required parts:** Llama 3.3 on Workers AI; the Agents SDK for the chat, with Workflows for approvals and grant expiry; a chat UI on Pages; stored conversation and account data.
- **Glossary:** about 35 terms, grouped as people and identities, subscriptions and entitlements, authorization and policy, and assistant and coordination.
- **Scenarios:** 24 in Given/When/Then form. 19 are Core and fit roughly 2–3 days; 5 are Stretch (seat-limit downgrade, caller verification, member role change, "what am I allowed to do?", voice).
- **Decisions for you (§7), each with my recommendation:**
  1. Call the human the "CSR" and the AI the "assistant", so "agent" doesn't mean two things.
  2. Use Cloudflare Access for CSR sign-in rather than building a login.
  3. Write a small rule engine first, built so Cedar can replace it later.
  4. Store accounts, grants and the audit log in D1.
  5. Treat the Core scenarios as the definition of done.

## How we'll work (the constitution)

I read the constitution and role docs from your other projects. I'll restate the general principles in this repo's own files, with no names, paths or details from those projects. The roles are orchestrator, Tester → Test Reviewer, Architect → Reviewer, Coder → Code Reviewer, Documentation Writer, and Adversarial Reviewer. The principles are:
- verification spec before code
- tests must fail first, then pass at a final check
- explicit contracts on functions
- glossary-first naming
- a rules register
- evidence that could actually fail.

I suggest a slimmed-down version sized for a 2–4 day project. Questions will come to you in chat with a recommendation. I'll write it once you've ruled on the §7 decisions.

I rewrote `entitlement-agent/tech-docs/entitlement-agent-proposal.md`. Your answers now live there as their own sections:
- a technology glossary (§4.1)
- the domain model (§5)
- authentication and sessions (§6)
- the policy model (§7)
- approvals and expiry (§8)

Short answers below.

## General questions

1. **Tool calling vs. MCP.** The model sees the same thing either way: a list of tools it can call. MCP is a protocol for exposing tools to a *separate* client over a network. Our tools and the code behind them live in the same Worker, so MCP would only add a network hop and a second login boundary. In the hexagonal design, the chat tools are one way into the use cases. An MCP server would be a second way into the same use cases, so I kept it as a stretch item (S29).
2. **Technology glossary:** added in §4.1. Besides your four (Agents SDK, Durable Object, Workflow, Worker), it defines Binding, Workers AI, Hyperdrive, Static assets, Wrangler, Tool calling, MCP and JWT.
3. **"Served from Pages":** Pages is Cloudflare's hosting for static front-end files (HTML/JS/CSS). Cloudflare now steers new projects to having the Worker serve those files itself. I changed the plan to that: one Worker, one origin, for both the UI and the API, which also makes cookie-based login simpler.
4. **SQLite per agent:** yes. Each Agent is a Durable Object, and each instance gets its own private embedded SQLite database. There is one instance per case, and it holds **only** the conversation and pending proposals. Accounts, subscriptions, grants, policies and the audit log go in the main database.
5. **Postgres:** good choice. Workers reach it through Hyperdrive. The job post lists PostgreSQL, and real transactions and unique constraints help with idempotency. The cost: a hosted Postgres for the deployed demo (Neon or Supabase free tier) and a local Docker instance for development.
6. **Who is authenticated:** only the CSR. Customer members are data, not users. Cloudflare Access is Cloudflare's Zero Trust proxy. It logs users in through an identity provider before requests reach the app, then forwards a signed JWT that the Worker checks. So it is also a stateless token; the difference is that Cloudflare issues it instead of our app (§6).

## Domain model and policy set

- **Domain model (§5):** an entity diagram plus the hierarchy. A **Feature** is the unit of entitlement, and Plans and Add-ons are bundles of features. A **Grant** gives one account one feature and always has its own expiry; it never inherits the subscription's. Customer accounts have an account status, a subscription and members, and each member has a role and a status.
- **Policy set:** all the policies evaluated together, plus the rule that combines their results into one decision, versioned as a whole.

## Scenario questions

- **Sessions (A1, A2):** a stateless signed JWT. In a browser it should travel in an **HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict cookie, not a Bearer header**:
  - Browsers can't set an `Authorization` header on a WebSocket, and the chat runs over one.
  - Page scripts can't read an HttpOnly cookie, so an XSS bug can't steal it.
  - S02 is now "every request needs a valid token" (rejected with 401). Authorization failures are a separate 403.
  - The stateless trade-off: a token can't be revoked before it expires, so its lifetime is short (proposed: 30 minutes, renewed on activity).
- **Impersonation (A3):** agreed. Claims typed in chat never change who the request runs as, and we don't build elevation (S05).
- **Lookup (B4):**
  - Reading an account is itself a checked action (`account.read`). S07 is a policy that restricts Enterprise accounts to Tier 2.
  - An account is found by account id, account name, or a member's email. If a name matches several accounts, the assistant asks the CSR to choose.
- **Entitlement questions (B5, B6):** three shapes:
  - one feature: yes/no plus its limit
  - the full list, each entry with where it comes from (plan, add-on or grant)
  - can this member use feature F, and why.
- **Denial reasons (B7, B8):** six, checked in order (§5.3):
  1. account suspended
  2. subscription status
  3. no entitlement
  4. limit reached
  5. member deactivated
  6. member role lacks the permission.

  CSR actions have their own list (§5.4).
- **Upgrade (C9):** no payment. A plan change is a state change plus an audit record.
- **Refusals (C10):** the CSR's role lacks the permission, the account is suspended, the target plan equals the current one, the downgrade is below seats in use, or the account changed after the proposal was made (S15).
- **Policy as a predicate (C11):** yes, exactly. Each policy has an effect (permit, forbid or require approval), a target, a **boolean condition** written as data over principal, resource and context attributes, and a reason. The combining rule:
  - any matching forbid → Deny
  - else any require-approval → RequiresApproval
  - else any permit → Allow
  - else Deny.

  These are Cedar's rules plus approval, so Cedar can replace our evaluator later. The same engine also answers "can this member use F", so one algorithm handles both questions (§7).
- **Grants (D12, D13):** entitlements attach to features. A grant is one feature, one account, with an explicit expiry, capped by policy.
- **Escalation (D14):** yes, it comes from policy. The CSR's role plus context such as the number of days decides whether a grant needs approval.
- **Approvals (D15):** yes, they're asynchronous. Each approval request gets its own Workflow, which waits for the supervisor's decision event or a 72-hour timeout.
  - The supervisor sees pending requests in an Approvals panel, or by asking the assistant.
  - The supervisor decides with a button, and can't approve their own request.
  - The Workflow then re-checks permission, applies or discards the change, and posts the result into the requesting CSR's case. If that CSR is offline, the message is waiting when they reopen it (§8).
- **Access denied (D16):** covered: it's a Deny from the policy check for an action outside the CSR's role (§5.4, S15).
- **Prompt injection (E17):**
  - (a) Yes, every piece of prompt text is tagged by origin: system, CSR, tool data, or untrusted customer text.
  - (b) We can only partly clean data at write time. Schema and length checks help. A classifier such as Llama Guard can *flag* suspicious text but can't guarantee anything. The real protection is that the model has no authority to lose (§9).
- **Human in the loop (E18):** yes, every change a CSR causes needs a UI confirmation tied to one stored proposal. Changes the system makes itself (grant expiry, approval timeout) run under a system identity and are audited.
- **Idempotency (F20):** the proposal id is the idempotency key, so a double confirm applies once. Each proposal records the account version it was built from, so a stale proposal is rejected. Workflow steps are idempotent, and resuming a case never re-runs tool calls.
- **Caller verification (G21):** yes, it authenticates the *caller*, not the CSR. The case records the caller and whether they were verified (for example, with a one-time code), and a policy blocks changes on unverified cases.
- **Member role change (G22):** a different resource. It's a change to a customer member's role, with its own rule that an account keeps at least one Owner. Approval applies only if a policy requires it.

## Two of your rulings conflict with your feedback

- **Decision 2:** you chose (a) Cloudflare Access, but A1 asks for our own sign-in with seeded CSRs and a stateless token. **My recommendation: switch to our own sign-in.** It matches what you described, shows both token issuance and verification, and works the same locally and deployed. Reviewers can log in with demo credentials from the README; with Access, each reviewer would have to be added to a Zero Trust organization. Token checking sits behind an interface, so Access can still be added later.
- **Decision 4:** you chose (a) D1, then raised Postgres. **My recommendation: Postgres through Hyperdrive.**

## Proposed first slice

Slice 1 is S01–S12: sign in, find an account, check and list entitlements, explain why a member can or can't use a feature, and resume a case. It is read-only, but it already covers all four required parts (LLM, Agent coordination, chat UI, memory), so it could be submitted on its own. Slice 2 adds subscription changes (confirmation, idempotency, injection, audit). Slice 3 adds grants and approvals (Workflows). The old scenario numbers changed with this regrouping.

## Next step

Once you rule on decisions 2 and 4 and confirm Slice 1, I'll write the slim constitution, including the hexagonal and clean architecture rules. Then I'll write the verification spec for Slice 1 before any code.

## What changed

**Proposal** (`tech-docs/entitlement-agent-proposal.md`)
- §13 records your rulings: our own sign-in with a stateless token, PostgreSQL through Hyperdrive, and Slice 1 first. The only open item is tool calling vs. MCP.
- I added a scenario: **S04, session renewal and the 8-hour limit**. Session management was missing from Slice 1. Later scenarios shift by one number, so Slice 1 is now S01–S13.
- The glossary gained four terms: grace period, entitlement restriction, remedy, and reason code.

**Constitution** (in `entitlement-agent/`; there's no mention of private projects)

| File | Content |
| --- | --- |
| `CLAUDE.md` | Entry point: how each session starts, the role prefixes (`arch:`, `dev:`, `test:`, `docs:`), hard rules |
| `AI_CONSTITUTION.md` | How you and I work, the quality bar, what counts as evidence, and a list of named rules, each with the test or check that enforces it |
| `AI_WORKFLOW.md` | How a slice gets built, from spec to deploy; roles, review limits, gate commands, where records go |
| `AI_AS_ARCHITECT.md` | Hexagonal and clean architecture: folder layout, dependencies point inward only, naming of interfaces and implementations, every external call behind an interface |
| `AI_AS_DEVELOPER.md` | The stack and TypeScript rules (strict types, branded ids, expected failures returned as values, contracts on functions) |
| `AI_AS_TESTER.md` | One scenario → one test; the test levels; fakes only at interface boundaries |
| `AI_AS_WRITER.md` | Documentation rules |
| `AI_SESSION.md`, `AI_TODO.md` | Where the work stands |

I also added a small `CLAUDE.md` in `clooudflare/` that points to `entitlement-agent/`, so sessions started there pick up the constitution.

**Slice 1 verification spec** (`tech-docs/slices/sign-in-and-explain-access/sign-in-and-explain-access.verification-spec.md`)
- **A complete seed dataset:** 5 features, 4 plans, 1 add-on, 8 accounts covering every account and subscription status, 12 members, 3 CSRs, and the policies in table form.
- **13 scenarios with exact expected results.** The security cases are covered:
  - an altered token
  - `alg: none`
  - a token signed by a foreign key
  - the wrong issuer
  - a WebSocket connection from a foreign origin

- **D5:** connecting to another CSR's case returns `404`, the same as a case that doesn't exist. Otherwise an attacker could tell which case ids exist. This replaces the `403` in the proposal, which I've already updated.

## TypeScript everywhere?

Yes, one language across the project: the React chat UI, the Worker, the case agent, the Workflows, and the database code. Since it's all in one repo, the UI and server can share types too (for example the `/api/me` response). It's the recommended path. The Agents SDK and Workflows are TypeScript/JavaScript libraries, and Workers AI is reached from a Worker through its `env.AI` binding. Workers can also run Python and Rust compiled to WebAssembly, but the agent tooling is TypeScript-first.

## Sign-in sequence diagram

`tech-docs/diagrams/sign-in.sequence.puml` (rendered as `sign-in.svg`), 48 numbered steps in three parts:
1. **Loading the app:** static files are served directly by Cloudflare, without running the Worker. A first `/api/me` call returns 401, so the app shows the sign-in form.
2. **Submitting credentials:**
   - The request passes through each hexagonal layer in turn: the Worker builds its adapters, then the HTTP endpoint, then the use case, then the database code, Hyperdrive, and PostgreSQL.
   - The endpoint checks the request's `Origin`, then the password is verified with PBKDF2.
   - If the email doesn't exist, the password is still checked against a dummy hash, so response time doesn't reveal which emails exist.
   - The JWT is signed with ES256, an audit record is written, and the token goes into the `__Host-session` cookie.
3. **Every later request:** the token is verified without any session lookup in the database.

## Data model

The ER diagram is `tech-docs/diagrams/data-model.er.puml` (rendered as `data-model.svg`). The explanation is in `tech-docs/data-model.md`. All 16 of your entities are in it. I added three you'll want: CSR Credential, Subscription Add-on, and Audit Record.

**Your question: can an Org and a User have N Accounts?**
- An organization **owns** N accounts.
- A user doesn't own accounts. A user is a **member** of N accounts, and an account has M members.
- That membership is its own entity, **Account Membership**, because it carries the user's role and status *in that account*.

Cloudflare's own dashboard works the same way. The account is the center of the model: it subscribes, receives grants, and holds members, and seats are a count of its active memberships.

§10 of the doc has 11 questions, each with a recommendation. These four matter most:
- **Q5, RBAC grants and policies constrain.** Role Permission says what a role may do. Policies only add conditions: forbid, or require approval. This changes the proposal's §7, where policies do both. I recommend the split, because each question then has one answer source.
- **Q8, Case Request.** I treated your Case Request as the old "Proposal": a requested *change* that has to be confirmed and applied. Reads only go to the audit log. Is that what you meant?
- **Q9, names.** `user` and `case` are reserved words in SQL. I recommend calling them **Customer User** and **Support Case** throughout. "User" alone is ambiguous here anyway, since the CSR is the user of the app.
- **Q4, one Role table.** One table with a scope column (CSR roles vs. account member roles). A composite key then makes the database itself refuse a member role on a CSR.

## Policy as an expression string

Yes, it's more readable, and it isn't much extra work if we use an existing expression language instead of writing our own:
- **CEL (Common Expression Language).** Google Cloud IAM Conditions and Kubernetes use it, which fits an IAM role well. Your example would be written as-is: `context.grantDurationDays <= 14 && resource.accountStatus == 'Active'`. TypeScript implementations exist, but I haven't checked them against the Workers runtime yet; a half-day spike would settle that.
- **Writing our own parser** is about a day.
- **JavaScript `eval`** is ruled out: it would be a security hole, and Workers block it anyway.

The real benefit is checking expressions when policies load. Your example has a typo, `grandDurationDays`. A type-checked expression fails at deploy, not silently at runtime. I've added this to the TODO for when we return to policy.

### Assistant

I agree with your model: Organization and Individual are siblings, the account is the operational unit, and an individual can belong to an organization while also having a subscription of their own. Here's how I'd structure it, with the decisions for you to rule on. I haven't changed any files; I'll update `data-model.md` and the ER diagram once you've ruled.

## 1. One table, two tables, or a third option

What you're describing is a well-known pattern (Fowler's *Party*): a supertype with two subtypes. There are three standard ways to store it:

| Option | Shape | Strength | Weakness |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| (a) One table with a kind column | `client` with `client_kind` plus every column of both kinds | Simple queries | Columns that only apply to one kind must allow nulls, so the database can't require e.g. an individual's email; check constraints have to fill the gap |
| (b) Two independent tables | `organization`, `individual` | Each table fully typed | Anything that points to "a client" (accounts, cases, audit records) needs two nullable references plus an "exactly one is set" check, repeated everywhere |
| **(c) Shared supertype + subtype tables** | `client` (id, kind, status) + `organization` and `individual`, each keyed by `client_id` | One reference target for the rest of the model, and typed, required columns per kind | One join to read kind-specific details |

**My recommendation: (c).** An account references one `client_id`, so no other subsystem needs to know which kind it is dealing with. A composite key `(client_id, client_kind)` lets the database guarantee that an `individual` row can only attach to a client whose kind is `Individual`.

## 2. A separate "Organization Member" identity?

**No. A person has exactly one identity, the Individual. Membership is a relationship, not a second identity.**

If Organization Member were its own identity, the same person would exist twice: two emails to keep in sync, and no way to answer "everything about Jane." In IAM terms, two identities for one person is the classic orphaned-account / duplicate-identity problem that identity governance exists to prevent.

So: **Organization Membership** links an individual to an organization, with a status (Active, Removed) and a joined date.

## 3. An individual with a personal subscription who is also an organization member

That is exactly what this structure supports:

```text
Individual: Jane ──owns──▶ Account acc_jane  (Pro)          ← her own subscription
     │
     └──member of──▶ Organization: Acme ──owns──▶ Account acc_acme (Business)
```

One rule needs to be explicit: **entitlements never flow between accounts.** Jane acting in her own account gets Pro features; Jane acting in Acme's account gets Acme's Business features. Her personal Pro plan gives Acme nothing, and Acme's plan gives her personal account nothing. Every entitlement question is asked *in the context of one account*. That's also why the assistant has to ask "which account?" when a person has more than one.

## 4. Where the member's role lives

The membership says *Jane belongs to Acme*. Her role says *Jane is an Admin in account `acc_acme`*. I'd keep these two facts in different subsystems:
- **Client Identity** owns Organization Membership: who belongs to whom. No role.
- **Authorization** (our next subsystem) owns the role assignment: an individual, an account, a role.

**Invariant:** a role in an organization's account requires an active membership in that organization.

Jane's role in her own account is also an explicit row (Owner), so "can Jane use SSO in account X?" always has the same answer path: her role assignment in X. Separating the two costs nothing while each client has one account, and keeps the model correct if that later becomes N.

## 5. Shape the tables for N, constrain them to 1

You simplified two relationships to one: one account per client, and one organization per individual. I'd keep the tables shaped for many and enforce one with a unique constraint:
- `account.client_id` is unique, so each client has one account.
- `organization_membership.individual_id` is unique, so each individual belongs to one organization.

Lifting either limit later means dropping one constraint, with no change to the tables.

## Decisions for you

| # | Question | My recommendation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| C1 | One table, two tables, or supertype + subtypes | **(c) supertype + subtypes** |
| C2 | A separate identity for organization members | **No:** one Individual identity, plus an Organization Membership relationship |
| C3 | Can an individual have their own subscription *and* be an organization member? | **Yes.** Entitlements stay per account and never merge |
| C4 | Where does the member's role live? | **Authorization, on the account.** Membership stays role-free |
| C5 | Must every individual have their own account? | **No: 0..1 for individuals, exactly 1 for organizations.** An employee invited to Acme shouldn't need a personal subscription. The alternative is an automatic free personal account for everyone (GitHub works this way). That gives one simpler rule ("every client has exactly one account"), but then nearly every question about a member becomes "which account?" |
| C6 | The term "Client" | **Use "Customer" instead.** In an IAM project, "client" already means an OAuth client (an application), and reviewers on an IAM team will read it that way. **Customer** = Organization or Individual. |
| C7 | Where suspension lives | **On the account only, for now.** Suspension is operational. A customer-level status (e.g. closed after a fraud review) can come later |

One more point, so it isn't assumed: individuals never sign in to *our* application; only CSRs do. So the Individual is the identity the *product* would authenticate, and we don't model its credentials. That keeps Client Identity small: no passwords, no sessions, just who the customer is and how people relate to organizations.

If you agree with C1–C7, or tell me what to change, I'll update the data-model document and the ER diagram for this subsystem. Then we move to Employee Identity.

Noted: C1–C4, C6 and C7 are ruled. I'll write them into the data-model document when we close this subsystem, since we're still discussing. Answers to your open points:

## C5: does a member without their own account have an identity?

**Yes. Every person has an identity, the Individual. What's optional is an *account* of their own.**

That's the distinction your model already makes: the identity is *who someone is*; the account is *the operational unit that subscribes and uses the product*. Here are the combinations:

| Person | Individual identity | Own account | Member of Acme | Role in `acc_acme` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Jane: personal subscriber and Acme employee | yes | yes, `acc_jane` (Pro) | yes | Admin |
| Bob: Acme employee only | yes | **no** | yes | Viewer |
| Ana: freelancer, no organization | yes | yes, `acc_ana` (Free) | no | — |
| Acme itself (an Organization) | yes, of kind Organization | exactly one, `acc_acme` | — | — |

So C5 is only about the "Own account" column for individuals:
- **0..1 (my recommendation):** Bob has no account. He reaches the product only through his role in Acme's account.
- **Always 1:** Bob would automatically get a Free personal account.

Either way, Bob has an identity. An individual whose membership has ended and who has no account still keeps their identity, because audit records and old cases point to it.

## 2: one individual with several roles in an organization

Good instinct, and it's common: Jane might be both *Admin* and *Billing* at Acme. Because the role lives in the role assignment (C4), not in the membership, supporting it only means the role assignment's key is (account, individual, role) instead of (account, individual). Jane then has two rows, and her permissions are the union of both roles.

It's your point 5 again: shape the table for N now. We can still limit it to one role per person by a rule, if we want simpler explanations. This decision belongs to Authorization, so I'll raise it formally when we get there.

## 3: acting on her own behalf or on Acme's — how is the context set?

You've got it: **the context is an account, and it is given with each request, never inferred from the person.** There's no "acting as" column on the identity. Jane doesn't *become* Acme; she acts *in* Acme's account.

**In the product itself** (how a SaaS would do it), every request names its account. Cloudflare's own API does exactly this: `/accounts/{account_id}/…`. The server then:
1. checks that Jane has a role in that account (otherwise 403)
2. authorizes with *that* role
3. applies *that* account's entitlements.

Jane switching between her account and Acme's is choosing a different `account_id` per request (an account picker in the UI).

**In our application** the CSR acts, not Jane, so the context arrives at a different moment:
- **When the CSR identifies the account**, it's stored on the case as the *account in focus* (`support_case.account_id`). From then on, every question in the case ("can Jane use SSO?") is evaluated against that account.
- **If it isn't known yet** and Jane has more than one account, the assistant asks: "Jane has her own account (Pro) and is a member of Acme Corp (Business). Which one is this about?" The answer sets the account in focus.
- **When Jane is the caller**, the case also records her as the caller. Later this gives us a useful rule: a caller can only request changes to an account where their role allows it. For example, only Acme's Owner or Billing members may ask for a plan change on Acme's account. That's where caller verification (a stretch scenario) would connect.

**What this means for the model:** every question about a person and the product is a *(individual, account)* pair. There's no such thing as "Jane's entitlements" without an account: entitlements belong to accounts, roles to (individual, account), and the case carries the account in focus.

## Does it still work if an individual has several accounts within one organization?

Yes. That's exactly why the account is given with each request instead of being attached to the person. Say Acme owns `acc_acme_prod` and `acc_acme_staging`, and Jane is Admin in one and Viewer in the other:

```text
GET /accounts/acc_acme_prod/...      → Jane's role in acc_acme_prod    (Admin)  + that account's entitlements
GET /accounts/acc_acme_staging/...   → Jane's role in acc_acme_staging (Viewer) + that account's entitlements
```

Nothing in the design changes. In the data, you drop the unique constraint on `account.customer_id` (the shape-for-N point from before). Authorization was already per (individual, account).

## Shouldn't the accounts go in the session token?

I'd recommend against it. **The token should say *who* you are. The request says *where* you're acting. What you may do there is looked up on each request.**

Putting the account list (or roles per account) in the token has three problems:

1. **Stale authority.** If Jane is removed from Acme, her token still lists Acme until it expires. With a stateless token, that's up to 30 minutes of access she shouldn't have, and nothing we can revoke. A per-request lookup makes the removal take effect on the very next request.
2. **Size.** Someone with many accounts gets a huge token, and cookies have size limits.
3. **Disclosure.** The token reveals every account the person belongs to, to anything that sees the token.

A middle option is an "active account" claim in the token. It still goes stale, and every account switch means re-issuing the token. That's extra complexity for no benefit.

**Where an account *does* belong in a token:** a token that *is* a grant. Cloudflare API tokens, for example, are scoped to specific accounts. That token's purpose is to delegate access to those accounts, and the server can revoke it. That's a different product from a sign-in session.

### This catches something in our own design

Our CSR session token carries the CSR's `role` (spec decision D4). By the argument above, a role change or demotion lingers for up to 30 minutes. I accepted that trade-off earlier, but it contradicts the principle I'm recommending now. **My recommendation: the token carries identity only (`sub`, `auth_time`, …), and the CSR's role is loaded on each request.** The cost is one indexed read per request, and almost every request already goes to the database anyway. If you agree, I'll update D4 and proposal §6 when we write things up.

## Is the CSR selecting the account explicitly?

Yes. **The account in focus is always explicit and visible, and never silently guessed by the LLM:**

- **Set through a tool, not by the model.** When the CSR's lookup resolves to exactly one account, that account becomes the case's account in focus, and the assistant says so ("Working on Acme Corp — `acc_acme`"). When there are several candidates (Jane's own account vs. Acme's, or two Acme accounts), the assistant asks and the CSR picks.
- **Always on screen.** The chat UI shows the account in focus in a header next to the case, so the CSR knows the context before every question.
- **Changed only on request.** Switching to another account is an explicit step, and it's recorded in the audit log.
- **Repeated in every change.** A change request (Slice 2 onward) stores the account id in its parameters, and the confirmation shows it again ("Upgrade **Acme Corp (`acc_acme`)** to Pro?"). So even if the conversation drifted, what gets confirmed is unambiguous.

The case's account in focus plays the same role for the CSR that the `/accounts/{id}/` path plays in the product: the context is named explicitly, not derived from who's asking.

### Assistant

You're right to push back. My last answer mixed two different situations. Let me separate them, then lay out the role question in enough detail for you to judge it.

## Two different situations

| | **The product** (the SaaS our customers use) | **Our application** (the CSR tool) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Who signs in | Jane | The CSR |
| Whose token | Jane's | The CSR's |
| Which accounts the session may use | Accounts where Jane has a role: a short, specific list | Any customer account, subject to policy (for example, Tier 1 cannot read Enterprise accounts) |

The `/accounts/{id}/…` example and the "account list in the token" question belong to **the product**. We aren't building that; Jane never signs in to our application. I used it only to show how account context works in general.

In **our application**, the authenticated identity is the CSR, and a CSR's authority over accounts doesn't come from a list. It comes from **role + policy + the account's attributes**. "May Alex work on `acc_umbrella`?" depends on Umbrella's plan (Enterprise), so the check needs the account's data anyway, and no token could list "every account Alex may touch."

The check doesn't cost an extra lookup. Every question reads the account, and the authorization decision uses the attributes that same read returns:

```text
CSR asks about Umbrella
  → load account acc_umbrella (one query: status, plan, subscription …)   ← needed to answer anyway
  → PDP: role from session + account attributes → Allow / Deny
  → answer, or a refusal with its reason
```

So for **accounts**, the question "token or database?" never comes up. What remains is your second point: **the CSR's own role**.

## The CSR's role: token or database? The details

### A wrinkle that changes the picture: the chat is a WebSocket

The token is checked once, when the WebSocket connection opens. After that, chat messages travel over the open connection **without the cookie**. So the agent works from whatever it learned at connection time, and a connection can stay open for hours.

```text
15:00  CSR connects ── cookie verified ── agent records: csr_alex, Tier1, token exp 15:30
15:05  message → tool → decision uses the role recorded at 15:00
15:10  a supervisor demotes or disables Alex in the database
15:20  message → tool → still uses the role recorded at 15:00      ← stale
16:45  message → …    ← token expired at 15:30, but the socket is still open
```

So "role in token" alone isn't enough, whichever option we choose. **The connection itself must end when the token expires.** The agent closes the socket at `exp`. The UI renews the session over HTTP (WebSocket messages can't set cookies) and reconnects. With that rule, staleness is bounded: a role change takes effect at the latest at the next renewal, which happens within about 15 minutes for an active CSR, and at most 30 minutes when the token expires.

### What each option costs

Our database lives in one region, while the Worker runs near the user. Each database round trip costs the network distance (from ~5 ms nearby to ~100 ms far away). Hyperdrive saves the connection setup, not that distance. The query itself (a lookup by primary key) takes under a millisecond.

| | Role from the token | Role read from the database |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Extra round trip per decision | none | one, unless folded into the query that loads the account |
| Role or status change takes effect | at the next renewal, within ~15 min (at most 30) | on the next request |
| Disabled CSR | keeps working until the next renewal | stopped on the next request |

### My recommendation: freshness by sensitivity

This follows your instinct that it depends on how sensitive the action is:

| Kind of decision | Role taken from | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Reads** (Slice 1: lookups, entitlement checks, explanations) | **the token**, recorded at connection time, with the socket closed at `exp` | Frequent and low risk. Staleness is bounded by the renewal interval. No extra round trip. |
| **Changes** (Slice 2 onward: confirmations, grants, approval decisions) | **the database**, re-read at the moment of decision, including whether the CSR is still `Active` | Rare and sensitive. One extra round trip is nothing next to a human confirmation, and a demoted or disabled CSR can never apply a change. |

That keeps D4 as it is (role in the token) and adds two rules:
1. **The WebSocket connection never outlives its token.**
2. **State-changing decisions re-read the principal's current role and status from the database.**

I withdraw the "identity-only token" recommendation from my last message. It was right in principle but heavier than this project needs.

If it would help to see it, I can draw this as a PlantUML sequence diagram once we finish Customer Identity: connect, a chat message, renewal and reconnect, and a change re-reading the role. It belongs to the sign-in and session scenarios we'll design later.

---

## What the diagram shows

- **`customer`** is the shared parent table (C1), with **`organization`** and **`individual`** as its two kinds. Each kind table has a composite foreign key `(customer_id, customer_kind)`, so the database refuses an `individual` row attached to an Organization customer.
- **`organization_membership`** links an individual to an organization, with a status and no role (C2, C4). It references the `organization` and `individual` tables directly, so only an individual can be a member, and only of an organization.
- **`account`** belongs to a customer, at most one per customer for now (`UNIQUE (customer_id)`, C5). Suspension lives on the account (C7).
- **`role_assignment`** is drawn in grey because it belongs to Authorization. It's there to show how the subsystems connect, along with the rule that a role in an organization's account requires an active membership.

## Have we covered everything?

The core of the model, yes. Drawing it in detail surfaced choices we hadn't discussed. I made a provisional call on each; please confirm or change them:

| # | Choice | What I drew | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| G1 | **Membership history** | Its own id (`membership_id`), plus "one *Active* membership per individual" (`UNIQUE (individual_id) WHERE membership_status = 'Active'`) | Jane can leave Acme, join Globex, or rejoin Acme, and every past membership is kept. With the key `(organization, individual)`, rejoining would overwrite the old row and moving to another organization would be blocked. |
| G2 | **Email is an attribute, not the identity** | `customer_id` is the stable identity. Email is unique (ignoring case) and can change | People change emails; references to the person stay valid. |
| G3 | **"An organization has exactly one account"** | Enforced by creating the organization and its account in the same transaction | A plain foreign key can enforce "at most one" but not "at least one". |
| G4 | **"Every customer has its kind row"** | Same: created together in one transaction | The composite key guarantees the kind *matches*, but not that the kind row *exists*. |
| G5 | **Customer lifecycle** | No status on the customer, and customers are never deleted | Accounts carry suspension (C7). Cases and audit records will point at customers, so deleting one would break history. |
| G6 | **Personal data** | Names and email live only in `individual`. Other subsystems store ids | Limits where personal data spreads; audit records reference ids, not emails. |
| G7 | **Organization domain** | Optional and unique, used for lookup only | No automatic membership by email domain; that's a product feature we don't need. |

## Left out on purpose

- **Individual credentials:** customers never sign in to our application.
- **Invitations** (a pending membership state).
- **Links to an external identity provider.**
- **Organization hierarchies** (subsidiaries).
- **Addresses and billing contacts.**
- **Several organizations per individual, or several accounts per customer:** the tables already allow it, and only a constraint stops it (your point 5).

If you confirm G1–G7, I'll write Customer Identity into `data-model.md` with your rulings (C1–C7, C5 = 0..1), update the full ER diagram, and record the two session rules (reads use the role from the token; changes re-read it from the database) for the sign-in design. Then we move to Employee Identity.

I've redrawn the diagram with your change (`tech-docs/diagrams/customer-identity.puml` and `.svg`). I also started `tech-docs/production-readiness.md`, which lists concerns, not solutions. It has a cross-cutting section (personal data at rest, lookup over encrypted data, access to production data, breach response, privacy rights vs. immutable audit, retention, residency) and a Customer Identity section. We'll add to it for each subsystem.

## Every individual has an account: I agree

Your design is better, for the reason you gave. It makes **the account the operational identity**: what *acts*, and what is *acted on*. Attribution then always reads "account X did Y in account Z", and the person behind it (name, email) stays in the identity tables. That also helps privacy: logs and audit records carry account ids, not personal data. It's the model GitHub uses (personal accounts and organization accounts).

What changed in the diagram:
- **`customer` 1 — 1 `account`** for both kinds (this reverses C5's 0..1).
- **`account.operating_mode`**: `Independent` or `OrganizationOnly`. This is the "can operate on their own" flag you described. An organization's account is always `Independent` (enforced by a database check).
- **`role_assignment`** now links **two accounts**: the member's account and the target account, plus a role. The individual no longer appears in it.
- **The account carries `customer_kind`**, so Authorization can require that the member account belongs to an Individual.
- **`customer.customer_status`**: `Active` or `Removed` (your G5).
- **`individual.email_normalized`** is unique, next to `email` as entered (your G2).

## Points this raises

**A1 — The account now has two jobs.** An individual's account is an *actor*, and also a *container* when it's `Independent`. An organization's account is only ever a container, because an organization doesn't act; its members do. That's fine, but the glossary must say it plainly, or "account" will be read two ways.

**A2 — What `OrganizationOnly` means in practice.** I'd define it as: the account holds no subscription and receives no grants; it acts only through its role assignments in organization accounts. The Entitlement subsystem then enforces "a subscription requires an `Independent` account."
- *Question:* if Bob leaves Acme, his account stays (attribution history) and can do nothing. Should that be allowed, or should leaving an organization switch the account to `Independent`?
- *My recommendation:* allow it, and switch it only when he subscribes. That keeps leaving and subscribing as two separate, explicit events.

**A3 — Removing a customer.** When `customer_status` becomes `Removed`, its account must stop working. There are two ways:
- **(a) Compute it:** a removed customer's account loses everything, with its own reason code, `customer-removed`.
- **(b) Suspend it:** removing the customer also suspends the account, in the same transaction.

*My recommendation:* (a). The CSR's explanation then says *why*: "the customer was removed", not just "the account is suspended". And there are no two status fields that could disagree.

**A4 — Your own account (for Authorization).** Is Jane an implicit Owner of her own `Independent` account, or does she get an explicit self-assignment (her account → her account, Owner)? The explicit row gives Authorization one rule for everything; the implicit version avoids a row that looks odd. This belongs to Authorization, so the diagram leaves it open.

## Email normalization

This needs to be a single building block: one `normalizeEmail` function, used both when storing and when looking up. If those two paths diverge, lookups miss.

1. Trim surrounding whitespace.
2. Unicode NFC normalization.
3. Lowercase the whole address. Strictly, the part before the `@` is case-sensitive under RFC 5321, but in practice providers treat it as case-insensitive, and treating it that way avoids near-duplicate identities.
4. Convert an internationalized domain to its ASCII (punycode) form.
5. **No provider-specific rules** (Gmail's dots and `+tags`). Those could merge two different people. That's listed as a production concern.

The address as entered is kept in `email` for display, and uniqueness is enforced on `email_normalized`.

## Recorded

G1, G3, G4, G6 and G7 are agreed. G2 is agreed, with normalization added. G5 adds a customer status. And: concerns get documented without solutions. Once you rule on A2 and A3 (A4 waits for Authorization), I'll write Customer Identity into `data-model.md` and we move to Employee Identity.

### Assistant

## A2 and A3 come down to one question: how do we know whether an account can operate?

These two need the same answer, so I'll treat them together.

**A3, your question: no. Under (a), the account's own status is not changed.** `account_status` records an *operational* decision: someone suspended the account (fraud, abuse, non-payment) and can lift it. Removing the customer is a different event. If removal also set the account to `Suspended`, we'd lose information: if the customer were reinstated, we couldn't tell whether the account had also been suspended for some other reason. So each event keeps its own record, and the account's **effective state** is computed from them.

**A2 works the same way.** When Bob leaves Acme, his membership becomes `Removed` (already recorded) and his `OrganizationOnly` account stays. Storing a status on the account as well would record the same fact twice, and the two copies could drift: someone rejoins and the account status isn't reset, or the reverse. Computing it instead gives you the "Removed or something like that" state you're after.

### Proposal: one computed effective state per account

Computed in one place (a single building block), and used by Entitlement and Authorization:

| Effective state | When (first match wins) | Reason code | Can the account operate? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `CustomerRemoved` | `customer_status = Removed` | `customer-removed` | No |
| `Suspended` | `account_status = Suspended` | `account-suspended` | No |
| `Dormant` | `OrganizationOnly` and no active organization membership | `account-dormant` | No: nothing to act through |
| `Active` | otherwise | — | Yes |

What the CSR sees: *"Bob's account is dormant: he left Acme Corp on 2026-09-30 and has no subscription of his own."* That's the information a stored "Removed" status would give, without a second copy of the facts.

I suggest `Dormant` rather than `Removed`, because "Removed" already means something else for customers and memberships. Reusing it on the account would give one word two meanings.

**What changes:** suspension stays on the account (C7), and the account's effective state is always computed. If Bob subscribes, his account becomes `Independent` and therefore `Active`. If he rejoins Acme, it becomes `Active` again with no update needed.

## A4: minors and dependents

That's a real case, and it settles A4. A minor's account needs someone else (a guardian) with authority over it, and possibly *limited* rights for the minor in their own account.

That's an argument for **explicit role assignments everywhere, including on your own account**:
- Jane → `acc_jane`: Owner
- guardian → `acc_minor`: Owner
- minor → `acc_minor`: Dependent, a restricted role.

With an implicit owner, the minor would automatically have full rights over their own account, which is exactly wrong.

Authorization then has **one rule**: what an account may do in another account (or in itself) is whatever its role assignments say. The invariant becomes "a role assignment needs a relationship that justifies it":

| Member → target | Justified by |
| --- | --- |
| self (same account) | always: own account |
| individual → organization's account | an active organization membership |
| individual → another individual's account | a **guardianship**: a future relationship in Customer Identity |

**My recommendation:**
- Make own-account roles explicit.
- Keep the role assignment's target open to any account, so the tables already fit guardianship.
- **Don't build guardianship now.** I'll add it to the production-readiness list (dependents, guardians, consent, what happens when a minor comes of age).

We'll formally settle this when we design Authorization.

## Email

Agreed. The `normalizeEmail` rules go into the write-up.

---

Once you confirm the computed effective state, with `Dormant` as the name, I'll write Customer Identity into `data-model.md`. That will include the rulings, the effective-state table, email normalization, and the A4 direction for Authorization, plus guardianship in the production-readiness list. Then we move to Employee Identity.

### Assistant

Agreed. It's fail-safe design: the one column every query already checks should be enough to block an inoperable account, so any code that forgets to look at the customer or the memberships is still stopped. That's a better default than relying on every reader to compute the state correctly.

That leaves my concern from before: if the status is overwritten, we lose *why* the account is blocked. A customer reinstated after removal would come back `Active` even if the account had also been suspended for fraud. A small addition handles both:

## Proposal: account status plus holds

```text
account_status : Active | Suspended          ← the single fail-safe column every query checks

account_hold                                 ← why it is suspended; one row per cause
  hold_id
  account_id
  hold_reason : OperationalSuspension | CustomerRemoved | NoActiveMembership
  placed_at, placed_by
  released_at, released_by
```

**Invariant:** `account_status = Suspended` exactly when the account has at least one unreleased hold. Placing or releasing a hold updates the status **in the same transaction**.

How it plays out:

| Event | Hold | Account status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A CSR suspends Acme for abuse | place `OperationalSuspension` | `Suspended` |
| Acme's customer is removed | place `CustomerRemoved` | stays `Suspended` |
| The customer is reinstated | release `CustomerRemoved` | **stays `Suspended`**: the abuse hold is still there |
| Bob leaves Acme (his `OrganizationOnly` account) | place `NoActiveMembership` | `Suspended` |
| Bob rejoins, or subscribes and becomes `Independent` | release `NoActiveMembership` | `Active` |

This gives you what you wanted:
- **One column decides whether an account can operate.** Code that only looks at the account is safe.
- **Every cause is kept**, with who placed it and when.
- **The CSR's explanation uses the holds as reason codes:** *"Acme's account is suspended: operational suspension placed by blair on 2026-10-01, and the customer was removed on 2026-10-20."* Holds are also naturally auditable.

It also replaces the computed effective state from my last message. `Dormant` becomes the `NoActiveMembership` hold.

**One cost to name:** the invariant spans rows (the status column vs. the hold rows), so a plain check constraint can't enforce it. The guard is a single place that changes holds: one use case, one transaction. A verification test that checks the invariant across the seeded data backs it up. A database trigger is the alternative; I'd keep the rule in application code, where it's visible and testable.

## Recorded

A4 is accepted: role assignments are explicit everywhere, including on your own account; the target can be any account; guardianship is a production-readiness item. Email normalization is agreed.

If you accept holds, Customer Identity is complete, and I'll write it into `data-model.md` and the diagram. Then we move to Employee Identity.

### Assistant

Here's my opening proposal for **Employee Identity**, in the same shape as Customer Identity. It's for discussion; I'll document and draw it once we agree. (I haven't committed anything to git; I'll wait until you ask.)

## What this subsystem covers

The people who work for the provider and sign in to our application, and how they prove who they are. **Their roles belong to Authorization**, just as customer roles do. This subsystem only says *who* an employee is and *whether they can sign in*.

## Proposed entities

```text
employee
  employee_id        emp_…            (stable identity)
  email              as entered
  email_normalized   unique           (same normalizeEmail as customers)
  given_name, family_name
  employee_status    Active | Suspended | Terminated
  created_at

employee_credential
  credential_id      crd_…
  employee_id        → employee
  credential_kind    Password          (unique per employee and kind)
  password_hash, salt, iterations, algorithm
  created_at, replaced_at
```

## The decisions behind it

**E1 — "Employee" is the identity; "CSR" is a role.** I'd name the entity `employee`, not `csr`. A CSR is an employee who holds a CSR role (Tier 1, Tier 2, Supervisor), and that role lives in Authorization. This mirrors what we did for customers: identity here, roles there. It also leaves room for other kinds of staff (an administrator, an auditor) without a new identity table. "CSR" stays the everyday term in the product and the UI.

**E2 — No account for employees.** You introduced the customer account for attribution and because customers act in operational containers. Employees don't hold subscriptions or act inside a container, and the employee is already the operational actor. Attribution by `employee_id` keeps personal data out of logs just as attribution by account does. So Attribution would record a principal that is one of three kinds: **account** (customer side), **employee**, or **system**.

**E3 — Credentials in their own table, shaped for many.** One row per credential kind (only `Password` for now). This gives three things:
- Profile queries never load the hash.
- Rotation keeps history (`replaced_at`).
- Adding a passkey or a second factor later adds rows, not columns. That's "shape for many, constrain to one" again.

**E4 — Status without holds.** `Active`, `Suspended` (temporary, e.g. leave or an investigation) and `Terminated` (final, offboarded). Only `Active` can sign in. I'd skip the hold pattern here: employee status changes are rare, the cause goes in the audit record, and `Terminated` is never lifted. If you'd rather be consistent with accounts, an `employee_hold` table is a small addition.

**E5 — Employee and customer identities are separate, even for the same person.** If a CSR also has a personal subscription, they have one employee identity and one customer identity, with no shared "person" table. Workforce identity and customer identity are deliberately separate domains (different lifecycles, different trust, different identity providers in production). It also sets up a useful policy for later: **a CSR must not work on a case about their own customer account**, a conflict-of-interest rule. Detecting it needs a link between the two identities, which I'd leave to production-readiness.

**E6 — No reporting lines or teams for now.** Approvals go to *any* supervisor, by role, never to the requester. Nothing in the scenarios needs "who reports to whom". Assigning work by team or region would be a production-readiness item.

**E7 — Seeded, not managed.** Employees and their credentials are seeded, with no registration and no admin UI. Hiring, offboarding and password resets are production concerns.

**E8 — The system principal is not an employee.** It's a fixed principal kind in Attribution, so automated actions (grant expiry, approval timeout) are never attributed to a person.

## Questions for you

| # | Question | My recommendation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| E1 | Name the entity `employee`, with CSR as a role? | Yes |
| E2 | Employees act as themselves, with no account? | Yes; principal kinds: account, employee, system |
| E3 | Credentials in their own table, one row per kind? | Yes |
| E4 | Employee status without holds? | Yes: `Active`, `Suspended`, `Terminated` |
| E5 | Separate identities even when an employee is also a customer? | Yes; the conflict-of-interest link goes to production-readiness |
| E6 | No reporting lines or teams? | Yes, for now |

For production-readiness I'd also list:
- multi-factor authentication and passkeys
- federation with the company's workforce identity provider
- password policy and breach-password checks
- lockout and rate limiting
- joiner-mover-leaver processes
- periodic access reviews.

Your thoughts?

## One file per subsystem, plus a common file?

Yes, I'd go with your layout:

```text
tech-docs/adr/
  README.md               ← index: id, title, status, date
  common.md               ← decisions that touch more than one subsystem
  customer-identity.md
  employee-identity.md
  authorization.md
  …
```

The usual alternative is one file per ADR (`0001-…md`). It gives each decision its own git history and link, but it scatters the reasoning across dozens of small files. Yours keeps a subsystem's decisions together, readable in order, and matches how we're designing.

**Numbering:** with several files, a global sequence makes ids hard to place. I'd use **a prefix per file**: `CI-1`, `CI-2` (Customer Identity), `EI-1` (Employee Identity), `AZ-1` (Authorization), `PR-1` (Product), `EN-1` (Entitlement), `SU-1` (Support), `AT-1` (Attribution), `PO-1` (Policy), and `CM-1` (Common). An id then says where its decision lives, and ids never collide.

**One home per decision:** each subsystem document's "Rulings" section is replaced by links to its ADRs. Otherwise the same decision would be written twice and the copies would drift.

## The template

Your template is solid. I'd suggest four additions and one rule:

```markdown
## CI-1. Customer as supertype with subtype tables

**Status:** Accepted · 2026-10-23 · decided by the owner
**Decision:** …
**Context:** what, when, why, where it applies
**Options considered:** each option, and why it was or wasn't chosen
**Rationale:** how the relevant quality attributes are affected
**Consequences:** what becomes easier, what becomes harder, what follow-up it creates
**Examples:** …
**Related:** CI-3, EN-2, data-model/customer-identity.md §2
**Amendments:**
- 2026-11-02 — clarification: …
```

1. **Status line: status, date, who decided.** Statuses are `Proposed`, `Accepted`, and `Superseded by XX-n`. Who decided matters: our constitution requires rulings to be dated and attributed, and it lets an ADR the assistant proposes stay visibly *Proposed* until you rule.
2. **Options considered.** Most of our discussions were "(a), (b) or (c)". The rejected options and the reasons are often what a later reader needs most, especially for a choice like C1 that you said may be revisited.
3. **Consequences, in both directions.** Positive and negative, so the trade-off is on record. For example: the supertype needs one join to read kind-specific columns.
4. **Related.** Links to other ADRs and to the model documents.

**The rule for amendments:** an amendment may *clarify* or *extend* a decision. If it *changes* the decision, it becomes a new ADR, and the old one is marked `Superseded by` the new one. Otherwise the decision text and its amendments would contradict each other.

## Which decisions become ADRs

Not every ruling deserves an ADR. Details like "`primary_domain` is optional" stay in the model document. Here's my proposed first batch:

**Customer Identity**

- **CI-2:** one identity per person; membership is a relationship (C2)
- **CI-3:** the account is the operational identity; one per customer, with an operating mode (C5)

- **CI-5:** account status as fail-safe, with holds for each cause (A2, A3)
- **CI-6:** the term "Customer" (C6)

### Assistant

## The email as entered: is it worth keeping?

Not much, honestly. It does two things:
1. **Display.** "Jane.Doe@Acme.com" instead of "jane.doe@acme.com", which is cosmetic. Also internationalized domains: the normalized form is `xn--…` (punycode), which is unreadable. But punycode converts back to Unicode exactly, so the display can be rebuilt from the normalized form.
2. **Delivery.** Strictly, the part before the `@` is case-sensitive, so sending to the exact address entered is the most faithful. In practice, mail providers ignore case.

Neither is worth a second column in this project. **My recommendation: keep only `email` (normalized), and convert the domain back to Unicode for display.** This also changes Customer Identity (G2) and CM-8 ("the address as entered is kept for display"), so I'd make the same change there, and put delivery fidelity on the production-readiness list.

## Iterations: part of the algorithm definition?

Yes. There's a standard for exactly that: the **PHC string format** (from the Password Hashing Competition), which libraries for Argon2, bcrypt and PBKDF2 use. One self-describing string carries the algorithm, its parameters, the salt and the hash:

```text
$pbkdf2-sha256$i=100000$<salt>$<hash>
```

So `employee_credential` needs one column, `password_hash`, instead of four (`password_hash`, `salt`, `iterations`, `algorithm`). Changing parameters or algorithms later needs no schema change. Each row records how it was hashed, so a password can be re-hashed with stronger settings the next time that employee signs in. **I recommend it.**

## E1: role as permissions, or role as business function?

Good distinction. They are two concepts, and IAM vocabulary has a name for each:

| Concept | Meaning | Lives in |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Job function** | The business function: `Tier1Representative`, `Tier2Representative`, `Supervisor` | Employee Identity, as an attribute of the employee (like an HR attribute) |
| **Role** | A named bundle of permissions | Authorization |

What you describe is the identity-governance pattern called **birthright access**:
- **A job function maps to default roles.** Every Tier 1 CSR automatically gets the "Tier 1 CSR" role.
- **Per-employee customization** comes on top:
  - **another role** for that employee (preferred: roles are reviewable and reusable), or
  - **a direct permission** granted to that employee. This is possible, but identity-governance practice discourages it, because direct grants are hard to review. I'd allow it only with a reason and an expiry.

One limit worth stating now: customization **adds** permissions; it doesn't remove default ones. Removal needs deny rules, and those make it hard to answer "what can this person do?". If an employee needs less than the default, they get a narrower job function or role.

So Employee Identity gains `employee.job_function_key`. The mapping to roles, extra roles and direct grants are designed with Authorization.

## E2: accounts for employees, across several products?

Agreed. Document it, don't build it. Every authorization check would need an extra scope, and nothing in the demo has more than one product. I'll record it as EI-2's alternative ("employee accounts per product scope: more scalable, adds a scope to every check") and as a production-readiness item.

## E5: linking the two identities

Agreed that knowing the link is better: the conflict-of-interest policy needs it. It would be a small table (employee, customer, how the link was verified). No scenario uses it, so I'd **document it, not build it**, unless you want a conflict-of-interest scenario later.

## A formal system identity?

Yes, I'd define it formally. Two options:
- **(a) A fixed principal kind `System`, with no table.** Attribution says "the system did it", and nothing more.
- **(b) A `system_identity` table, seeded with one row per automated actor** (`sys_grant_expiry`, `sys_approval_timeout`, …): id, name, description, status. Attribution then says *which* automated process acted, and a misbehaving process can be disabled through its status.

**My recommendation: (b).** "The system did it" is weak attribution. These are workload identities (non-human identities), which the IAM posting explicitly mentions alongside workforce identities. They have no credentials here, because they run inside our own Worker. How workload identities authenticate in production (certificates, a workload identity standard) goes on the production-readiness list.

**Where it lives:** system identities aren't employees. Options:
- **(i)** rename this subsystem **Internal Identity**: employees plus system identities
- **(ii)** keep "Employee Identity" and document system identities in it, as a separate section
- **(iii)** make them their own small subsystem.

I'd take **(i)**: one subsystem for every identity that belongs to the provider rather than to customers. Your call, since you named the subsystems.

Attribution then has three principal kinds: **account** (customers), **employee**, **system identity**.

## Seeding

Noted. Employees and system identities are seeded for the demo, and the joiner/mover/leaver lifecycle goes on the production-readiness list.

---

**To rule:**
1. Email: keep only the normalized email, in both identity subsystems?
2. Passwords: use the PHC string format?
3. Job function as an employee attribute, with the role mapping deferred to Authorization?
4. System identity: (b), a table?
5. Where system identities live: (i), (ii) or (iii)?

| **Job level** | `1`, `2` | The level within the function (Tier 1, Tier 2) |

This gives cleaner rules:
- **Default roles** (birthright) are keyed by department + function + level.
- **Policies** can use the attributes directly. For example, "Enterprise accounts need level 2 or higher, or a Supervisor" becomes `principal.jobLevel >= 2 or principal.jobFunction = Supervisor`.

I'd model **department** and **job function** as small reference tables (one and two rows: shape for many, CM-6), and **job level** as a positive integer column whose meaning is defined per function.

## Deny: an explicit column, not `!RoleName`

I'd go with an explicit column. Encoding meaning inside the value (`!Admin`) has a concrete cost: `!Admin` isn't a role key, so the database can't check it against the role table, and every reader has to parse the string. An `effect` column (`Grant` or `Deny`) on the assignment keeps the database check and is obvious to read. Deny overrides grant, the same rule as `forbid` in the policy model. We'll settle the details in Authorization.

## System identity: (a) or (b)?

With your Internal Identity idea, (b) costs almost nothing. System identities simply become rows of the same subsystem, with a different kind. So I'd do **(b)** now: a few seeded rows, and attribution names *which* automated process acted.

## Internal Identity with a kind column

Yes. It's the same supertype pattern as customers (CI-1), which keeps the model consistent:

```text
internal_identity                       ← supertype
  internal_identity_id   emp_… | sys_…  (the prefix shows the kind)
  identity_kind          Employee | System
  identity_status        Active | Suspended | Terminated
  created_at

employee                                ← subtype (identity_kind = Employee)
  internal_identity_id   → internal_identity
  email                  normalized, unique (CM-8)
  given_name, family_name
  department_key         → department
  job_function_key       → job_function
  job_level              positive integer

system_identity                         ← subtype (identity_kind = System)
  internal_identity_id   → internal_identity
  name, description                     e.g. sys_grant_expiry, sys_approval_timeout

employee_credential                     ← employees only
  credential_id          crd_…
  employee_id            → employee
  credential_kind        Password       (unique per employee and kind)
  password_hash          PHC string: $pbkdf2-sha256$i=…$<salt>$<hash>
  created_at, replaced_at

department, job_function                ← reference tables
```

What follows from this:
- **Attribution has two principal kinds**: **account** (customers) and **internal identity** (employees and system processes). The prefix in the id shows which kind of internal identity acted.
- **One status for both kinds.** For a system identity, `Terminated` means decommissioned, and `Suspended` stops a misbehaving process.
- **Credentials only for employees.** The credential references the `employee` subtype, so the database refuses a password for a system identity.

## Recorded

- Email: only the normalized form, in both identity subsystems.
- Passwords: the PHC string format.
- E3, E4, E5 (document the link only), E6 agreed.
- E2: employees act as themselves; employee accounts per product scope go into an ADR alternative and production-readiness.

2. Internal Identity as drawn above, with system identities included now?

## Attribution: one actor, or several?

It can be "and", and often is. An action always has **exactly one actor**: the principal that executed it. Other principals often take part, each with a distinct part:

| Action | Actor (executed it) | Other parties |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Alex reads Acme's entitlements | Alex (employee) | — |
| Alex upgrades Acme after Jane called | Alex | on behalf of: Jane's account (the caller) |
| A 60-day grant applied after approval | `sys_approval_workflow` (system) | requested by: Alex · approved by: Sam |
| A grant expires | `sys_grant_expiry` (system) | originally granted by: Alex |

So I'd model attribution as **one required actor, plus zero or more parties, each with its part** (`Requester`, `Approver`, `OnBehalfOf`, …). That answers both "who did it?" (non-repudiation) and "who was involved?" (traceability). It belongs to the Attribution subsystem, so the details wait until then. ### Assistant

## Authorization: opening proposal

**Scope:** what an actor may do, through roles and permissions. Authorization answers "does this actor *hold* this permission?". Conditions such as account status, Enterprise accounts or grant duration belong to Policy. Entitlements (whether the account has the feature at all) belong to Entitlement.

**What it inherits from the identity subsystems:**
- **Customers (CI-4):** roles are assigned from a member account to a target account, own-account roles are explicit, and every assignment needs a justifying relationship.
- **Employees (II-3):** default roles come from department + job function + level (birthright access). On top of those: extra roles, and direct permissions with a reason and an expiry. No deny (production-readiness only).

### Proposed model

```text
permission                        what can be done
  permission_key      account.read · grant.create · feature.sso.configure · …
  permission_scope    Customer | Internal
  feature_key         → feature (Product), for feature permissions only
  description

role                              a named bundle of permissions
  role_key            Owner · Admin · Billing · Viewer · SupportRepresentative · …
  role_scope          Customer | Internal
  name, description

role_permission                   role × permission, same scope (composite keys)

── customer side ─────────────────────────────────────────────
account_role_assignment
  assignment_id
  member_account_id   → account (an individual's)
  target_account_id   → account
  role_key            → role (scope Customer)
  assigned_at, assigned_by, ended_at

── internal side ─────────────────────────────────────────────
birthright_role                   default roles from job attributes
  department_key, job_function_key, minimum_job_level
  role_key            → role (scope Internal)

employee_role_assignment          extra roles
  assignment_id, employee_id, role_key, reason
  assigned_at, assigned_by, expires_at (optional), ended_at

employee_permission_grant         direct permissions, the exception
  grant_id, employee_id, permission_key, reason
  granted_at, granted_by, expires_at (required), ended_at
```

**Effective permissions are computed, never stored, by one building block:**
- **Employee:** permissions of birthright roles, plus active extra roles, plus active direct grants.
- **Customer:** permissions of the member account's active roles in the target account.

### Decisions for you

| # | Question | My recommendation | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AZ1 | **RBAC grants, policies constrain.** Do role permissions say what an actor *may* do, with policies only adding conditions (forbid, require approval)? | Yes | Each question has one source: "what can a Viewer do?" is answered by one table; "when is it refused anyway?" by the policy set. This changes the proposal's §7, where policies did both. |
| AZ2 | One `role` table with a scope column, or separate customer and internal role tables? | **One table with a scope.** Composite keys stop a customer role from being bundled with an internal permission, or assigned to an employee. | One mechanism for roles and bundles. Same pattern as CI-1 and II-1. |
| AZ3 | Several roles per member in one account (your earlier thought)? | **Yes.** The assignment has its own id and one active row per (member, target, role). | Shape for many (CM-6). Jane can be Admin and Billing at Acme. |
| AZ4 | How do birthright roles use job level? | **A `minimum_job_level`, with additive roles.** Every Representative gets `SupportRepresentative`; level 2 and up also get `SupportRepresentativeSenior`. | Higher levels add permissions rather than duplicating roles. |
| AZ5 | Reasons and expiries | **Extra roles:** reason required, expiry optional. **Direct grants:** reason and expiry both required. | Direct grants are the exception that identity governance discourages, so they must justify themselves and end on their own. |
| AZ6 | What if a membership ends? | **The member's role assignments on that organization's accounts end in the same transaction**, and evaluation also ignores any assignment without a justifying relationship. | Fail-safe twice over, the same spirit as CI-5. |
| AZ7 | **A suspended account can neither act nor be acted on.** If Jane's own account is suspended, does she lose access to Acme? | **Yes.** Her account is her operational identity (CI-3); suspended means suspended. | This doesn't punish unpaid bills: billing lives in subscriptions, not holds, so Jane's unpaid personal plan doesn't touch her work at Acme. Holds are for abuse, removal and lost membership, which *should* stop an actor everywhere. |
| AZ8 | Who manages assignments? | **Seeded for the demo.** Granting or ending roles is itself a permission (`authorization.assign-role`), designed but not built. The stretch scenario "change a member's role" would be its first use. | Keeps the demo to reading authorization, not administering it. |

### Seeded roles (a first cut)

| Scope | Role | Permissions (sketch) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Customer | Owner | every feature permission of the account, plus `authorization.assign-role` |
| Customer | Admin | `feature.sso.configure`, `feature.api-access.use`, `feature.audit-log-export.use`, `feature.advanced-analytics.use` |
| Customer | Billing | `feature.audit-log-export.use`, `feature.advanced-analytics.use` |
| Customer | Viewer | `feature.advanced-analytics.use` |
| Internal | SupportRepresentative | `account.read`, `case.open`, `subscription.change-plan`, `grant.create` |
| Internal | SupportRepresentativeSenior | `subscription.cancel` |
| Internal | SupportSupervisor | `approval.decide` |

Birthright rows: CustomerSupport + Representative + level ≥ 1 → `SupportRepresentative`; level ≥ 2 → `SupportRepresentativeSenior`; CustomerSupport + Supervisor + level ≥ 1 → `SupportRepresentative` and `SupportSupervisor`.

Limits such as "Tier 1 grants up to 14 days" or "Enterprise accounts need level 2" stay out of the roles. Those are policy conditions (AZ1).

- deny assignments (already listed)
- resource-scoped roles (a role on part of an account)
- delegated administration
- access certification campaigns
- separation-of-duties rules between roles
- just-in-time elevation.
