### Assistant

## What a permission key is, and how it relates to OAuth2 scopes

A **permission key** is the readable identifier of one permission: an action on a kind of resource, such as `account.read`, `grant.create` or `feature.sso.configure`. You're right that it *looks* like an OAuth2 scope (`repo:read`), and the syntax is similar. The concepts are different, though:

| | Permission | OAuth2 scope |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Answers | What may this **principal** do? | What did the user **delegate to this client application**? |
| Comes from | Roles and assignments | The user's consent when authorizing an application |
| Limits | The person or account | The token an application holds |

They connect like this: a token's scopes can only narrow what the user is already permitted. We don't issue OAuth tokens to applications, so we only have permissions. OAuth delegation would be a production-readiness item.

## `feature_key`, `feature_code` or `feature_id`?

There's a convention behind the suffix (data-model README):
- **`_id`** is an opaque generated identifier for things with a lifecycle (`cus_7Q2…`, `acc_…`).
- **`_key`** is a readable, stable identifier *we choose* for reference data (`sso`, `Business`, `CustomerSupport`).

A feature is reference data, so it gets `feature_key`, like `plan_key`, `role_key`, `department_key` and `job_function_key`. "Code" works just as well (it's common for things like country codes); what matters is using one suffix everywhere. **My recommendation: keep `_key`.** If you prefer `_code`, I'll rename every reference-data key.

## Why `permission_scope` and `role_scope`, and a better name

Their job: say **which kind of principal can hold** the role or permission, so the database refuses nonsense. For example, a customer role can't contain `approval.decide`, and an employee can't be assigned a customer role.

To your question: a role is for **exactly one** kind, never both. Even a role that sounds shared, like "Viewer", means different permissions on each side.

You're right that "scope" is the wrong word; it suggests OAuth or resource scope. Attribution already has a name for this: the **principal kind**, either `Account` or `InternalIdentity`. **My recommendation: rename both columns to `principal_kind`**, with the same values everywhere.

## `account_role_assignment`: the two account ids

| Column | Meaning | Jane at Acme | Jane in her own account |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `member_account_id` | **Who** holds the role: the acting account, always an individual's | `acc_jane` | `acc_jane` |
| `target_account_id` | **Where** the role applies: the account acted on | `acc_acme` | `acc_jane` |
| `role_key` | **What** role | Admin | Owner |

## A status to remove an assignment?

The `ended_at` timestamp already does that job. An assignment is active exactly when `ended_at` is empty, and ending it keeps the row (CM-7: ending is a timestamp). A status column would record the same fact twice, and the two could disagree. I'd add `ended_by` and an `end_reason` (`Revoked`, `Expired`, `MembershipEnded`), so you can see *why* an assignment ended without searching the audit log.

## `employee_…` tables, or `internal_…`?

You've caught a real gap: **system identities need permissions too.** `sys_grant_expiry` revokes grants, so it needs something like `grant.revoke`. So:
- `employee_role_assignment` → **`internal_role_assignment`** (references `internal_identity`)
- `employee_permission_grant` → **`internal_permission_assignment`**
- `birthright_role` stays employee-only, since only employees have job attributes.

## "Assignment" or "grant"?

**Assignment, everywhere in Authorization.** "Grant" already means something else: an *entitlement grant* (a feature given to an account). Using it here too would give one word two meanings. So the names are **role assignment** and **permission assignment**.

## `SupportRepresentativeSenior`: the same mistake

Yes, "Senior" is the job level, so it doesn't belong in a role name. The fix also clarifies what roles are: **a role names a bundle of capabilities, not a job.** Job attributes decide *which* bundles you get, through the birthright rules:

| Role (capability bundle) | Permissions |
| --- | --- |
| `SupportCaseHandling` | `account.read`, `case.open`, `subscription.change-plan`, `grant.create` |
| `SubscriptionCancellation` | `subscription.cancel` |
| `ApprovalDecision` | `approval.decide` |
| `EntitlementMaintenance` (for system identities) | `grant.revoke`, `case-request.apply` |

Birthright rules:
- CustomerSupport · Representative · level ≥ 1 → `SupportCaseHandling`
- CustomerSupport · Representative · level ≥ 2 → `SubscriptionCancellation`
- CustomerSupport · Supervisor · level ≥ 1 → `SupportCaseHandling`, `SubscriptionCancellation`, `ApprovalDecision`

The system identities get `EntitlementMaintenance` through an internal role assignment.

## What does "use" mean?

It was meant as "exercise the feature" (call the API, run the export), as opposed to "configure" (change its settings). As you noticed, that's vague. **My recommendation: a concrete verb per feature**, so a permission reads like what it allows, and a CSR's explanation reads naturally ("Carol's role doesn't include creating API tokens"):

| Feature | Permission |
| --- | --- |
| SSO | `feature.sso.configure` |
| API access | `feature.api-access.create-token` |
| Audit log export | `feature.audit-log-export.export` |
| Advanced analytics | `feature.advanced-analytics.view` |

## Recorded

1. RBAC grants, policies constrain
2. one role table
3. several roles per member
4. birthright with a minimum level
5. reasons and expiries
6. assignments end with the membership
7. a suspended account can neither act nor be acted on

2. Keep `_key` for reference data?

4. Rename to `internal_role_assignment` and `internal_permission_assignment`, with "assignment" throughout Authorization?
5. Roles named as capability bundles, as listed above?
6. A concrete verb per feature permission?

### Assistant

## The two permission patterns

You asked me to comment on them, and they're the right two:

```text
<resource>.<operation>                  account.read · subscription.change-plan · approval.decide
feature.<feature_key>.<operation>       feature.sso.configure · feature.api-access.create-token
```

**Why two, rather than one:**
- The first pattern names a **kind of resource** our application manages (accounts, cases, grants…). There are a handful, and they're fixed by the code.
- The second names **one specific feature from the product catalog**. Features are data (the Product subsystem), so the middle segment is a `feature_key` that references the catalog, and the set grows whenever the catalog does.

Merging them (`<resource>[.<instance>].<operation>`) would hide that difference.

**One clarification:** the pattern follows the **resource**, not who holds the permission. Customer roles can hold non-feature permissions too. For example, an account Owner managing roles in their own account holds `role-assignment.create`, which follows the first pattern. Which principals may hold a permission is `principal_kind`'s job.

### The values

**Resources and operations** (first pattern), covering every scenario we've planned:

| Resource | Operations | Held by | Used in |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `account` | `read` | Employee | lookups, entitlement checks, explanations |
| `audit` | `read` | Employee | account history |
| `case` | `open` | Employee | every case |
| `subscription` | `change-plan`, `cancel` | Employee | plan changes |
| `grant` | `create` | Employee | temporary grants |
| `grant` | `revoke` | System | grant expiry |
| `approval` | `decide` | Employee | supervisor decisions |
| `case-request` | `apply` | System | applying approved requests |
| `role-assignment` | `create`, `end` | Individual | an Owner managing members (designed, not built) |

**Features and operations** (second pattern), one concrete verb per feature:

| Feature | Operations |
| --- | --- |
| `sso` | `configure` |
| `api-access` | `create-token` |
| `audit-log-export` | `export` |
| `advanced-analytics` | `view` |

Both lists are closed: an unknown resource, feature or operation is refused when permissions are loaded. Features gain operations in the Product subsystem.

## `member_of_account_id`

Yes, better than `target_account_id`. The pair reads as a sentence: *`member_account_id` is a member of `member_of_account_id` as `role_key`*. "Jane's account is a member of Acme's account as Admin." For Jane's own account it reads "a member of itself as Owner", which is slightly odd but accurate. I'll use your name.

## `principal_kind`: high level or low level?

**My recommendation: low level, with three values: `Individual`, `Employee`, `System`.**
- **More precise.** The database can then refuse to assign a system-only role (`EntitlementMaintenance`, with `grant.revoke`) to an employee, or an employee role to a system.
- **Matches attribution.** These three are exactly the kinds of actor that exist. Organizations never act, so a customer actor is always an individual's account.
- **The cost:** a role meant for both employees and systems would need two roles. Nothing in our model needs that.

## Status vs. dates: one rule

You're right, it's mixed today. Membership even has both a status *and* `removed_at`. Here's a rule that separates the two cleanly:

| Kind of record | Mechanism | Why | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Things with a lifecycle**: they can move between states and come back | a **status** | the current state is the point, and it can change in either direction | `customer_status`, `account_status`, `identity_status` |
| **Facts in time**: they start, may end, and never reopen | **start and end timestamps** (plus `ended_by` and `end_reason`); active means not ended and not expired | coming back is a *new* fact, a new row, so history is kept | holds, memberships, role and permission assignments, credentials |

Applying it changes one thing we've already ruled: **organization membership loses `membership_status`.** It becomes `joined_at` + `ended_at` + `ended_by` + `end_reason`, and "one active membership" becomes a unique index on rows where `ended_at` is empty. Rejoining was already a new row (G1), so membership was always a fact in time. I'd record the rule as **CM-11** and note the change on **CI-2** as an amendment.

## Jane on her own, or in Acme's account?

`principal_kind` doesn't need to distinguish this, because **the actor is the same in both cases**: Jane's account (`acc_jane`, kind `Individual`). What differs is **the account she's acting in**, which is the account named in each request (the account context we discussed under Customer Identity). That account decides which of her role assignments applies:

| Jane acts in… | Actor | Assignment that applies | Effectively |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| her own account | `acc_jane` | `acc_jane` → member of `acc_jane` · Owner | on her own |
| Acme's account | `acc_jane` | `acc_jane` → member of `acc_acme` · Admin | for Acme, through her membership |

Attribution records both: actor `acc_jane`, account acted in `acc_acme`. "Jane did it, in Acme's account, as Admin." No extra kind is needed. `principal_kind` says *who can hold* a role; the account context says *where* it's being used.

One wording point, to keep two ideas apart:
- **"In an organization's account"**: Jane using her own membership, as above.
- **"On behalf of"**: someone acting *for another party*, like a CSR acting for a caller. That's the `OnBehalfOf` party in attribution.

Jane in Acme's account is the first, not the second.

Question
Authorization is a decision; should the subsystem and the work we just did have a slightly different name?

### Assistant

Yes, I think so. You're right that **authorization is a decision**: "may this actor do this, here, now?". What we just modeled is only *one input* to that decision. The decision combines three subsystems:

```text
Authorization decision (the PDP, at request time)
  ├── what the actor holds        ← the subsystem we just designed: roles, permissions, assignments
  ├── what the account has        ← Entitlement: subscriptions, grants, effective entitlements
  └── the conditions that apply   ← Policy: forbid / require approval
```

Calling the first one "Authorization" mislabels the part as the whole. It would also make "Authorization" mean two things in our documents: a table design and a runtime decision.

## Name options

| Name | Fit | Concern |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Access** | Common IAM usage ("access management", "access reviews", "standing access"): *who holds what access* | none significant |
| Permission Management | Precise | Long. Sounds like an admin feature, and we don't build administration (AZ-7) |
| Role Management | Familiar | Too narrow: also covers direct permission assignments and birthright rules |
| Entitlement (identity-governance sense) | Identity-governance tools call held permissions "entitlements" | **Collides** with our product Entitlement subsystem. Avoid |

**My recommendation: rename the subsystem to Access.** Then:
- **Access** (data): permissions, roles, assignments, birthright rules. Answers *"what does this actor hold?"*
- **Authorization** keeps its proper meaning: the decision process that combines Access, Entitlement and Policy. It's described in the glossary and implemented by the Policy Decision Point, but it isn't a data subsystem.

## What the rename touches

- **Files:** `data-model/authorization.md` → `access.md`, `adr/authorization.md` → `access.md`, `diagrams/authorization.er.puml` → `access.er.puml`.
- **ADR ids `AZ-1…8` → `AC-1…8`.** Our ADR README says ids never change. I'd still make an exception now: they were created today and are referenced only from our own documents, which I'd update in the same change. After this, the rule holds without exception. The alternative is keeping `AZ` as a historical prefix, but then the prefix would contradict the name forever.
- **References:** CI-4's link, the data-model index, the ADR index, the production-readiness section heading, and the glossary. I'd add a short **Authorization** glossary entry: "the decision of whether an actor may perform an action in an account context, combining Access, Entitlement and Policy".

Should I go ahead with **Access** and the `AC` prefix?

### Proposed model

```text
product                         the product being sold (one row for now)
  product_key, name

feature                         the unit of entitlement
  feature_key, product_key, name, description
  is_paid                       lost when a subscription lapses
  is_metered, limit_unit        e.g. seats → "seats"

feature_operation               what can be done with a feature (Access builds permissions from it)
  feature_key, operation, description

plan                            a tier of the product
  plan_key, product_key, name
  tier_rank                     unique per product; orders upgrade vs downgrade

addon                           an extension bought on top of a plan
  addon_key, product_key, name, description

plan_feature_bundle             one feature a plan includes
  plan_key, feature_key, limit_value (null = unlimited)

addon_feature_bundle            one feature an add-on includes
  addon_key, feature_key, limit_value

addon_plan_eligibility          which plans an add-on can be added to
```

| `api-access` | yes | no | `create-token` | — | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | — |
| `sso` | yes | no | `configure` | — | — | ✓ | ✓ | — |
| `audit-log-export` | yes | no | `export` | — | — | ✓ | ✓ | — |
| `advanced-analytics` | yes | no | `view` | — | — | — | ✓ | `advanced-analytics`, eligible on Pro and Business |

### Assistant

## Feature: combine the flags into one bit field?

I'd advise against a bit field:
- **Queries get harder to read and to index.** `WHERE is_paid` becomes `WHERE (flags & 1) = 1`, which needs a special index and is easy to get wrong.
- **Weaker constraints and types.** The database can't express "a quota only on metered features" against a bit, and TypeScript loses its named booleans.
- **No space to save.** PostgreSQL stores a boolean in one byte.

You've spotted a real redundancy, though. **`is_metered` is implied by the unit:** a feature is metered exactly when it has a unit. So:
- **drop `is_metered`**
- **rename `limit_unit` → `metered_unit`**, empty for unmetered features. "Is it metered?" becomes `WHERE metered_unit IS NOT NULL`.
- **keep `is_paid`** as a plain boolean. It can't be derived reliably: "not in the Free plan" assumes we know which plan is free.

And yes, both will be queried. Entitlement needs "which features are lost when a subscription lapses" (`is_paid`) and "which features have quotas" (`metered_unit`).

## Plan: one product per plan?

Yes. Each plan belongs to exactly one product, and its bundles may only include that product's features. Composite keys (plan and product, feature and product) let the database enforce it. A plan spanning several products (a "suite") would be a production concern.

**`tier_rank`** is a number that orders a product's plans from lowest to highest: Free 1, Pro 2, Business 3, Enterprise 4. It isn't a price. It answers two questions:
- **Is a change an upgrade or a downgrade?** Higher rank means upgrade.
- **What's the lowest plan that includes a feature?** That's the remedy in an access explanation.

It's unique within a product.

## Add-on: per product?

Yes, the same as plans: an add-on belongs to one product. Eligibility only pairs it with plans of that product.

## What the bundle tables mean

They describe **what each plan (or add-on) contains**. One row says "this plan includes this feature, with this quota". Pro, for example:

| plan_key | feature_key | quota |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `pro` | `seats` | 25 |
| `pro` | `api-access` | — |

So Pro includes 25 seats and API access, and nothing else. `addon_feature_bundle` is the same for add-ons: the `advanced-analytics` add-on contains one row, `advanced-analytics`.

**`limit_value` is a quota**, as you guessed: the maximum quantity of a metered feature (25 seats). It's empty in two cases with different meanings, which a constraint keeps apart:
- **an unmetered feature:** the feature is simply included (on/off), and a quota is forbidden
- **a metered feature:** unlimited (Enterprise seats).

I named it `limit_value` only because `limit` is a reserved word in SQL. I'd **rename it to `quota`**, which is clearer and not reserved. (Quotas that reset per period, like "requests per day", would need a period column. That's for production-readiness.)

## P4: an add-on for something the plan already includes?

Good catch. The answer depends on the feature:
- **Unmetered feature** (Advanced Analytics on Enterprise, which already includes it): **not allowed.** It adds nothing. Eligibility simply doesn't pair the add-on with Enterprise, and a check when the catalog loads refuses any eligibility where the add-on adds nothing to the plan.
- **Metered feature** (a seat pack on Business): **allowed.** It's the whole point of a seat pack: 100 seats + a 10-seat pack.

That also settles most of P4: **for metered features, add-on quotas add to the plan's quota**, and for unmetered features overlap can't happen. How *grants* combine with all this is left for Entitlement.

## Summary of changes

| Change | |
| --- | --- |
| `feature` | drop `is_metered`; `limit_unit` → `metered_unit` (empty = not metered); keep `is_paid` |
| Bundles | `limit_value` → `quota`; forbidden on unmetered features; empty on metered features means unlimited |
| `plan`, `addon` | each belongs to one product; bundles only include features of the same product |
| Eligibility | an add-on is eligible for a plan only if it adds an unmetered feature the plan lacks, or a metered quota |
| Quotas | for metered features, add-on quotas add to the plan's |

## Recorded

- **P1:** a `product` table
- **P3:** add-on eligibility
- **P5:** kebab-case keys everywhere
- **P6:** pricing and lifecycle go to production-readiness
- **P7:** your bundle names.

**To rule:** the changes in the table above (they cover P2 and P4).

### Assistant

## A plan with several products (a suite)

The current model can't express that: `plan.product_key` ties each plan to one product. If suites are plausible, I'd change the shape now. That's cheap today and costly once there's data (CM-6).

- **Plans don't belong to a product.** A plan's bundles can include features from any product, and the plan's products are whatever its features belong to. A "Pro" plan covering only one product is simply a plan whose features all come from that product.
- **Features still belong to a product.** So the `product` table keeps its purpose: grouping features, and later scoping employees per product.
- **`tier_rank` needs a new home.** Without a product, there's nothing to rank plans within. I'd add a **plan family**: a group of plans meant to be compared (Free → Pro → Business → Enterprise), with `tier_rank` unique within the family. Moving between families is a plan change that's neither an upgrade nor a downgrade. A suite would be its own family.

## An add-on for several products

The same change covers it. Add-ons don't belong to a product either: an add-on's bundles can include features of several products, and eligibility says which plans it can join.

## Changing plans when the account has add-ons

Yes, a plan change has to check add-ons. The rule follows from eligibility:

| Change | Example | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Upgrade to a plan that **includes** the add-on's feature | Pro + Advanced Analytics → Enterprise | The add-on is redundant and is removed as part of the change |
| Change to a plan where the add-on is **not eligible** | Business + Advanced Analytics → Free | The add-on is removed, and the feature is lost |
| Downgrade with a **metered** add-on | Business + 10-seat pack → Pro | The pack stays; seat usage is checked against Pro's quota plus the pack (refused if over) |

In every case, the add-on changes appear in the proposal's entitlement diff, so the CSR sees them before confirming.

No Slice 2 scenario involves add-ons (the upgrade is Initech, Free → Pro), so I agree with your suggestion: **record the rule in the Entitlement design, implement it only if a scenario needs it**, and add the rest to production-readiness:
- proration and refunds when an add-on is removed mid-period
- whether a removal waits for the end of the billing period
- notifying the customer.

## To rule

**Plans and add-ons independent of products, with a plan family for tier ranking.** I recommend it. If you agree, I'll write up Product with everything else you've accepted:
- `metered_unit`, `is_paid`, `quota`
- eligibility only where an add-on adds something; add-on quotas add to the plan's
- kebab-case keys, applied to the Access and Internal Identity documents as well.

### Assistant

The Product diagram is ready: `tech-docs/diagrams/product.er.puml`, rendered to `product.svg`. It uses your template, with every column declared, and I checked the rendered image. I'll write the model document and ADRs once you're comfortable with the shape.

## The model

```text
product ──offers──▶ feature ──allows──▶ feature_operation ··named by··▶ permission (Access)
                       ▲         ▲
          included in  │         │  included in
                       │         │
plan_family ──ranks──▶ plan     addon
                       │  ▲       │
                       │  └─ addon_plan_eligibility (which add-ons a plan accepts)
                       ▼          ▼
              plan_feature_bundle   addon_feature_bundle   (feature + quota)
```

## What changes when plans and add-ons are independent of products

**1. A plan's products are derived from its features.** Nothing on `plan` says which product it's for. The answer is "the products of the features it includes":

| Plan | Family | Features included | Products, derived |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `pro` | `standard` | seats, api-access | the one product |
| `security-suite` (hypothetical) | `suites` | sso (product A), threat-alerts (product B) | A and B |

A one-product plan and a suite are the same structure; there's nothing special to build for suites.

**2. Tier rank moves to the plan family.** `tier_rank` is unique within a `plan_family`, not within a product:
- **Within a family,** comparing ranks tells an upgrade from a downgrade (Free 1 → Pro 2 → Business 3 → Enterprise 4).
- **Across families,** a change is just a plan change: neither up nor down.
- **For a remedy** ("what plan would include this feature?"), the lowest-ranked plan in the account's *current* family is the natural answer, with other families as alternatives.

**3. Add-ons work the same way.** An add-on's features can come from several products. `addon_plan_eligibility` is the only link between add-ons and plans, and it's limited to pairs where the add-on adds something (the note in the diagram).

**4. The product still matters.** Features belong to exactly one product (`feature.product_key`). So:
- grouping and displaying features by product keeps working
- permissions inherit the product through their feature (`feature.<feature_key>.<operation>`)
- a later per-product scope for employees attaches to features, not plans.

**5. A cost to name.** The database no longer stops a plan from mixing products *by accident*, because mixing is now allowed by design. A family could hold an intended rule such as "the `standard` family only includes features of product A". That would be a catalog validation when the catalog loads, not a constraint. I'd leave it out unless you want it.

## Seeded for the demo

- one product
- one family, `standard`: `free` (1), `pro` (2), `business` (3), `enterprise` (4)
- the five features with their operations
- one add-on, `advanced-analytics`, eligible on `pro` and `business`.

### Assistant

## Plan–feature: too granular? Why not plan–product?

Plan–feature is the granularity the plans actually differ at, so I'd keep it as the source of truth. Within one product:
- **Pro has `api-access` but not `sso`.** "All features of the product" can't say that.
- **Quotas differ per plan.** Seats are 25 on Pro and 100 on Business, so metered features need a row per plan anyway.

A plan–product link meaning "every feature of this product is included" also has a hidden cost: **it includes features that don't exist yet.** Adding a new paid feature to the product would silently give it to every plan linked that way, with no row anyone reviewed. And an access explanation could only say "your plan includes the whole product", not the exact row that grants the feature.

**My recommendation:**
- **The data stays explicit:** plan–feature rows, which are auditable, and explanations cite exact rows.
- **The convenience lives in authoring.** The seeded catalog file can say "Enterprise includes all features of the product", and loading expands it into one row per feature. Adding a feature then means re-running the load and seeing the new rows, a visible change rather than a silent one.
- **The plan's products** are available as a derived view (`plan_product`) for anyone who wants to query them.

## What a plan family is

A **plan family** is a set of plans meant to be compared on **one ladder**. In our catalog there's one family, `standard`:

```text
standard:  free (1)  →  pro (2)  →  business (3)  →  enterprise (4)
```

It exists because `tier_rank` needs something to rank *within*. When plans belonged to a product, the product was that scope. Without it, we need a scope that says which plans are comparable. Examples of other families a real catalog might have:

| Family | Plans, lowest to highest |
| --- | --- |
| `standard` | free → pro → business → enterprise |
| `security-suite` | suite-basic → suite-plus |
| `education` | edu-classroom → edu-campus |

Moving from `pro` to `business` is an upgrade (same family, higher rank). Moving from `pro` to `suite-basic` is a plan change, neither up nor down.

**Alternatives, if the family feels like too much:**
- **(a) One global `tier_rank` across all plans.** Simpler, but it forces an arbitrary order between unrelated plans. Is a suite "above" Pro?
- **(b) No rank at all; "upgrade" computed** as "the target includes everything the current plan has, with equal or larger quotas". Elegant, but "the lowest plan that includes a feature" (the remedy) still needs an order.

**My recommendation:** keep the family, with one seeded row. It costs one small table and keeps the ranking honest (CM-6). If the name isn't intuitive, **plan ladder** says the same thing more vividly. Your choice of name.

**To rule:**
1. Keep plan–feature as the only bundle; "whole product" is an authoring shortcut, expanded at load?
2. Keep the plan family (one row)? Named `plan_family` or `plan_ladder`?

### Assistant

A family isn't a view on a product. It's closer to the second thing you said: **a way of packaging the catalog for one audience or purpose**, as a ladder of plans.

| Concept | What it is | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Product** | What is *built*: a set of features | the product, with sso, api-access, seats… |
| **Plan family** | How it's *sold to an audience*: a ladder of plans | `standard` (general use), `security-suite` (security buyers), `education` (schools) |
| **Plan** | One step on a ladder: a chosen set of features with quotas | `pro`: 25 seats and API access |

So, yes, families often reflect an angle (security, everyday use, education). Each family picks its own features, possibly from several products, and the **same feature can appear in several families**, for example `sso` in both `standard/business` and `security-suite/suite-basic`. Within a family, moving between plans is an upgrade or a downgrade. Between families, it's a switch of packaging, neither up nor down.

## Entitlement: opening proposal

**Scope:** what each account *has*. That covers its subscription (plan, add-ons, status), the grants CSRs give it, and the **effective entitlements** computed from both. Entitlement is the second input to the authorization decision (Access: what the actor holds; Entitlement: what the account has; Policy: the conditions).

### Proposed model

```text
subscription                          lifecycle → status (CM-11)
  subscription_id      sub_…
  account_id           → account (must be Independent)
  subscription_status  Trialing | Active | PastDue | Canceled
  trial_ends_at        set exactly when Trialing
  past_due_since       set exactly when PastDue
  started_at, canceled_at

subscription_plan                     fact in time: which plan, from when to when
  subscription_plan_id, subscription_id, plan_key
  started_at, ended_at, ended_by, end_reason (PlanChanged)

subscription_addon                    fact in time
  subscription_addon_id, subscription_id, addon_key
  started_at, ended_at, ended_by, end_reason (Removed | IncludedByPlan | NotEligible | SubscriptionCanceled)

entitlement_grant                     fact in time
  grant_id             grt_…
  account_id, feature_key, quota
  reason, granted_by, case_request_id (Support)
  starts_at, expires_at (required)
  ended_at, ended_by, end_reason (Revoked | Expired)
```

**Effective entitlements** are computed, never stored, by one building block. For an account at an instant:
1. **Suspended account** → nothing (AC-6).
2. **Sources:** the current plan's bundles, plus active add-ons' bundles, plus active grants. Each entitlement lists all its sources.
3. **Quotas** for metered features: plan + add-ons + grants, added together.
4. **Lapsed subscription** (`Canceled`, or `PastDue` beyond the grace period) → paid features from the plan and add-ons are removed.
5. **No subscription** → nothing from a plan (e.g. an `OrganizationOnly` account).

### Decisions for you

| # | Question | My recommendation | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| EN1 | Subscription lifecycle | **Status `Trialing`, `Active`, `PastDue`, `Canceled`.** `Canceled` is final: subscribing again creates a new subscription. At most one non-canceled subscription per account. Only `Independent` accounts subscribe. | A lifecycle gets a status (CM-11). A final `Canceled` keeps each subscription's history clean. The constraints follow CI-3. The Free plan is a subscription too, just at no cost. |
| EN2 | Plan and add-ons: columns on the subscription (history in the audit log), or facts in time? | **Facts in time:** `subscription_plan` and `subscription_addon` rows with start and end | It follows CM-11. "What plan was Acme on in September?" becomes a query rather than an audit-log search, and the add-on removals of PR-6 get a recorded reason. |
| EN3 | Where does the grace period live? | **On the plan** (`grace_period_days`, a Product column). An Enterprise plan might allow 30 days, Pro 14. | Grace is part of the commercial terms, and terms vary by plan. A single global setting is the simpler alternative. |
| EN4 | What can a grant be? | **One feature, one account, an explicit expiry.** Allowed only where it adds something: an unmetered feature the account lacks, or a metered quota (added to the total). | The same rule as add-ons (PR-6): a grant for something the account already has is noise. |
| EN5 | **Does a grant survive a lapsed subscription?** | **Yes**, but not a suspension | Goodwill during a payment problem is one of the main reasons to grant ("keep SSO working while billing is sorted out"). If grants lapsed with the plan, they'd fail exactly when they're needed. Suspension still stops everything (AC-6). |
| EN6 | When does a grant end at expiry? | **Both ways:** `sys_grant_expiry` ends it explicitly (`end_reason = Expired`, audited), and evaluation ignores expired grants anyway | Fail-safe twice: a late or failed job never extends access. |
| EN7 | What are "seats in use"? | **The distinct member accounts holding at least one active role assignment in the account** | Counted, never stored. It follows Access (assignments are per account). Downgrades check it against the new quota. |
| EN8 | Plan changes with add-ons | **Record the PR-6 rule here** (an add-on the target plan includes or no longer accepts ends, with reason `IncludedByPlan` or `NotEligible`). Implement it only if a scenario needs it. | As agreed. |

```text
  subscription_status  Trialing | Active | Suspended | Canceled

  status_ends_at       when the current status is scheduled to end (e.g. a trial's end)
  started_at
```

| Status | Meaning | `status_ends_at` |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Trialing` | Full plan, for a limited time | the end of the trial |
| `Active` | Normal | empty |
| `Suspended` | The plan's **paid** features stop, for a reason set from outside (billing, an expired trial) | empty, or when the suspension is scheduled to lift |
| `Canceled` | Final. Subscribing again creates a new subscription | empty |

**Two points to confirm:**
- **The word "Suspended" is now used twice:** an account can be suspended (it stops everywhere: AC-6), and a subscription can be suspended (paid features stop *in that account only*: Jane still works at Acme). The noun always disambiguates ("account suspended" vs "subscription suspended"), and the reason codes differ (`account-suspended`, `subscription-suspended`). If you'd rather avoid the double use, `Restricted` is the alternative for subscriptions.
- **When a trial's `status_ends_at` passes,** I'd have a system identity move it to `Suspended` with `status_reason = TrialEnded`. Paid features stop (fail-safe) until billing converts the trial or someone cancels it.

Yes, it was "how long a subscription can be past due and still keep its paid features". With your change, that's billing's decision (it moves the subscription to `Suspended` whenever its own grace ends), so **EN3 goes away**: no grace column anywhere.

## EN4: what a grant can be, with examples

A grant gives **one account one feature, until an expiry date**. It's allowed only if it actually adds something:

| Grant | Account has | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| SSO to Initech for 7 days | Free plan, no SSO | **Allowed:** Initech gets SSO until the expiry |
| SSO to Acme for 7 days | Business plan, which includes SSO | **Refused:** Acme already has it |
| 10 extra seats to Acme for 30 days | 100 seats from Business | **Allowed:** 110 seats until the expiry, then 100 again |

It's the same rule as add-ons (PR-6): for an on/off feature, only if missing; for a metered feature, the quota adds up.

## EN5: how a suspended or canceled subscription is managed

1. **Who changes the status:** in production, the billing integration, plus CSR actions (a CSR cancels with `subscription.cancel`). In the demo, statuses are seeded, and a CSR can cancel.
2. **What it does:** the plan's and add-ons' **paid** features stop. Unpaid ones (seats) remain. The explanation says why: "Globex's subscription is suspended (payment overdue), so API access is unavailable."
3. **Grants:** my recommendation is that **grants keep working** during a subscription suspension. Keeping a customer going during a billing problem is one of the main reasons to grant. An account suspension still stops everything.

Do you agree that grants survive a *subscription* suspension?

## EN6: expiry, explained

A grant has `expires_at`. Two things happen around it, deliberately redundant:

```text
Oct 1   grant created: SSO for Initech, expires_at Oct 8
Oct 8   00:00  expires_at passes → every entitlement check from now on ignores the grant
Oct 8   00:03  sys_grant_expiry runs → sets ended_at, end_reason = Expired, writes an audit record
```

- **The check at evaluation time is what actually stops access**, at the exact instant.
- **The background process makes the end visible:** a recorded end and an audit record that says the system ended it.

If the process is late or fails, access still stopped on time. That's what "fail-safe twice" meant.

## EN7: how seats are counted

Seats in use = **the number of different member accounts with at least one active role assignment in the account**. For Acme:

| member_account_id | role | ended? | Counts |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `acc_olivia` | owner | no | ✓ |
| `acc_jane` | admin | no | ✓ |
| `acc_jane` | billing | no | (already counted) |
| `acc_carol` | viewer | no | ✓ |
| `acc_dan` | admin | yes | ✗ |

So Acme uses **3 seats**: Jane counts once despite two roles, and Dan's ended assignment doesn't count. Jane's own account uses 1 seat (her Owner role on it). It's a single query over the account's active assignments, never stored.

## Proposal: usability comes from the date, not a status

A subscription stays `Trialing` or `Active`, and **`status_ends_at` is the date until which it's usable**. Billing (out of scope) only ever pushes that date forward: each payment extends it. When the date passes without an extension, the subscription stops being usable, with no status change and no background job.

```text
subscription
  subscription_id      sub_…
  account_id           → account (must be Independent)
  subscription_status  Trialing | Active | Canceled
  status_ends_at       usable until this instant; empty = no end (e.g. the Free plan)
  started_at
```

A subscription is **usable** when its status is `Trialing` or `Active` **and** `status_ends_at` is empty or still in the future.

| Situation | Status | `status_ends_at` | Usable | Explanation the CSR sees |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Pro, paid through Nov 30 | Active | Nov 30 | yes (until Nov 30) | — |
| Pro, payment not received | Active | Oct 15 (passed) | **no** | "Globex's subscription ended on Oct 15 (not renewed)" |
| Trial | Trialing | Nov 5 | yes (until Nov 5) | — |
| Trial not converted | Trialing | Oct 20 (passed) | **no** | "Wayne's trial ended on Oct 20" |
| Free plan | Active | empty | yes | — |
| Canceled | Canceled | — | **no** | "Stark's subscription was canceled" |

**What this gives us:**
- **Fail-safe by construction.** Access stops at the exact instant the date passes, like grant expiry. A late billing system can't extend access by accident; only an explicit extension does.
- **Payments stay out of the model.** Billing's whole interface with Entitlement is "extend `status_ends_at`" or "cancel".
- **No collision with account suspension.** The word "Suspended" belongs only to accounts again.
- **Your earlier point 2 (an ended trial) needs no rule.** A trial whose date has passed is simply unusable, and the explanation says "trial ended".
- **`status_reason` can go.** Why a subscription isn't usable is derived: the date passed while Trialing (trial ended), the date passed while Active (not renewed), or Canceled. The cause of a cancellation goes in the audit record.

## When the subscription isn't usable, what stops?

Following your answer to point 3:

| Source | When the subscription isn't usable |
| --- | --- |
| Plan and add-ons, **paid** features | stop |
| Plan and add-ons, **unpaid** features (seats) | remain |
| **Grants** | **stop**: a grant needs a usable subscription |

And, as always, a **suspended account** (holds) stops everything (AC-6).

This replaces EN5. Grants are tied to the subscription: a CSR who wants to keep a customer going during a billing problem would extend the subscription's date (a subscription change with its own permission and policy) rather than grant features around it.

Paid features stop and grants stop with them: agreed.

## Are unpaid features independent of the subscription?

In the model so far, no: unpaid features (`seats`) come from the plan, so they depend on the subscription. When the subscription isn't usable, the rule I wrote keeps the plan's unpaid features **at that plan's quota**. That gives an odd result: a canceled Business account would keep 100 seats forever. Your question exposes it, so here are three ways to decide what an account keeps:

| Option | When the subscription isn't usable, the account has… | Globex (Pro, not renewed) | Consequence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **(a)** Keep the plan's unpaid features | the plan's unpaid features, at the plan's quota | 25 seats | A lapsed Business account keeps 100 seats. Odd. |
| **(b)** Fall back to the ladder's entry plan | **what the lowest-ranked plan of its ladder includes** (`free`: 3 seats) | 3 seats | "Not renewed means back to Free". A common SaaS behavior. |
| **(c)** Nothing | no entitlements at all | 0 | Simplest. `is_paid` would lose its purpose. |

**My recommendation: (b).** It's how customers expect a lapsed subscription to behave, and it gives a clear explanation: *"Globex's subscription ended on Oct 15 (not renewed). Until it's renewed, Globex has the Free plan's entitlements: 3 seats, no API access."*

It needs one catalog rule, checked when the catalog loads: **a ladder's entry plan (rank 1) includes only unpaid features.** That's where `is_paid` keeps a job (validating the entry plan). A ladder without a free entry plan simply falls back to nothing, as in (c).

So unpaid features wouldn't be "independent of the subscription". They'd come from the **entry plan of the subscription's ladder**, whichever plan was bought.

The full rule, with (b):

| Subscription | Entitlements |
| --- | --- |
| usable | plan + add-ons + grants (quotas added) |
| not usable (date passed, or `Canceled`) | the ladder's entry plan only; add-ons and grants stop |
| none (e.g. an `OrganizationOnly` account) | nothing |
| any, **account suspended** | nothing (AC-6) |

**To rule:** (a), (b) or (c)?

I think you've named the real distinction. "Not usable" was mixing two things:

1. **The subscription itself**: the account's relationship with its plan. It either exists and is in force, or it's canceled.
2. **Paid access**: whether what was *paid for* is currently covered. That's a separate fact with its own time span.

So: **the subscription must be in force for anything to work, unpaid features included. Paid access is controlled at its own level.**

## Proposal: paid access as its own fact in time

```text
subscription
  subscription_id      sub_…
  account_id           → account (must be Independent)
  subscription_status  Active | Canceled          ← in force, or not; Canceled is final
  started_at

paid_access_period                                ← fact in time (CM-11), one row per covered span
  period_id            pap_…
  subscription_id      → subscription
  source               Trial | Payment | Courtesy
  starts_at, ends_at
  granted_by           (billing integration, CSR, or a system identity)
```

Paid features are covered while **a period includes the current instant**:
- **Billing** adds a `Payment` period each time it's paid.
- **A trial** is a `Trial` period.
- **A CSR keeping a customer going** during a billing problem adds a `Courtesy` period. That's a recorded, attributable decision, with its own permission and policy, instead of working around the subscription.

| Source | Subscription `Active`, paid access covered | Subscription `Active`, paid access **not** covered | Subscription `Canceled` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Plan and add-ons, **unpaid** features (seats) | ✓ | ✓ | ✗ |
| Plan and add-ons, **paid** features | ✓ | ✗ | ✗ |
| Grants | ✓ | ✗ (they're paid-level, as you said) | ✗ |

And as always, a **suspended account** stops everything (AC-6).

**What this simplifies:**
- **`Trialing` stops being a status.** It's a `Trial` period. A trial ending is just a period ending, and a trial converting is billing adding a `Payment` period right after it.
- **`status_ends_at` is no longer needed.** The dates live on the periods. (I know you asked for that name. Its purpose has moved to `paid_access_period.ends_at`.)
- **No entry-plan fallback.** A lapsed account keeps its plan's unpaid features while the subscription is in force.
- **The CSR's explanations name the exact fact:** "Globex's paid access ended on Oct 15 (last payment period); API access is unavailable until it's renewed."
- **History for free:** "was Globex covered in September?" is a query over the periods.

**One consequence to accept:** while paid access is lapsed, unpaid quotas stay at the plan's level (a Business account keeps 100 seats). I think that's fine, because the lapse is a temporary state: billing eventually either renews (a new period) or cancels (everything stops).

2. Paid access as `paid_access_period` rows (`Trial`, `Payment`, `Courtesy`)?
3. Unpaid features need an `Active` subscription; paid features and grants also need a covering period?

## Prompt — 2026-10-23T23:41:01.899Z

and somehow we need to model it that if it is not there then it means it is usable there is no enforceable period

### Assistant

Renamed: **`subscription_access_period`**.

## "No period means no enforced period"

I understand the need: some subscriptions shouldn't be time-bounded at all. Examples are a negotiated Enterprise contract, an internal or partner account, or the Free plan (which has nothing paid to bound anyway). There are two ways to model it, and the difference is what happens when something goes wrong:

| | (A) Absence means unbounded | (B) An open-ended period means unbounded |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Unbounded subscription | no rows in `subscription_access_period` | one row with `ends_at` empty (source `Contract`) |
| Periodic subscription, covered | a row covering now | a row covering now |
| Periodic subscription, **billing never posted the first period** | no rows → **reads as unbounded: paid features open** | no rows → **not covered: paid features closed** |
| How "unbounded" is recognized | inferred from missing data | stated by a row |

**The risk with (A):** a missing record *opens* access. If billing fails to post the first period of a new Pro subscription, the account gets paid features indefinitely, and nothing looks wrong. To make (A) safe, the subscription needs an explicit marker anyway (`access_enforcement`: `Unbounded` or `Periodic`), so that "no rows" is only read as unbounded when the subscription *says* so.

**My recommendation: (B).** One mechanism, and still fail-safe: no row means not covered, and unbounded access is a deliberate, attributable row:

```text
subscription_access_period
  period_id           sap_…
  subscription_id     → subscription
  source              Trial | Payment | Courtesy | Contract
  starts_at
  ends_at             empty = open-ended (no enforced end)
  granted_by
```

| Subscription | Periods |
| --- | --- |
| Pro, paid monthly | one `Payment` row per paid month |
| Enterprise under contract | one `Contract` row, `ends_at` empty |
| Free plan | none needed: it has no paid features, so there's nothing to cover |
| New Pro, billing late | none → paid features closed until billing posts the period |

If you prefer (A), I'd add the `access_enforcement` marker on the subscription so absence is never ambiguous.

Still discussing. Which way do you lean?

### Assistant

A period only ever covers **paid** features. Unpaid features (seats) need no period at all: they work while the subscription is `Active`. So the question becomes: *when paid features are covered without anyone paying, what's the source?* The source records **why** the paid features are covered, and three of the reasons involve no payment:

| Source | Paid? | Meaning | Typical `ends_at` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Payment` | yes | Billing received a payment for this span | end of the billing period |
| `Contract` | yes, on negotiated terms | Covered by a contract (invoiced separately, e.g. Enterprise) | contract end, or empty |
| `Trial` | no | Evaluation before buying | trial end |
| `Courtesy` | no | A CSR's temporary goodwill, e.g. during a billing problem | short, always set |
| **`Complimentary`** (new) | no | Free of charge **by policy**: partners, non-profits, internal accounts | often empty |

I'd add **`Complimentary`** to keep two "free" cases apart:
- **`Courtesy`** is a *temporary* goodwill decision by a CSR, always with an end. Policy can cap it (e.g. Tier 1: up to 14 days).
- **`Complimentary`** is a *standing* commercial decision, possibly open-ended. It would need a higher authority (a separate permission, not a Tier 1 action).

Separating them stops a goodwill gesture from quietly becoming indefinite free access. It also lets reporting answer "how much access are we giving away, and why?".

Recorded: **(B)**, an open-ended period with an empty `ends_at` means no enforced end, and "no row" means not covered.

**To rule:**
1. The sources `Payment`, `Contract`, `Trial`, `Courtesy`, `Complimentary`?
2. The earlier three, carried over:
   - a subscription is `Active` or `Canceled`
   - paid access is covered by `subscription_access_period` rows
   - unpaid features need an `Active` subscription, while paid features and grants also need a covering period.

## Prompt — 2026-10-23T23:48:21.013Z

Could we have an integrity problem with the disconnect at the db level between subscriptions and entitlements? I assume we determine the entitlement grants based on the subscriptions, right?

### Assistant

You're right to probe this. Let me separate two things, because only one of them has an integrity gap.

## Grants are not derived from subscriptions

Two different things are called "entitlement":
- **Effective entitlements** are **derived**: computed at every check from the subscription (plan, add-ons, access periods) plus grants. They're never stored, so there's nothing to fall out of sync. That part is safe by construction.
- **Entitlement grants** are **not derived**. A grant is an *exception*: a CSR gives a feature *on top of* the subscription. It's stored, because it's a decision someone made.

## Where the gap really is

A grant currently references the **account**, not the subscription. So the database doesn't tie a grant to the subscription it was given under:

| Situation | What happens today | Problem |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A grant created while the account has no subscription | stored, but never usable (no covering period) | a dangling exception the database allows |
| Subscription A canceled, then the customer subscribes again (B) | **the grant silently carries over to B** | goodwill given under one subscription reappears under another, with no one deciding it |
| Looking at a subscription's history | its grants must be found through the account and the dates | the link is inferred, not recorded |

## Proposal: a grant references its subscription

```text
entitlement_grant
  grant_id
  subscription_id   → subscription      (instead of account_id; the account is reached through it)
  feature_key, quota, reason, granted_by, case_request_id
  starts_at, expires_at, ended_at, ended_by, end_reason
```

- **The database enforces existence:** a grant can't exist without a subscription.
- **The lifecycle is tied:** canceling a subscription ends its grants (`end_reason = SubscriptionCanceled`, set in the same transaction). A new subscription starts with none.
- **EN-3 reads naturally:** a grant works while *its own* subscription is covered by an access period.
- **History is recorded,** not inferred: a subscription's grants are a direct query.

## One thing the database still can't hold

"A grant only where it adds something" (EN-4) depends on the plan *at the moment the grant is created*. If Initech gets an SSO grant on Free and later upgrades to Business, the grant becomes redundant. That's harmless: the entitlement simply lists both sources (plan and grant), and the grant expires on its own. I'd treat it as a creation-time rule (the grant use case checks it), not a permanent invariant.

**To rule:** should the grant reference `subscription_id` instead of `account_id`, with grants ending when their subscription is canceled? If yes, I'll update the diagram, the model document, and EN-4 (as an amendment, since the decision itself stands and only the link changes).

My assessment: **yes, it's the right call.** I looked for a reason not to and didn't find one that holds:

| Possible objection | Why it doesn't hold |
| --- | --- |
| "We lose grants for accounts without a subscription." | Such a grant could never work anyway: EN-3 requires a covering access period, which only a subscription has. `OrganizationOnly` accounts never subscribe, and they shouldn't receive grants either, since they don't hold entitlements. |
| "Grants disappear when the plan changes." | They don't. A plan change keeps the same subscription (EN-2 records the new plan as a new `subscription_plan` row), so the grant stays. |
| "Grants disappear when the customer cancels and resubscribes." | That's intended. Goodwill given under one subscription shouldn't silently reappear under another. If it's still warranted, a CSR grants again: a visible, attributable decision. |
| "We need the account on the grant for lookups." | It's one join away through the subscription. Storing it twice would invite the two to disagree. |

It also strengthens what we already decided:
- integrity moves into the database (a foreign key instead of a convention)
- the grant's lifecycle follows its subscription
- EN-3's rule ("nothing works without a covering period") applies to grants with no special case.

## Prompt — 2026-10-23T23:51:03.543Z

### Assistant

## Support: opening proposal

**Scope:** the CSR's work. That covers cases, the changes requested in them (case requests), and the approvals some of those changes need. The **conversation** itself isn't here: it lives in each case agent's own storage (proposal §3). PostgreSQL holds what must be queried, enforced and audited.

### Proposed model

```text
support_case                          fact in time (CM-11)
  case_id              case_…
  owner_employee_id    → internal identity (an employee)
  opened_at
  closed_at, closed_by, close_reason   (Resolved | Abandoned)

case_account_focus                    fact in time: which account the case is working on
  focus_id, case_id, account_id
  focused_at, focused_by, ended_at

case_request                          a requested change; lifecycle → status
  case_request_id      creq_…          (also the idempotency key for applying it)
  case_id
  request_kind         ChangePlan | CancelSubscription | CreateGrant | AddCourtesyPeriod
  account_id           the target, explicit (repeated in the confirmation)
  parameters           jsonb, typed per kind
  account_version      optimistic concurrency
  entitlement_diff     jsonb: exactly what the CSR was shown
  request_status       AwaitingConfirmation | AwaitingApproval | Applied | Canceled | Refused | Expired
  reason_codes         from the policy decision
  requested_by, requested_at, confirmed_at, applied_at, applied_by

case_request_approval                 at most one per request; lifecycle → status
  case_request_id      (primary key)
  approval_status      Pending | Approved | Denied | Expired
  workflow_instance_id
  requested_at, expires_at
  decided_by, decided_at, comment
```

**How a change flows:**

```text
CSR asks ──▶ case_request (AwaitingConfirmation, with diff)
               ├─ CSR cancels ─────────────────────────────▶ Canceled
               ├─ policy Deny ─────────────────────────────▶ Refused
               └─ CSR confirms ─┬─ policy Allow ───────────▶ Applied
                                └─ RequiresApproval ─▶ AwaitingApproval + approval (Pending, Workflow waits)
                                        ├─ supervisor approves ──▶ Applied   (re-authorized at apply time)
                                        ├─ supervisor denies ────▶ Refused
                                        └─ timeout ──────────────▶ Expired
```

### Decisions for you

| # | Question | My recommendation | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SU1 | Case lifecycle | **A fact in time:** opened, then closed with a reason. Owned by one employee, with no reassignment. Reopening means a new case. | It's a fact that starts and ends (CM-11). Reassigning cases goes on the production-readiness list. |
| SU2 | The account in focus: a column on the case, or its own history? | **Its own history** (`case_account_focus`) | The focus can change during a case, and which account a CSR was working on *when* is exactly what attribution needs. The current focus is the row not ended. |
| SU3 | The caller (stretch: caller verification) | **Designed, not built:** a `case_caller` fact (caller account, identified at, verified at, method), added when that scenario is built | Keeps the core small. The caller is an individual's account, which matches the attribution party "on behalf of". |
| SU4 | What is a case request? | **A requested change only.** Reads are recorded in the audit log, not as requests. | A request has a lifecycle (confirm, approve, apply) that reads don't have. |
| SU5 | Request parameters | **A `jsonb` column, typed per `request_kind`** by a TypeScript discriminated union and validated strictly. Each kind maps to exactly one permission (`ChangePlan` → `subscription.change-plan`, …). | A table per kind adds foreign keys on a few fields. One typed column fits a small, closed set of kinds. |
| SU6 | What the CSR confirms | **The stored request, exactly as shown:** target account, parameters, `entitlement_diff`, and the `account_version` it was computed against. Confirming re-checks the version (stale → refused, re-propose) and re-authorizes (CM-9). | The confirmation can't apply anything different from what was displayed. |
| SU7 | Approvals | **A separate row, at most one per request,** with its Workflow instance id, a 72-hour expiry, and the decider. **Separation of duties** (the decider isn't the requester) is a **policy**, not a column rule. | Approval has its own lifecycle and timing. Self-approval is an authorization question, so it belongs in the policy set (AC-1). |
| SU8 | The conversation | **Agent storage only.** PostgreSQL keeps case ownership, focus, requests and approvals. | The conversation is memory, not a system of record. Retention and export go on the production-readiness list. |

**For production-readiness:**
- case reassignment and queues
- service levels
- caller verification methods
- notifications beyond in-app
- conversation retention, export and privacy
- linking cases to external ticketing systems.

Your thoughts on SU1–SU8?

### Assistant

Accounts for internal identities: **I'd settle it right after Support**, together with Attribution, which it affects most. In Support I'll already write **internal account** wherever an employee acts or owns something, so nothing needs rework later.

One question to bring to that discussion: should internal accounts be **their own table**, or share the **same `account` table** as customers (with a kind)? A shared table would let every `*_by` column (`assigned_by`, `ended_by`, `granted_by`) become a real foreign key to one table, instead of a text id. That's a real integrity gain we don't have today.

## support_case

**Owner → internal account, and reassignment (SU1).** Agreed on both. Because a case can be reassigned, ownership becomes a fact in time:

```text
case_assignment
  assignment_id, case_id
  internal_account_id      who owns the case during this span
  assigned_at, assigned_by
  ended_at, ended_by, end_reason (Reassigned | CaseClosed)
```

The current owner is the row not ended. This keeps the full history ("Alex had it, then Blair").

## "Focus" (SU2)

Not a topic. It's **the customer account the case is about**: the account context for every question ("can Jane use SSO?" is asked about *that* account). It wasn't meant as dividing a case into parts.

**Can a case involve several customer accounts?** It could (Jane calls about her own account, then about Acme's). Two ways:
- **(a) One customer account per case:** a `customer_account_id` column, set once when the account is identified. Another account means another case.
- **(b) Several over time,** with a history table (what I proposed).

**My recommendation: (a).** It's simpler, attribution reads cleanly (case → one account), and each change request names its account anyway. I withdraw the focus table.

## Calling it "request" (SU4)

Agreed that the name should say it changes state. **My suggestion: change request** (`change_request`). It's the standard service-management term for a proposed change that's confirmed, possibly approved, then applied.

## Parameters (SU5)

They're **the details of the change**, which differ per kind. One column holds them, with a strict shape per kind:

```yaml
- change_kind: ChangePlan
  parameters:
    target_plan_key: pro
- change_kind: CancelSubscription
  parameters: {}
- change_kind: CreateGrant
  parameters:
    feature_key: audit-log-export
    quota: null
    duration_days: 7
    reason: Evaluating before upgrading
- change_kind: AddCourtesyPeriod
  parameters:
    duration_days: 10
    reason: Payment issue being resolved
```

The code defines each shape and rejects anything else. Each kind maps to exactly one permission (`ChangePlan` → `subscription.change-plan`).

## The account version, not a request version (SU6)

The version answers one question: **has the customer account changed since the CSR saw the proposal?** The request itself never changes after creation (a different change is a new request), so it has nothing to version. A timeline:

```text
10:00  Alex proposes: Initech Free → Pro.  Stored: account_version = 7, diff = "+ api-access, seats 3 → 25"
10:02  Blair, in another case, adds a 10-seat grant to Initech → account_version becomes 8
10:03  Alex confirms → version 7 ≠ 8 → refused as stale; the assistant re-proposes with a fresh diff
```

Without it, Alex would confirm a diff that's no longer true. On a valid confirmation, the server also **re-authorizes** (CM-9: role and status re-read from the database), then applies everything in one transaction.

## The entitlement diff

**What the account can use before vs after the change**, shown to the CSR before confirming:

```text
Initech: free → pro
  + api-access
  ~ seats: 3 → 25
```

It's stored so the record shows exactly what the CSR approved.

## Reason codes

They're the ids of the policies that decided (for example `grant-beyond-14-days-needs-supervisor`): why a change was refused or needs approval. They're already on the **audit record** of that decision, which references the change request. So **I'd drop them from the request**, keeping one home for them.

## Owner vs `requested_by`, and `applied_by`

| Column | Who | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| case owner (assignment) | who's responsible for the case **now** | Blair, after reassignment |
| `requested_by` | who **proposed** this change | Alex, before the reassignment |
| `applied_by` | who **executed** it | Alex (allowed directly), or `sys_approval_workflow` (after approval) |

## Approvals (SU7)

- **`workflow_instance_id`:** the id of the Cloudflare Workflow run that waits for the supervisor's decision. The decision is sent to that instance, and it traces the asynchronous part.
- **The two `requested_at` columns:** the approval's was the moment the change was sent for approval, which is the change request's confirmation. That's a duplicate, so **I'd drop it** and use `confirmed_at`.
- **Expiry:** what expires is **the wait for an approval**, not the change itself. That's why it sat on the approval.
- **A reason on the approval:** yes, as the supervisor's **comment** ("Denied: 60 days exceeds what this customer qualifies for"). The policy codes for the decision go to the audit record.
- **Why a separate table if it's at most one?** Because of CM-6, shape for many. Multi-step approvals (a supervisor, then finance) are a realistic next step, and a separate table holds them by dropping a constraint. If you'd rather keep it simple, the approval columns can live on the change request with no table. I'd keep the table, for consistency with CM-6.

## The caller (SU3)

Agreed, how the CSR verified the caller should be recorded:

```text
case_caller                 (designed now, built with the caller-verification scenario)
  case_id
  caller_account_id         the individual's account
  identified_at
  verified_at
  verification_method       KnowledgeCheck | OneTimeCode | …
  verified_by
```

## The gist of the case (SU8)

Yes. **`case_summary`** on the case, written by the assistant when the case closes and reviewed by the CSR: a durable record without the full conversation. Together with the change requests, approvals and audit records, the case's outcome is fully on record.

## The revised model

```text
support_case        case_id, customer_account_id, opened_at, closed_at, closed_by, close_reason, case_summary
case_assignment     case_id, internal_account_id, assigned_at, assigned_by, ended_at, ended_by, end_reason
case_caller         (designed) case_id, caller_account_id, identified_at, verified_at, verification_method, verified_by
change_request      change_request_id, case_id, change_kind, customer_account_id, parameters, account_version,
                    entitlement_diff, change_status, requested_by, requested_at, confirmed_at, applied_at, applied_by
change_approval     change_request_id, approval_status, workflow_instance_id, expires_at, decided_by, decided_at, comment
```

1. Ownership as `case_assignment`, so reassignment is supported?
2. One customer account per case?
3. Rename to `change_request` and `change_approval`?
4. Drop the reason codes (audit) and the approval's `requested_at` (use `confirmed_at`)?
5. Keep the approval as its own table (CM-6)?
6. `case_caller` designed now, with `verification_method`; `case_summary` on the case?
7. Settle internal accounts right after Support, including the shared-table question?

Answers to the open points:

## The column for the account a case is about

With a shared account table, the name should say the account's **role in the case** rather than its kind:

| Name | Reads as |
| --- | --- |
| **`subject_account_id`** | "the account this case is about" |
| `customer_account_id` | names the kind, not the role (the kind is enforced anyway) |
| `beneficiary_account_id` | suggests the case benefits the account, which isn't always true (cancellations, refusals) |
| `recipient_account_id` | suggests something is delivered |

**My recommendation: `subject_account_id`**, with a constraint that it's a customer account. (`support_case_account` would be a table name; with one account per case, it's a column.)

## The diff format

It's **structured data**, not a text diff. One entry per feature that changes, which the UI renders as a diff:

```yaml
entitlement_diff:
  - feature_key: api-access
    change: Added
    before: null
    after:
      quota: null
      sources:
        - plan:pro
  - feature_key: seats
    change: QuotaChanged
    before:
      quota: 3
      sources:
        - plan:free
    after:
      quota: 25
      sources:
        - plan:pro
```

`change` is one of `Added`, `Removed`, `QuotaChanged`.

## Reason codes and the word "policy"

You've spotted something: not every reason comes from a policy.

| Reason | Comes from |
| --- | --- |
| the actor doesn't hold the permission | **Access** (no role or assignment gives it: AC-1) |
| the subscription's access period ended | **Entitlement** |
| a Tier 1 grant over 14 days needs approval | **Policy** |

So a column named `policy_id` would be too narrow. I'd **namespace the codes by their source**, so each code says where it comes from:

```text
access.permission-not-held
entitlement.access-period-ended
policy.grant-beyond-14-days-needs-supervisor
```

The column lives on the audit record, so we'll define it in Attribution.

## A name for `workflow_instance_id` that isn't tied to one provider

**`process_instance_id`**: the id of the long-running process waiting for the decision. "Process instance" is the vendor-neutral term from process orchestration. Cloudflare Workflows is just one way to implement it, behind a port (CM-1).

## A change whose approval expired

Not in limbo: the change request moves to a **final** status, `Expired`. It's never applied, the CSR's case is notified, and a CSR can create a new change request if it's still needed. All the final statuses:

| Status | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `Applied` | done |
| `Canceled` | the CSR withdrew it, or the case closed while it was awaiting confirmation |
| `Refused` | policy denied it, or a supervisor denied it |
| `Expired` | the approval wasn't decided in time |

**One rule this adds:** closing a case cancels its change requests still awaiting confirmation, so nothing is left pending on a closed case.

## The approval's decision code

Agreed: a closed set of values, required when denying, plus an optional comment:

| `decision_reason` | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `InsufficientJustification` | the request's reason doesn't support it |
| `ExceedsCustomerAgreement` | beyond what this customer's terms allow |
| `RiskConcern` | fraud or abuse risk |
| `DuplicateRequest` | already handled elsewhere |
| `Other` | requires a comment |

## The caller: a table or columns? (SU6)

You're right. With one customer account per case, **columns on `support_case`** are enough:

```text
caller_account_id, caller_verified_at, caller_verification_method, caller_verified_by
```

Failed verification attempts are security events, so they go to the **audit records**, not to the case. A separate table would only pay off with several callers per case, which nothing needs. They're designed now and built with the caller-verification scenario.

## To rule

1. `subject_account_id`?
2. Namespaced reason codes (`access.`, `entitlement.`, `policy.`), to be defined in Attribution?
3. `process_instance_id`?
4. The final statuses, plus "closing a case cancels its pending change requests"?
5. The `decision_reason` values?
6. Caller columns on `support_case`?

### Assistant

## The diff: JSON Patch

Good thinking. The YAML was only my notation for showing it here; it's stored as JSON. And there is a standard for it: **JSON Patch (RFC 6902)**. It has one feature that makes it a particularly good fit here, the **`test`** operation.

The diff becomes a patch over the account's **effective-entitlements document**:

```json
{
  "seats":      { "quota": 3,    "sources": ["plan:free"] }
}
```

Free → Pro becomes:

```json
[
  { "op": "test",    "path": "/seats/quota",   "value": 3 },
  { "op": "replace", "path": "/seats/quota",   "value": 25 },
  { "op": "replace", "path": "/seats/sources", "value": ["plan:pro"] },
  { "op": "add",     "path": "/api-access",    "value": { "quota": null, "sources": ["plan:pro"] } }
]
```

- **It's a standard**, with libraries to apply and validate it. That's better than a format we invented.
- **The `test` operations record the "before" values.** The UI shows "seats: 3 → 25" from the `test` and the `replace`.
- **The `test` operations can't succeed against a changed document.** If the entitlements moved since the proposal, the patch no longer applies cleanly, which is a second staleness check on top of `account_version`.

The alternative, JSON Merge Patch (RFC 7396), is simpler but can't record before-values. **My recommendation: JSON Patch (RFC 6902)** with `test` operations, stored as `jsonb` in `entitlement_diff`.

## Who sets a change to `Expired`?

**The approval process does, when its wait times out.** When a change needs approval:

```text
10:00  CSR confirms → change AwaitingApproval; approval Pending, expires_at = +72 h
       → a process instance starts and waits: "decision received" OR "72 h passed"

Case A — a supervisor decides in time
  12:00  decision sent to the process instance → it wakes up
         → one transaction: re-check, re-authorize, apply (or refuse), set the statuses, write the audit record

Case B — nobody decides
  +72 h  the wait times out → the process instance wakes up (acting as sys_approval_workflow)
         → one transaction: approval Expired, change Expired, audit record → the CSR's case is notified
```

So yes: **applying is a single database transaction**, and so is expiring. Each status change and its audit record commit together, or not at all.

It's **fail-safe twice**, like grant expiry: a decision that arrives after `expires_at` is refused even if the process wakes up late. The deadline is enforced by the data, not only by the timer.

## Caller or subject?

Both, because they're different things:

| | Meaning | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **`subject_account_id`** | The customer account the case is **about** | any customer account (an organization's or an individual's) |
| **`caller_account_id`** | The **person** contacting support | always an individual's account |

| Situation | Subject | Caller |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Jane calls about Acme | `acc_acme` | `acc_jane` |
| Jane calls about her own account | `acc_jane` | `acc_jane` |
| Olivia (Acme's owner) calls about Acme | `acc_acme` | `acc_olivia` |

Keeping them apart is what later enables the rule "a caller can only request changes to a subject account where their own role allows it". For example, Jane (Admin) may not be allowed to request a plan change for Acme, while Olivia (Owner) may.

3. The `decision_reason` values (`InsufficientJustification`, `ExceedsCustomerAgreement`, `RiskConcern`, `DuplicateRequest`, `Other`)?
4. `caller_account_id` and its verification columns on `support_case`, next to `subject_account_id`?

### Assistant

## Does this need a cron job?

Not for approvals. The approval process is a **durable process instance with a timer**. It sleeps until either the decision event arrives or the deadline passes, and the platform wakes it up. Nothing polls. With Cloudflare Workflows, that's a single step: "wait for the event, with a 72-hour timeout". It costs nothing while waiting and survives restarts. Behind our port (CM-1), any orchestrator with durable timers would do.

Where a **scheduled sweep** fits better is grant expiry: a Workers Cron Trigger every few minutes records the grants that have expired. A sweep is enough there because evaluation already ignores expired grants at the exact instant (EN-5). The sweep only makes the end visible, so a few minutes' lag is harmless.

| Deadline | Mechanism | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Approval timeout | a timer inside the approval's process instance | the instance is already waiting for the decision |
| Grant expiry | a scheduled sweep (cron trigger) | access already stops at evaluation; the sweep only records it |

## The caller needs an account

Yes, and it always has one: the caller is an individual, and every customer owns an account (CI-3). Until the CSR identifies the caller, `caller_account_id` is empty, and an unidentified caller can't be the basis for requesting changes.

**The model as you shaped it:**
- **`support_case`** has `subject_account_id` (set once), the caller and its verification as columns (designed, built with that scenario), opened and closed facts, a close reason, and `case_summary`.
- **`case_assignment`** records ownership as a fact in time, so cases can be reassigned. The assignee is an internal account.
- **`change_request`**:
  - a closed set of kinds, each requiring one permission
  - typed `parameters`
  - `account_version` and an RFC 6902 `entitlement_diff`
  - final statuses.

- **`change_approval`** is its own table (CM-6), with `process_instance_id`, `expires_at`, and a closed `decision_reason` set plus an optional comment.

**The ADRs:**
- **SU-1:** case ownership through assignments
- **SU-2:** one subject account per case, and a separate caller
- **SU-3:** change requests, for changes only
- **SU-4:** confirming applies exactly what was shown
- **SU-5:** every change request ends in a final status
- **SU-6:** approvals in their own table, with a durable timer
- **SU-7:** the conversation stays in the agent; the summary goes in the database

- **Entitlement:** grants reference `change_request_id` (the diagram is re-rendered).
- **Access:** the system permission `case-request.apply` → `change-request.apply`.
