### Assistant

Answers, and the rename applied.

## `caller_verified_by`

**The internal account (the CSR) who verified the caller.** It's usually the case's assignee at that moment, but not always: after a reassignment, Blair may own the case while the record still shows that Alex verified the caller. Recording who verified matters because a weak verification is a security finding, and someone has to answer for it.

## `subject_account_id` repeated in `change_request`

Strictly it's redundant, since the case already says it. I kept it for three reasons:
1. **It can't disagree.** The composite foreign key `(case_id, subject_account_id)` → `support_case` forces it to equal the case's subject, so the duplication carries no risk of the two drifting.
2. **No change before the subject is known.** On the case, the subject is empty until identified. On the change request it's required. So the foreign key also guarantees **a change can't be created before the case has a subject**. Without the column, that's an application rule.
3. **The record stands alone.** "All changes ever requested for Acme" is a direct query, and an audit or explanation reads the change without a join.

If you'd rather keep strictly one copy, dropping it is safe; reason 2 then moves into the change-request use case. I'd keep it.

## `subject_account_version`

Renamed in the diagram, the model document and SU-4. ✓

## `assigned_by` and `ended_by` in `case_assignment`

**Who made the assignment, and who ended it.** These differ from the assignee:

| Event | Assignee | `assigned_by` | `ended_by` | `end_reason` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Alex opens a case (it's his) | Alex | Alex | — | — |
| Sam (supervisor) moves it to Blair | Alex's row ends | — | Sam | `Reassigned` |
| | Blair's new row | Sam | — | — |
| Blair closes the case | Blair's row ends | — | Blair | `CaseClosed` |

## `decided_by` in `change_approval`

**The internal account (a supervisor) who approved or denied.** It's empty while the approval is `Pending`, and stays empty when it `Expired`: nobody decided, so nobody is named. The audit record for the expiry names `sys_approval_process` as the actor that recorded it.

| A customer (Jane) | her individual account | `acc_jane` |

## Prompt — 2026-10-24T02:33:19.748Z

### Assistant

## Internal accounts and the shared account table: opening proposal

**The idea:** every actor acts through an account, customers and internal identities alike, and all accounts share one table. It's the same supertype pattern as CI-1 and II-1:

```text
account                               supertype: every account, whoever holds it
  account_id          acc_… (customer) · iac_… (internal)
  account_scope       Customer | Internal
  account_name
  account_status      Active | Suspended      (fail-safe, kept by holds: CI-5)
  created_at

customer_account                      subtype (account_scope = Customer)
  account_id          → account
  customer_id, customer_kind          (unique customer_id: one account per customer)
  operating_mode      Independent | OrganizationOnly
  version             optimistic concurrency (subject_account_version)

internal_account                      subtype (account_scope = Internal)
  account_id          → account
  internal_identity_id, identity_kind (unique internal_identity_id: one account per identity)

account_hold                          now on any account
  hold_reason         OperationalSuspension | CustomerRemoved | NoActiveMembership | InternalIdentityInactive
```

### Decisions for you

| # | Question | My recommendation | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IA1 | Shape of the shared table | **Supertype `account`, with subtypes `customer_account` and `internal_account`** | Shared columns once; specific ones required in their subtype. The same pattern as customers (CI-1) and internal identities (II-1). |
| IA2 | Where it lives | **A new small subsystem, Account:** `account`, both subtypes, and `account_hold`. Customer Identity and Internal Identity keep only identities. | Accounts are now shared by both identity kinds, so neither identity subsystem should own them. Holds are an account-level mechanism. |
| IA3 | Status and holds for internal accounts | **On the supertype, for every account.** A new hold reason, `InternalIdentityInactive`, mirrors `CustomerRemoved` (placed when an internal identity is suspended or terminated). `version` stays on `customer_account` only. | The same fail-safe everywhere: a compromised system account can be suspended like any other. Optimistic concurrency only matters for accounts that are the subject of changes. |
| IA4 | Accounts per internal identity | **Exactly one now** (unique `internal_identity_id`), shaped for many | This is where "employees across several products" (II-2's alternative) would land later: one account per product scope. |
| IA5 | Access for internal actors | **Internal role and permission assignments are held by the internal account** (`internal_identity_id` → `account_id`). Birthright roles still read the identity's job attributes. | Mirrors customers (roles on accounts, CI-4). The actor in every decision is an account. |
| IA6 | Enforcing which accounts a `*_by` column allows | **A foreign key to the subtype table where the scope matters**: internal-only columns reference `internal_account`, others reference `account`. Employee-vs-system distinctions are enforced by permissions (only employee roles hold `approval.decide`). | A foreign key to a subtype needs no extra kind column in every table. Permissions already separate employees from system processes (AC-2). |
| IA7 | The session token's subject | **The employee's internal account** (`iac_…`). The credential stays with the employee identity (II-4). | Authentication proves the *identity*; every decision is made for the *account* that acts. The token carries the actor. |
| IA8 | Id prefixes | **`acc_` for customer accounts, `iac_` for internal accounts** | One table, but the kind stays visible in logs and audit records. |

### What it changes in documents already ruled

- **Customer Identity:** `account` and `account_hold` move out to Account. Customer Identity keeps customers, organizations, individuals and memberships. CI-3 and CI-5 stay true and get an amendment pointing to the new home.
- **Internal Identity:** II-2 ("employees act as themselves, no account") is **superseded** by a new record: employees act through internal accounts.
- **Access:** the internal assignments reference `internal_account`; AC-5 gets an amendment.
- **Every `*_by` column** in every subsystem becomes a foreign key to `account` or `internal_account`. The convention is recorded once (`*_by` references an account).
- **CM-3 / the sign-in design:** the token's subject is the internal account (IA7).
- **Diagrams:** a new `account.er.puml`. The other subsystem diagrams show `account` as a grey reference box.

**For production-readiness:** several internal accounts per identity (product scopes), and workload credentials for system accounts (already listed).

Your thoughts on IA1–IA8?

### Assistant

## The caller's authority: how do we know Jane may ask for changes to Acme?

You're right, that's missing. The building blocks exist; we just haven't connected them. **A caller's authority comes from their own role assignments in the subject account** (AC-4). Jane may request a plan change for Acme only if her account holds the right **customer-side permission** in Acme's account.

The gap is in the permission list: the permissions for these changes today are all held by **employees** (the CSR performs the change). By AC-2, a permission is held by one principal kind, so customers need their own:

| Change kind | The CSR needs (Employee) | The caller needs (Individual), in the subject account |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ChangePlan` | `subscription.change-plan` | `subscription.request-plan-change` (Owner, Billing) |
| `CancelSubscription` | `subscription.cancel` | `subscription.request-cancellation` (Owner) |
| `CreateGrant` | `grant.create` | `grant.request` (Owner, Admin) |
| `AddCourtesyPeriod` | `subscription.add-courtesy-period` | none: it's the CSR's own initiative |

Then **a change request needs both**: the CSR holds the employee permission, *and* the identified, verified caller holds the customer permission in the subject account. In the database that's three new rows in `resource_operation` plus their role bundles, all seeded. No new table is needed: `account_role_assignment` already records who may act for which account.

**For production-readiness:** *authorized contacts*, meaning people who aren't members but may act for an account (an external accountant or a reseller). That would need an explicit delegation table.

## The word instead of "scope"

We replaced `role_scope` and `permission_scope` with **`principal_kind`**. Here, following the `customer_kind` and `identity_kind` pattern, it becomes **`account_kind`** (`Customer` or `Internal`).

## `account_name`

The account's **display name**, for the UI and the chat: "Acme Corp", "Jane Doe". It matters most once an identity has several accounts and they need telling apart ("Acme Corp — Production", "Acme Corp — Staging"). Without it, the assistant could only show ids.

## `version` on the supertype

Agreed. It costs nothing and covers any account that becomes the subject of a change later. It moves to `account`.

## Operating mode: an immutable characteristic?

Good point. "Mode" does suggest something that changes, and in our A2 discussion it *did* change: Bob's account was to become `Independent` when he subscribed. If it's an **inherent, immutable** characteristic instead, then:
- **Bob subscribing means a second account**, a personal one. That fits the N-accounts shape we're keeping open.
- **The `NoActiveMembership` hold only ends** if Bob rejoins an organization; it no longer ends by the account changing mode.

**My recommendation: make it immutable** and rename it to **`account_category`**, with clearer values:

| Value | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `Standalone` | can hold its own subscription (every organization's account; a personal account) |
| `OrganizationMember` | acts only through organization accounts; never holds a subscription |

## `account_hold`: a table, and what "hold" means (your 3)

Yes, it's a table. **It really is something temporal:** a block **placed** on an account at one moment and **released** at a later one, like a hold on a bank account. It's a fact in time (CM-11), not a denial reason. Denial reasons are **derived** from holds: when Acme is suspended, the explanation lists its unreleased holds as reason codes (`account.operational-suspension`).

**On the column name:** our closed-enum columns so far use the pattern `<thing>_reason`: `end_reason`, `close_reason`, `decision_reason`. So `hold_reason` follows it. `reason_codes` (plural, namespaced) is the audit record's list. I'd keep `hold_reason` for consistency. If you prefer `reason_code` everywhere, I'd rename all the `_reason` columns together.

## A generic name for the subsystem (your 2)

| Name | Fit |
| --- | --- |
| **Account** | Short; already the domain term; also covers future service accounts or API clients |
| Operational Identity | Precise (CI-3's phrase), but long |
| Principal | IAM's term, but only for *actors*, and accounts are also *acted on* |

**My recommendation: Account.** Anything future that acts or is acted on (a service account, an API client) would be another account kind, so the name stays valid.

## N accounts per identity: consistency (your 4)

It's already consistent. Both sides are shaped for many and constrained to one in the same way:
- **Customers:** `UNIQUE (customer_id)` on `customer_account`.
- **Internal identities:** `UNIQUE (internal_identity_id)` on `internal_account`.

Dropping either constraint opens N, with no table change (CM-6).

## Database constraints or code? (your 6)

This deserves a clear principle. The trade-off:

| | Database constraint | Code (domain and use cases) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Holds even for | seeds, scripts, bugs, any future service writing to the database | only paths that go through our use cases |
| Good at | structure: existence, uniqueness, what *type* of thing a column holds | rules: who may do what, state transitions, eligibility, conditions |
| Cost of change | a migration | a code change, with tests |

**My recommendation, as a common ADR:** **the database enforces structure; code enforces rules.**
- **Database:** a column's *type*, when it's a fixed fact about the column. `assignee_account_id` is always an internal account, and `member_account_id` is always an individual's account. These get a foreign key to the subtype, or a composite key.
- **Code:** anything that is really *authorization* or *business policy*. "Who may have assigned this?", "may this account decide approvals?", "is this add-on eligible?".

Applied here: **every `*_by` column references the generic `account`.** Who's allowed to *be* the actor is an authorization decision, already made by Access and Policy before the write. Encoding it again in constraints would duplicate the rule in a place that's harder to change. That also simplifies IA6.

## Several accounts per identity: which account does a session use? (your 7)

Your model, choosing the account in the authentication flow, is a sound and common pattern; identity providers do this for tenants and organizations. How I'd shape it, with the security points:

1. **Authenticate the identity first**: password (later, MFA).
2. **Then bind the session to one account:**
   - **declared** in the sign-in request ("sign me in to account X"), **or**
   - **chosen** from the identity's accounts right after authentication, **or**
   - **defaulted** when there's only one, as in our demo.
3. **Issue a token whose subject is that account.** Every decision in the session acts as that account (IA7).
4. **Switching accounts means a new token** (a token exchange), not a flag inside the session. Whether it needs **step-up** (re-entering a credential or MFA) is a **policy** per target account. Sensitive accounts would require it; ordinary ones wouldn't.

**Security points:**
- **Don't reveal accounts before authentication.** If the account is declared up front, the response to a wrong password must be identical whether or not the account exists or is linked to the identity. Membership is checked only after authentication succeeds.
- **One account per token** limits the damage a stolen token can do, and makes attribution exact.
- **Switching is an auditable event**, because it's a token issuance.

For the demo, every employee has one internal account, so step 2 is automatic. I'd record the full model in the sign-in design, with switching and step-up on the production-readiness list.

## Recorded

- IA1: the supertype
- IA5: internal assignments held by internal accounts
- IA8: `acc_` and `iac_`

**To rule:**
1. Caller authority: the customer-side permissions above, so a change needs both the CSR's and the caller's permission?
2. `account_kind`; `version` on the supertype; `account_name` kept?
3. `account_category` (`Standalone`, `OrganizationMember`), immutable?
4. Keep `hold_reason` (the `_reason` pattern), or switch everything to `reason_code`?
5. Subsystem name: **Account**?
6. A common ADR, "the database enforces structure; code enforces rules", with every `*_by` column referencing the generic `account`?
7. Session bound to one account, chosen at sign-in; switching by token exchange, with step-up by policy (designed; the demo has one account)?

## The attribution chain of a change request

Agreed. One gap shows when tracing the chain: **the caller lives on the case, not on the change request.** If a case's caller is ever corrected or re-identified, a change requested earlier would point to the *current* caller, not the one it was requested for. I'd snapshot it:

```text
change_request
  on_behalf_of_account_id    the verified caller when the change was requested; empty for CSR-initiated changes (a courtesy period)
```

The full chain of an approved grant then reads:

| Part | Who | Recorded on |
| --- | --- | --- |
| On behalf of | `acc_jane` (verified caller, Admin at Acme) | `change_request.on_behalf_of_account_id` |
| Requested by | `iac_alex` | `change_request.requested_by` |
| Approved by | `iac_sam` | `change_approval.decided_by` |
| Applied by | `iac_sys_approval_process` | `change_request.applied_by` |
| Subject | `acc_acme` | `change_request.subject_account_id` |

Attribution will record the same parts on the audit records.

## A name for the account's display name

| Option | Reads as | Concern |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **`account_display_name`** | exactly what it is for | none |
| `account_label` | could be read as a tag; accounts may get several tags later | ambiguous |
| `account_title` | unusual for an account | — |
| `display_name` | fine, but breaks the `account_` prefix the other columns share | inconsistent |

**My recommendation: `account_display_name`.**

## What a hold means, as a status

Yes, it translates directly. **An account with at least one unreleased hold has `account_status = Suspended`**; with none, it's `Active` (CI-5). `Suspended` means it can neither act nor be acted on (AC-6). The status is the single fail-safe column, and the holds say why.

## Anticipating future concepts tied to accounts

Good instinct. What's expensive to change later isn't adding a table or a column. It's **changing what existing references point to**. Today every role assignment, subscription and case points to an `account`. The question is whether some future concept would need those references to point at something else.

These are the likely future concepts, and whether the current shape absorbs them:

| Future concept | Example | What it would be | Absorbed without restructuring? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Account relationships** | authorized contacts (an accountant), resellers managing customers, parent and child accounts, **guardians of minors** | `account_relationship` (from account, to account, kind, start and end) | **Yes.** It's a new table. It would also generalize AC-4's "justifying relationship" (today: same account or organization membership) to any relationship kind |
| **Resources within an account** | projects, environments, zones; roles on part of an account | `resource` (belongs to an account) | **Mostly.** Role assignments would gain an optional resource target. Existing rows stay valid (empty means the whole account) |
| **Credentials owned by an account** | API keys, service tokens | `account_credential` | **Yes.** A new table |
| **Account settings and contacts** | billing contact, preferences | new tables | **Yes** |
| **New kinds of account** | a service account, an API client | a new `account_kind` value and subtype | **Yes.** That's what the supertype is for |

**My conclusion:** the shape is sound. Nothing on this list requires re-pointing existing references. The one I'd **name now as the extension point** is **account relationships**, because it unifies four things we've already listed as production concerns (authorized contacts, resellers, hierarchy, guardianship), and it's the natural home for "who may act for whom". I'd record it in the Account ADR's consequences and in production-readiness, without building it.

## Several accounts: declared at authentication

Recorded:
- The account is **declared in the authentication request**; it isn't chosen afterwards.
- It may be **omitted only when the identity has exactly one account**.
- A **default-account preference** is a production convenience.
- Failure responses are identical whether or not the account exists or is linked to the identity.

This goes into the sign-in design, with switching (token exchange, step-up by policy) on the production-readiness list.

## To rule

1. `on_behalf_of_account_id` on `change_request`, a snapshot of the verified caller?
2. `account_display_name`?
3. Record **account relationships** as the named extension point (not built)?

Already recorded: `account_kind`, `account_category` (`Standalone` | `OrganizationMember`, immutable), `account_hold` with `hold_reason`, CM-13, the declared-account sign-in, and the caller's authority (the customer-side permissions, so a change needs both the CSR's and the caller's).

## Prompt — 2026-10-24T17:38:24.077Z

Still a bit confused; the on behalf of is in reality the subject_account_id? right? and the caller is who is operating on it because there are some permissions that allows it? trying to make sure I understand

### Assistant

Almost. One correction: **the caller doesn't operate on the account; the CSR does.** The caller *asks*, and her permissions decide whether she's *allowed to ask*.

| Who | Part | Why they're allowed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Acme** (`subject_account_id`) | The account **changed**, on whose behalf everything happens | — |
| **Jane** (`caller_account_id`) | **Asks** for the change | Her role in Acme's account (Admin) includes the customer permission, e.g. `grant.request` |
| **Alex** (`requested_by`) | **Performs** it in our system | His role includes the employee permission, e.g. `grant.create` |

So:
- **"On behalf of"** is indeed the subject: Alex changes Acme, on Acme's behalf.
- **Two permission checks** stand behind every customer-requested change:
  1. Jane may **ask** for it (her role in Acme).
  2. Alex may **perform** it (his role as a CSR).

  If either fails, the change is refused. Jane being a Viewer at Acme would stop it, even though Alex could perform it.
- **When the CSR acts on his own initiative** (a courtesy period), there's no caller to check. Only Alex's permission applies, and `caller_account_id` stays empty.

In short: **the subject is changed, the caller asks, the CSR performs.**

Great; let's continue to Attribution

### Assistant

## Attribution: opening proposal

**Scope:** knowing **who did what, to what, why it was allowed or refused, and being able to prove it later.** Your four topics map to three distinct mechanisms. They're often blurred, and keeping them apart is the first decision:

| | **Audit records** | **Traces** | **Logs** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Purpose | **Evidence:** who did what, and why it was allowed | Following one request across Worker, agent, process and database | Operational diagnosis |
| Completeness | **Every** decision and change, never sampled | May be sampled | May be sampled or dropped |
| Retention | Long, governed | Short | Short |
| Personal data | Account ids only | Ids only | None |
| Lives in | **PostgreSQL** (this subsystem) | Platform observability | Platform observability |

Non-repudiation is a property of the audit records: they can't be altered or denied afterwards.

### Proposed model

```text
audit_record                          append-only; one row per event
  audit_record_id      aud_…
  sequence_number      strictly increasing (orders the hash chain)
  occurred_at
  actor_account_id     → account      who executed it (required, except a failed sign-in)
  session_id           the session token's jti: groups a session's records
  trace_id             W3C trace context: links to traces and logs
  action               what was attempted: account.read · change-request.apply · session.sign-in …
  outcome              Allow | Deny | RequiresApproval | Succeeded | Failed
  reason_codes         text[], namespaced: access.… · entitlement.… · policy.… · account.… · session.…
  policy_set_version
  subject_account_id   → account      the account acted on (the account context), if any
  case_id, change_request_id
  detail               jsonb, typed per action, never personal data
  previous_hash, record_hash          tamper evidence

audit_party                           the other parties, each with its part
  audit_record_id, account_id → account
  party_role           Caller | Requester | Approver
```

The chain of an approved grant, as audit records:

| Record | Actor | Action | Outcome | Parties |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `iac_alex` | `grant.create` (requested) | RequiresApproval | Caller `acc_jane` |
| 2 | `iac_sam` | `approval.decide` | Allow | Requester `iac_alex` |

### Decisions for you

| # | Question | My recommendation | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AT1 | Three mechanisms | **Only audit records in the database; traces and logs to platform observability.** All three carry the `trace_id`. | Evidence needs completeness and integrity; diagnostics need volume and speed. One store can't serve both. |
| AT2 | One actor, plus parties | **`actor_account_id` is always the executor.** Other participants go in `audit_party` with their part. The subject stays a column, since every record has at most one. | Answers "who did it?" (non-repudiation) and "who was involved?" (traceability). Shaped for many parties (CM-6). |
| AT3 | What is audited | **Every authorization decision** (reads included, denials included), **every state change**, **every authentication event** (sign-in success and failure, renewal, sign-out, account switch) and **every system action** (grant expiry, approval timeout) | "Every decision is audited" is already a rule in our constitution. Reads matter: who looked at a customer's data is an audit question. |
| AT4 | Atomicity | **A change and its audit record commit in the same transaction. If the audit record can't be written, the action fails, reads included.** | Fail closed: nothing happens unaudited. |
| AT5 | Tamper evidence | **A hash chain:** each record stores the previous record's hash and its own hash, computed over its content. A verification process walks the chain. Append-only is enforced by the database role (`INSERT` and `SELECT` only, CM-13). | Altering or deleting a record breaks the chain detectably. Signing and anchoring the chain externally go to production-readiness. |
| AT6 | Reason code namespaces | **A closed set:** `access.`, `entitlement.`, `policy.`, `account.`, `session.` | Each code says where the reason came from, as agreed. |
| AT7 | Correlation | **`trace_id` (W3C trace context) on every record, plus `session_id` (the token's `jti`)** | Links one request's audit records to its traces and logs, and groups a session's activity. |
| AT8 | A failed sign-in has no actor | **`actor_account_id` may be empty only for `session.sign-in` with outcome `Failed`** (a check constraint). The attempted email is stored as a keyed hash (HMAC) in `detail`. | Repeated attempts against one identity stay detectable without storing the email in the clear. |
| AT9 | The conversation | **Not audited as text.** Tool calls are audited through the decisions they cause, and `detail` references the tool call id. | The conversation lives in the agent (SU-7). Evidence is about decisions, not prose. |

**A cost to name for AT5:** a single hash chain serializes audit writes (each record needs the previous hash). That's fine at demo scale. At production scale, the usual answer is one chain per partition (per account, or per time window), and that goes on the production-readiness list.

**For production-readiness:**
- signing records, and anchoring the chain to an external timestamping service
- partitioned chains
- retention and legal holds
- exporting to a SIEM (security event management)
- alerting on denial bursts or brute force
- privacy erasure vs. immutable records (already listed).

Your thoughts on AT1–AT9?

### Assistant

## "Actor" instead of "caller" in Support?

I'd keep **caller**. In Attribution, **actor** means the *executor*: the account that performed the action (AT2). The caller never executes anything; she asks, and the CSR performs. Calling her "actor" in Support would give the word two meanings. In Attribution she appears as a **party** with the part `Caller`.

## `audit_party`: its purpose

An audit record has exactly one actor, but **a variable number of other participants**: none for a simple read, one caller for a request, a caller, requester and approver for an applied grant. Instead of one column per possible role (mostly empty), each participant is a row:

```text
audit_record   aud_3   actor iac_sys_approval_process   action change-request.apply   subject acc_acme
audit_party    aud_3   acc_jane    Caller
audit_party    aud_3   iac_alex    Requester
audit_party    aud_3   iac_sam     Approver
```

It's the same "one table per many-valued fact" pattern as the bundles. A new kind of participant later is a new `party_role` value, not a new column.

## `sequence_number`, and how ids are generated

**Purpose:** the hash chain needs an unambiguous "previous record". Timestamps can tie, and clocks on different machines disagree. So each record gets the next number, assigned in the database when it's inserted, and "previous" means number − 1.

**Would time-ordered ids (UUIDv7) replace it?** No, for two reasons:
1. **Ordering doesn't survive base64url, as you suspected.** Base64url's alphabet (`A–Z a–z 0–9 - _`) isn't in ASCII order, so encoded strings don't sort like the bytes they encode.
2. **Even hex-encoded UUIDv7 isn't strictly ordered across writers.** It has millisecond precision plus random bits, and is generated in the application on different machines. Two records in the same millisecond can come out in either order. The chain needs a *strict* order assigned at one place, which is the database.

**How ids are generated:** this deserves a common decision, because it applies to every table. My recommendation:
- **Generate in the application** (an `IdGenerator` port), as **UUIDv7**. The time prefix keeps new rows close together in indexes.
- **Store as PostgreSQL's native `uuid`** (16 bytes): smaller and faster than text.
- **Show externally** (API, chat, logs) as **prefix + base64url**: `aud_AZK6v3Wr7kq9Yg3hTs1wXQ` (22 characters for the 128 bits). Your byte-saving goal applies where ids are actually transmitted, and the prefix keeps the kind visible.
- **Nothing relies on id order.** Ordering comes from timestamps, or from `sequence_number` for the chain.

That would be **CM-14** if you agree.

## `session_id` vs `trace_id`, and W3C Trace Context

They're two granularities:

| | Groups | Typical question |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `trace_id` | **one operation**: a request and everything it triggers (the agent, a tool call, database queries, a Workflow start) | "What happened while processing this message?" |
| `session_id` | **every operation of one sign-in session**, over hours | "This token may have been stolen: what did it do?" |

**W3C Trace Context** is the standard (a W3C Recommendation) for passing a trace across services. It's an HTTP header:

```text
traceparent: 00-4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736-00f067aa0ba902b7-01
             │  └── trace-id (16 bytes, hex) ──┘ └ parent span ─┘ └ flags (01 = sampled)
```

- **At the edge,** if a request has no `traceparent`, one is created. Each hop (Worker → agent → database or Workflow) keeps the **trace-id** and creates a new **span id** for its own work.
- **Platform tracing** (Cloudflare Workers supports OpenTelemetry) records the spans. The audit record stores the trace-id, so you can jump from evidence to diagnostics.
- **One nuance for our chat:** the WebSocket stays open for hours, so **each chat message starts a new trace**. One trace per connection would make traces meaningless.

## `action`: the same template as permissions?

Yes, deliberately. `action` uses the **same `<resource>.<operation>` template**:
- **For authorization decisions, it's exactly the permission key** that was checked: `account.read`, `grant.create`.
- **Some actions aren't permissions**, because nobody "holds" them: `session.sign-in`, `session.renew`, `session.sign-out`. They're added as audit-only actions in the same template.

So the values of `action` are the permission keys plus a few session events. It's a closed list, validated in code (the feature permissions grow with the catalog, so a database check list wouldn't keep up).

## `outcome`: what are its values?

An enumeration in a `CHECK` column (PascalCase, as CM-12 says for enumeration values):

| Value | For |
| --- | --- |
| `Allow`, `Deny`, `RequiresApproval` | authorization decisions |
| `Succeeded`, `Failed` | executions: a change applied, a sign-in attempted, an expiry recorded |

## `reason_codes`: how `text[]` is stored

PostgreSQL has **native arrays**: `reason_codes text[]` is one column holding a list. A GIN index makes "every record with reason `policy.grant-beyond-14-days-needs-supervisor`" fast, and a `CHECK` validates every element against the pattern `^(access|entitlement|policy|account|session)\.[a-z0-9-]+$`.

The alternative is a child table (`audit_record_reason`) with a foreign key to a catalog of codes. It doesn't fit here, because policy reason codes are policy ids, which live in versioned policy files rather than in the database. **I'd keep the native array.**

## A sample `detail`

Typed per action, never personal data:

```json
{ "action": "change-request.apply",
  "detail": { "change_kind": "ChangePlan", "from_plan_key": "free", "to_plan_key": "pro" } }

{ "action": "account.read",
  "detail": { "tool": "explainMemberAccess", "tool_call_id": "call_8fK2", "feature_key": "sso", "operation": "configure" } }

{ "action": "session.sign-in", "outcome": "Failed",
  "detail": { "attempted_identifier_hmac": "u0Yq3J9xw2Rk1A8sT-pZ4g", "failure": "InvalidCredentials" } }
```

## The hashes: what each does, and what's hashed

- **`previous_hash`** = the `record_hash` of record `sequence_number − 1`. The first record uses 32 zero bytes.
- **`record_hash`** = SHA-256 over a canonical serialization of **the whole record, including `previous_hash`** and its parties. So **yes, the chain is preserved:** each hash depends on everything before it.

**The string that's hashed** uses the JSON Canonicalization Scheme (RFC 8785): sorted keys, fixed number formats, no whitespace. Timestamps are RFC 3339 in UTC with microseconds; parties are sorted by role, then account:

```json
{"action":"change-request.apply","actor_account_id":"iac_…","audit_record_id":"aud_…","case_id":"case_…",
 "change_request_id":"chg_…","detail":{…},"occurred_at":"2026-10-24T15:02:11.482113Z","outcome":"Succeeded",
 "parties":[{"account_id":"acc_jane","role":"Caller"},…],"policy_set_version":"1",
 "previous_hash":"9f2c…","reason_codes":[],"sequence_number":1042,"session_id":"…","subject_account_id":"acc_acme",
 "trace_id":"4bf9…"}
```

**Verifying (reconstructing) the chain:** walk the records by ascending `sequence_number`, recompute each hash, and check that it matches the stored one and that each `previous_hash` matches the record before. The first mismatch shows where tampering happened.

**Granularity:** **one chain for the whole audit log.** Sessions and requests aren't chains; they're filters (`session_id`, `trace_id`) over it. Partitioned chains (per account, or per day) are a production scaling concern.

## AT5: so we rely on the database for immutability?

Two layers, with different jobs:

| Layer | Job | Limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Database privileges** (`INSERT` and `SELECT` only, no `UPDATE` or `DELETE` for the application's role) | **Prevent** changes through the application | A database administrator or a stolen superuser can bypass them |
| **Hash chain** | **Detect** any change made by any means | Someone with full write access could recompute the *whole* chain |

The honest gap: what defeats a full recomputation is **anchoring**, meaning periodically publishing the latest hash outside our database (a separate store, or a timestamping service), so a recomputed chain no longer matches the published anchor. That's a production-readiness item. The demo has the two layers above.

## AT4: what guarantees atomicity?

- **PostgreSQL transactions.** The use case runs inside a **unit of work** (Fowler's Unit of Work pattern, behind a port), so the change and its audit record share one transaction: `BEGIN` … `COMMIT`, or roll back together. Hyperdrive supports transactions on a single connection.
- **The chain head:** a single-row table, `audit_chain_head` (last sequence number, last hash), locked with `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` inside that same transaction. That serializes audit writers, so there are no gaps and no forks. This is the serialization cost I mentioned.
- **Across systems** (a Workflow step plus the database), there's no shared transaction. Each step's database transaction is atomic, and steps are idempotent (keyed by the change request id).

## AT3: what wouldn't be audited

| Not audited | Why | Where it's visible instead |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The conversation's text and the LLM's prompts | memory, not evidence (SU-7) | agent storage |
| Requests rejected **before** authentication (a missing or expired token, `401`) | no identity to attribute; high volume | logs and traces |
| Reading the product catalog (plans, features) | no customer data involved | — |
| Static files, health checks | no decision | logs |
| **Direct database access** by operators, migrations, seeding | it bypasses the application | **a gap:** needs database-level auditing (production-readiness) |

## AT8: do we keep the attempted username?

**Not in the clear**, by design. A classic failure mode is people typing their **password into the username field**: logging attempted usernames in plain text ends up logging passwords. We keep a **keyed hash (HMAC) of the normalized identifier**. Repeated attempts against one identity are still detectable (same hash), but nothing readable is stored. The HMAC key is a server secret, so the hash can't be reversed by guessing emails without it.

## To rule

1. Keep **caller** in Support (actor = executor)?
2. **CM-14:** UUIDv7 generated in the application, stored as native `uuid`, shown as prefix + base64url; nothing relies on id order?
3. `sequence_number` plus an `audit_chain_head` row, for the chain?
4. The hash design: SHA-256, RFC 8785 canonical JSON over the full record including `previous_hash`, one global chain?
5. `outcome` values, `action` following the permission template plus session events, and `reason_codes` as a native array with a pattern check?
6. Failed sign-ins: HMAC of the identifier, never the plain value?

Recorded: AT1, AT6, AT7, AT9.

### Assistant

## A more generic name than "caller"

Fair point: "caller" assumes a phone. Two candidates:

| Name | Fit | Concern |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Requester** (the common spelling; "requestor" is a variant seen in legal and technical writing) | Channel-neutral | **Collides:** `change_request.requested_by` is the CSR, and "Requester" is already the CSR's part in attribution |
| **Contact** | Channel-neutral, and the standard support term for the customer person behind a case (phone, chat, email, web form) | none |

**My recommendation: contact.** `support_case.contact_account_id`, `change_request.contact_account_id`, and the participant role `Contact`. The verification columns become `contact_verified_at`, `contact_verification_method`, `contact_verified_by`.

## `audit_participant` or `audit_participation`?

**`audit_participant`**: one row per participant in a record, singular like our other tables. The role column becomes `participant_role`.

## Where the next sequence number lives

In a **separate, single-row table**, `audit_chain_head`. It holds the **last** sequence number used and the **last** record hash, global, for the one chain. Writing an audit record goes like this, inside the action's transaction:
1. Lock that row (`SELECT … FOR UPDATE`). Concurrent writers wait here.
2. Next number = last + 1. `previous_hash` = last hash.
3. Insert the record, then update the head to the new number and hash.
4. Commit together with the change.

**Why not a PostgreSQL `SEQUENCE`?** A sequence hands out numbers *outside* transactions: a rolled-back transaction leaves a gap, and gaps would look like deleted records in the chain. We need the last hash anyway, so one locked row serves both.

## `action_key`

Yes: **`action_key`**, a superset of permission keys (every permission key, plus session events such as `session.sign-in`). The `_key` suffix fits CM-12.

## A name for `outcome` that says it's a closed value

Our convention already marks closed enumerations by role words: `*_status`, `*_kind`, `*_reason`, `*_category`, `source` are all `CHECK` columns (data-model README: "enumerations are text columns with CHECK constraints"). Two ways:
- **(a) Keep the convention:** name it **`audit_outcome`**, and add "outcome" to the list of role words that mean "closed enumeration".
- **(b) A suffix for every enumeration:** `outcome_code`, and then also `account_status_code`, `hold_reason_code`, … everywhere. That's consistent, but it renames every enumeration column we have.

`_key` would be misleading: our keys name **reference-data rows** (a plan, a role) that other tables point to. Outcome values aren't rows anywhere. **My recommendation: (a), `audit_outcome`.**

## `reason_codes`: queries and portability

**Queries with a native array** work fine in PostgreSQL:
- `WHERE 'policy.x' = ANY(reason_codes)` finds records, using a GIN index.
- `unnest(reason_codes)` counts reasons across records.

**Your portability concern is fair.** Arrays are PostgreSQL-specific. A child table is plain standard SQL that every database supports, and it matches `audit_participant`:

```text
audit_reason
  audit_record_id → audit_record
  reason_code        policy.grant-beyond-14-days-needs-supervisor
  PRIMARY KEY (audit_record_id, reason_code)
```

**I'd switch to the child table.** It's portable, simple to join, and consistent. Reason codes are still part of the hashed record content.

## Is `detail` really "parameters"?

Not quite. `parameters` on a change request are the **inputs** of something requested. `detail` holds **what happened**, which can include inputs and also results: `"result": "NotFound"`, `"failure": "InvalidCredentials"`. Calling it "parameters" would hide the results, so I'd keep **`detail`**.

## The whole history is preserved?

Yes. Every audited event, across all accounts and sessions, in one chronological trail, never deleted. Accounts, sessions and traces are ways to *filter* it. How long it's kept is a production policy (retention), already listed.

## Anchoring, explained more simply

The chain only proves that **records are consistent with each other**. Picture someone with full write access to the database:
1. They alter record 500.
2. They recompute the hashes of records 500, 501, … up to the last one.
3. The chain verifies perfectly, because every link is consistent again.

Verification compared the chain only against *itself*, so nothing caught it. **Anchoring** means regularly copying the current head hash ("record 1,000 had hash `9f2c…`") to a place that attacker **can't** change: a separate system, write-once storage, or a timestamping service. Their recomputed chain now gives a different hash for record 1,000 than the copy outside, so the tampering shows. It's like a notary keeping a copy of a ledger's last page: you can rewrite your own book, but not the notary's copy.

## Requests rejected before authentication

Right: that isn't the audit's purpose. The audit is **attributable evidence**: decisions and actions by identified accounts. **Attack analysis** is **security monitoring**, which needs different data (IP addresses, user agents, rates, patterns) and different tools.

| Event | Audit record | Security event log |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Failed sign-in (it targets an identity) | ✓ (with the HMAC of the identifier) | ✓ |
| Missing or expired token, malformed requests, probing | — | ✓ (IP, user agent, path, rate; identifiers as HMACs) |

So we wouldn't lose attack data: it goes to security event logs (structured logs that a security event management tool consumes in production). I'd add that to production-readiness.

## Your rulings

**2 — UUIDv4 or UUIDv7?** **UUIDv7.**
- **Index performance:** new ids are time-ordered, so inserts land at the end of the index instead of scattered across it.
- **Unguessability:** v7 still has 74 random bits (v4 has 122). That's enough, because **our ids aren't secrets**: every access is authorized, and a case that isn't yours answers `404` regardless.
- **The trade-off:** a v7 id reveals its creation time, to the millisecond. I don't see a problem with that for our entities.

**3 — Next or last?** **The last** sequence number and hash. Next is computed under the lock.

**4 — Salting and iterations?** No. Salt and iterations exist to slow down guessing **low-entropy secrets** (passwords). The audit hash fingerprints **non-secret** data, and verification has to recompute millions of them, so it must be deterministic and fast.

There's one upgrade worth considering, though: **HMAC-SHA-256 instead of plain SHA-256**, keyed with a secret held outside the database (a Worker secret). Then someone with database access but without the key **can't recompute the chain at all**. That closes most of the gap anchoring addresses, at the cost of one more secret to manage. **I'd do it:** it's cheap, and it makes the tamper evidence real even before anchoring.

**5 —** `action_key` and `audit_outcome` (above). The array becomes `audit_reason` (above).

**6 —** Recorded: no sensitive data in plain text.

## To rule

1. **contact** instead of caller (`contact_account_id`, participant role `Contact`)?
2. `audit_participant` with `participant_role`?
3. `audit_outcome`, adding "outcome" to the enumeration role words?
4. `audit_reason` as a child table instead of an array?
5. **UUIDv7** (CM-14: generated in the application, native `uuid`, shown as prefix + base64url)?
6. **HMAC-SHA-256** for the chain, with the key outside the database?
7. Pre-authentication rejections go to security event logs (production-readiness)?

Recorded: `sequence_number` with `audit_chain_head` (last number and hash, global), `action_key`, `detail`, RFC 8785 canonical JSON, one global chain.

### Assistant

## Where the HMAC key is kept

- **As a Worker secret** (`wrangler secret put`). It's injected into the Worker at runtime, never stored in the database or the repository, and never logged. In production it would live in a secret manager with rotation (Cloudflare has a Secrets Store for this).
- **Each record names its key:** a **`hash_key_id`** column. When the key is rotated, new records use the new key, and old records still verify with the key they name. Without that column, rotating the key would make the whole history unverifiable.

**One trade-off:** verifying the chain needs the key, so whoever verifies must be trusted with it. The production alternative is **asymmetric signatures** (for example Ed25519): the application signs with a private key, and anyone, including an external auditor, verifies with the public key. I'd list that with anchoring in production-readiness.

**Several data centers:** the key isn't in the database, so database replication never copies it. Worker secrets are distributed by the platform to every location where the Worker runs. Any region with the key can verify any replica. A record carries only two 32-byte hashes, which is negligible for storage and replication.

## Optimistic locking instead of the row lock?

Optimistic locking would work: the sequence number itself can act as the version (`UPDATE audit_chain_head SET last = n + 1 WHERE last = n`). It's the wrong fit here, though:
- **With optimistic locking,** two concurrent writers both read `n`, both compute, and one fails. It must **retry its whole business transaction** (the change included), and under contention retries pile up.
- **With the lock** (`SELECT … FOR UPDATE`), the second writer simply **waits** a few milliseconds. The lock is taken at the **last** step, just before commit, so it's held very briefly.

**I'd keep the row lock.** There's a more scalable design we'll want in production, which also answers your next question.

## Across data centers: no, a single chain doesn't scale

A single global chain has one point every write must pass through. Across regions, that means cross-region lock latency on every audited action. The established answers, as used by certificate transparency logs:
- **Asynchronous chaining:** a transaction inserts its audit record *without* chain fields (still atomic with the change). A single chainer per partition then assigns sequence numbers and hashes in order, moments later. Business transactions never wait on the chain.
- **Partitioned chains:** one chain per region (or per writer).
- **A Merkle tree over the chains:** periodically, the heads of all chains are combined into one root hash, and that root is what gets anchored.

Our system of record is a single PostgreSQL database in one region (CM-2), so one synchronous chain is right for the demo. I'll put these three on the production-readiness list.

## "Snapshot"?

If you mean the anchoring step: I'd avoid "snapshot". It usually means a copy of the *data* (a database snapshot), and here we copy only a fingerprint. The established term, used by transparency logs, is **checkpoint**: a small, signed statement that "record *N* had hash *H* at time *T*". If we model it later, it would be a `audit_checkpoint` table, or an external record of the same shape.

## `participant_role` values

| Value | Who | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Contact` | the customer person who asked for the action | `acc_jane` |
| `Requester` | the employee who proposed the change | `iac_alex` |
| `Approver` | the employee who approved or denied it | `iac_sam` |

It's a closed set. The **actor** (who executed) and the **subject** (what was acted on) are columns on the record, not participants, since every record has exactly one of each at most. New values get added when a new kind of participation appears.

## Recorded

- **contact** replaces caller
- `audit_participant` with `participant_role`
- `audit_outcome`, with "outcome" added to the enumeration role words
- `audit_reason` as a child table
- `detail`
- **UUIDv7** (CM-14)
- the `audit_chain_head` row (last number and hash)
- RFC 8785 canonical JSON
- **HMAC-SHA-256**
- security event logs for pre-authentication rejections.

2. Keep the row lock (`FOR UPDATE` at the last step); asynchronous chaining, partitioned chains and Merkle anchoring go to production-readiness?
3. The term **checkpoint** for anchoring?
4. `participant_role`: `Contact`, `Requester`, `Approver`?

### Assistant

## How much slower is Ed25519 than HMAC?

Roughly **10–100 times slower per operation**, but still small per record. Typical figures on one CPU core (they vary by hardware and implementation):

| Operation | HMAC-SHA-256 (small message) | Ed25519 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Create | ~1 µs | sign ~20–50 µs |
| Verify | ~1 µs | ~50–150 µs |
| Verifying 10 million records | seconds | minutes |

For writing, it makes no practical difference: either is tiny next to the database round trip (milliseconds). The difference shows when **verifying a long history**. That's why systems that use signatures usually **sign the `audit_checkpoint`s rather than every record**: records are chained with HMAC or a plain hash, and checkpoints are signed so anyone can verify them with the public key. Web Crypto in Workers supports Ed25519, so it's an easy step later.

**Trusting the provider with the key:** yes. With Worker secrets, we trust the platform to distribute and protect the key. In production, the stronger option is a key that **never leaves a hardware security module** (a KMS): the application asks the KMS to compute the HMAC or signature, so the key itself is never transmitted at all.

### Assistant

## `audit_record` vs `audit_chain`

I'd keep **`audit_record`** and **`audit_chain_head`**. The names describe different things on purpose:

| Name | What it names | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `audit_record` | **one row = one record** | Our tables are named for what a single row is (`subscription`, `change_request`). Calling it `audit_chain` would name the whole collection, and "one row of `audit_chain`" reads oddly. |
| `audit_chain_head` | **the head of the chain**: the end the records form, where the next one attaches | The chain is the structure the records form; this row marks its current end. |
| `audit_record_head` | — | Would suggest the head *of a record*, and a record has no head. |

So the two names fit together: **records form a chain, and the chain has a head.** The concepts list in the model document already defines "audit chain" that way.

## `policy_set_version`

It's **the version of the policy set that made the decision**. The policy set is all the policies evaluated together, kept as versioned files in the repository (proposal §7.5).

**Why the record needs it:** policies change, and decisions must stay explainable afterwards:

```text
Oct 24   Alex grants 7 days of SSO      → Allow,  policy set v3 (Tier 1: grants up to 14 days)
Nov 02   policy set v4 deployed          (Tier 1: grants up to 5 days)
Nov 10   an auditor asks: "why was a 7-day grant allowed?"
         → the record says v3, and v3 allowed it. Without the version, it looks like a violation.
```

**Details:**
- **It's empty** for records that involve no policy evaluation, such as session events.
- **Where the value comes from** is a Policy-subsystem question: a version number in the policy files, or the content hash of the loaded set, which is tamper-evident on its own. We'll settle it in the next subsystem.

### Assistant

## Policy: opening proposal

This combines the Policy subsystem's storage with the **policy model** you scheduled next, since they're inseparable. It builds on what's ruled:
- **CM-4:** our own evaluator, behind a Policy Decision Point (PDP) port.
- **AC-1:** RBAC grants and policies constrain, so policies only ever **forbid** or **require approval**.

### 1. The authorization decision: one building block, in a fixed order

The PDP combines all three inputs (Access, Entitlement, Policy) and stops at the first that refuses:

| Step | Check | Refusal and reason code |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | The actor's account, and the subject account if any, aren't suspended (AC-6) | Deny · `account.<hold>` |
| 2 | The actor holds the permission for the action (Access) | Deny · `access.permission-not-held` |
| 3 | If there's a contact, the contact holds the customer permission in the subject account (SU-8) | Deny · `access.contact-permission-not-held` |
| 4 | For feature actions: the subject account is entitled (Entitlement) | Deny · `entitlement.<reason>` |
| 5 | **Policies:** any matching `forbid` | Deny · `policy.<policy_key>` |
| 6 | **Policies:** any matching `requireApproval` | RequiresApproval · `policy.<policy_key>` |
| 7 | Otherwise | Allow |

Deny-by-default already lives in step 2: no role, no permission. So **when no policy matches, the result is Allow.** Policies only subtract.

### 2. What a policy looks like

```yaml
policy_key: grant-beyond-14-days-needs-approval
effect: requireApproval
action_keys:
  - grant.create
condition: context.duration_days > 14 && context.duration_days <= 90
reason: Grants longer than 14 days, up to 90, need a supervisor's approval.
examples:
  - given:
      context:
        duration_days: 30
    expect: requireApproval
  - given:
      context:
        duration_days: 7
    expect: noMatch
```

- **`policy_key`** is kebab-case, and the reason code is `policy.<policy_key>`.
- **`action_keys`** says which actions the policy applies to (from AT-5's vocabulary).
- **`condition`** is a boolean expression over typed attributes (§3).
- **`examples`** are policy-as-code tests. Every policy carries its own, and loading **runs them and refuses a set where any example fails**.

**The starting policy set:**

| `policy_key` | Effect | Actions | Condition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `enterprise-accounts-need-level-2` | forbid | `account.read` | `resource.plan_key == 'enterprise' && principal.job_function == 'representative' && principal.job_level < 2` |
| `grant-beyond-14-days-needs-approval` | requireApproval | `grant.create` | `context.duration_days > 14 && context.duration_days <= 90` |
| `grant-beyond-90-days-forbidden` | forbid | `grant.create` | `context.duration_days > 90` |
| `courtesy-period-up-to-14-days` | forbid | `subscription.add-courtesy-period` | `context.duration_days > 14` |
| `downgrade-must-fit-seats` | forbid | `subscription.change-plan` | `resource.seats_in_use > context.target_seat_quota` |
| `contact-must-be-verified` | forbid | `subscription.change-plan`, `subscription.cancel`, `grant.create` | `context.has_contact && !context.contact_verified` |
| `no-self-approval` | forbid | `approval.decide` | `principal.account_id == context.requester_account_id` |

### 3. The condition language and its attributes

You asked earlier whether a policy could be a readable predicate string. That's now my recommendation:

- **CEL (Common Expression Language).** Google Cloud IAM Conditions and Kubernetes use it. Expressions read like the table above, are side-effect free and always terminate, and **are type-checked when loaded**, so a typo like `grandDurationDays` fails at deploy rather than evaluating silently to false.
- **It needs a spike first:** a CEL library running in Workers without `eval`. If none passes, the fallback is a small expression parser of our own with the same syntax subset (comparisons, `&&`, `||`, `!`, `in`), about a day's work.
- **Attributes form a closed, typed schema,** loaded by a Policy Information Point (PIP):

| Namespace | Attributes (first cut) |
| --- | --- |
| `principal` | `account_id`, `account_kind`, `identity_kind`, `department`, `job_function`, `job_level` |
| `resource` (the subject account) | `account_id`, `account_category`, `plan_key`, `plan_ladder_key`, `tier_rank`, `seats_in_use` |
| `context` (the request) | `action_key`, the change's typed parameters (`duration_days`, `target_plan_key`, `target_seat_quota`), `has_contact`, `contact_verified`, `requester_account_id` |
| `environment` | `now` |

An expression may use only declared attributes. Adding one is a schema change, with the PIP loading it.

### 4. Storage: authored as files, loaded as immutable rows

```text
policy_set                          one per distinct loaded set; never changed
  policy_set_version   the content hash (SHA-256 of the canonical set, base64url)
  source_revision      the git commit it came from
  loaded_at, loaded_by

policy                              the policies of one set
  policy_set_version, policy_key
  effect, condition, reason

policy_action                       which actions each policy applies to
  policy_set_version, policy_key, action_key

policy_set_activation               fact in time: which set was in force, when
  activation_id, policy_set_version
  activated_at, activated_by, ended_at, ended_by
```

- **Authoring stays in files** (reviewed in git, policy as code). **Each deployed set is loaded into the database once, unchanged forever** (CM-7).
- **`policy_set_version` is the content hash.** That answers your earlier question: it's tamper-evident by itself, and two identical sets get the same version.
- **`audit_chain_record.policy_set_version`** becomes a foreign key. An auditor can read the **exact text** of the policy that decided, years later, after the files have changed.
- **Exactly one active set** at any time (a unique index over activations not ended).

### Decisions for you

| # | Question | My recommendation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PO1 | Effects and combining: `forbid` and `requireApproval` only; forbid wins; no matching policy means Allow | Yes (follows AC-1) |
| PO2 | The fixed decision order in §1, as one building block | Yes |
| PO3 | The policy shape, including mandatory `examples` run at load | Yes |
| PO4 | CEL for conditions, after a spike; fallback to our own parser with the same syntax subset | Yes |
| PO5 | A closed, typed attribute schema (`principal`, `resource`, `context`, `environment`), loaded by the PIP | Yes |
| PO6 | Files as the source; loaded sets immutable in the database, versioned by content hash; one active set; the audit references it | Yes |

**For production-readiness:**
- a policy authoring and review workflow (who may change policy)
- staged rollout (shadow evaluation of a new set before activation)
- policy analytics (which policies fire, and how often)
- replacing the evaluator with Cedar behind the same port (CM-4).

Your thoughts on PO1–PO6?

### Assistant

I'll answer your questions first, then propose a revised policy shape. Several of your points lead to the same improvement: **writing policies in positive form.**

## 1. Chain of Responsibility?

Yes, that's the right pattern (GoF). Each check in the decision order is a handler: account status, actor permission, contact permission, entitlement, policies. Each one either refuses or passes the request on, and the end of the chain allows.

One adjustment to the classic pattern: classic Chain of Responsibility stops at the first handler that acts. We decided that **explanations report every failing reason** (spec decision D2). So each handler **adds its findings** (reason codes) and passes the request on, and the decision is the most restrictive finding. The chain can still stop early where nothing further would matter (a suspended account). Handlers stay independent and replaceable, and adding a check means adding a handler.

## The security concern: is it default deny?

Yes. **The system as a whole is default deny:** nothing is allowed unless a role explicitly grants the permission (Access, AC-1). The decision order puts that check *before* policies, so an action without a permission never reaches the policies at all.

Policies are a **second layer** that can only narrow what roles allow. That's defense in depth: allow-listing first, then extra conditions. So "no policy matched" doesn't mean "allowed by default". It means "allowed by the role, and no further condition applies".

Your instinct about **positive phrasing** is still right for readability: "a grant must be at most 14 days" is clearer than "forbid when more than 14 days". That leads to the revision below.

## The revised policy shape: positive requirements

```yaml
policy_key: grants-up-to-14-days
description: Grants longer than 14 days need a supervisor's approval.
governed_actions:
  - grant.create
applies_when: "true"
requires: context.duration_days <= 14
unmet_outcome: RequiresApproval
examples:
  - given:
      context:
        duration_days: 7
    when: grant.create
    then: Met
  - given:
      context:
        duration_days: 30
    when: grant.create
    then: RequiresApproval
```

| Field | Meaning | Answers your question |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `governed_actions` | the actions this policy **applies to** | Not "allowed actions": the policy doesn't allow anything (roles do). It governs these actions by adding a requirement. "Allowed actions" would suggest the opposite. |
| `applies_when` | a predicate that narrows when the policy applies (default `"true"`) | Keeps `requires` positive. Example: applies when the account is Enterprise; requires level 2. |
| `requires` | a predicate that **must hold**: positive phrasing | Your "predicate" naming, and your positive strategy. |
| `unmet_outcome` | what happens when the requirement doesn't hold: `Deny` or `RequiresApproval` | Replaces "effect". The values are a subset of `audit_outcome` (AT-5), so the vocabulary is shared. |
| `description` | human-readable text, shown in explanations | Your naming. |
| `examples` | `given` (attributes), `when` (the action), `then` (`Met`, `Deny` or `RequiresApproval`) | Yes, Given/When/Then, with the expected result under `then`. `Met` replaces "noMatch": the requirement held, so this policy contributes nothing. |

**On your naming questions:**
- **"Forbid" or "deny"?** "Deny" is more common (AWS IAM, XACML). Cedar uses "forbid". We use **`Deny`**, matching our audit outcome.
- **Is `requireApproval` a different kind of thing?** You're right that it is. In XACML terms it would be "permit, with an obligation to obtain approval". Making it a value of `unmet_outcome` keeps that clear: the requirement isn't met, and the consequence is approval rather than refusal. It's also exactly the audit outcome `RequiresApproval`.
- **"Result" instead of "effect"?** `unmet_outcome` says precisely *when* it applies (the requirement wasn't met), which neither "effect" nor "result" does.

**Combining:** evaluate every policy that governs the action and applies. Any unmet `Deny` gives Deny; otherwise any unmet `RequiresApproval` gives RequiresApproval; otherwise Allow (the role already allowed it). All unmet policies are reported as `policy.<policy_key>` reasons.

**The starting set, in positive form:**

| `policy_key` | Governs | Applies when | Requires | If unmet |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `enterprise-accounts-need-level-2` | `account.read` | `resource.plan_key == 'enterprise'` | `principal.job_level >= 2 \|\| principal.job_function == 'supervisor'` | Deny |
| `grants-up-to-14-days` | `grant.create` | always | `context.duration_days <= 14` | RequiresApproval |
| `grants-up-to-90-days` | `grant.create` | always | `context.duration_days <= 90` | Deny |
| `courtesy-up-to-14-days` | `subscription.add-courtesy-period` | always | `context.duration_days <= 14` | Deny |
| `downgrade-fits-seats` | `subscription.change-plan` | always | `resource.seats_in_use <= context.target_seat_quota` | Deny |
| `contact-is-verified` | `subscription.change-plan`, `subscription.cancel`, `grant.create` | `context.has_contact` | `context.contact_verified` | Deny |
| `no-self-approval` | `approval.decide` | always | `principal.account_id != context.requester_account_id` | Deny |

## Who sets the variables (PO5)

The **Policy Information Point (PIP)**, an outbound port of our own. The use case calls the PDP with the action and ids, and the PIP builds the attributes from trusted sources only:

| Namespace | Set by the PIP from |
| --- | --- |
| `principal` | the **session's account** (from the verified token) and its identity's job attributes |
| `resource` | the **subject account**: Account, Entitlement (plan, seats in use) |
| `context` | the **validated change request** (typed parameters, SU-3), the case (contact, verification), the approval (requester) |
| `environment` | the `Clock` port |

**The LLM never supplies attributes.** Tool arguments become change-request parameters only after strict validation. Everything else is loaded from the database, never taken from text.

## Running examples at load, or just tests? (PO3)

**Tests are the primary gate:** CI runs every policy's examples on each change. Running them again when loading guards one case: a policy set deployed separately from code. If policy files always ship through CI with the code, the load-time run is redundant. **My recommendation: tests in CI only, plus a type check when loading** (every attribute exists, every action key is known). That matches your instinct.

## Files, the database, and how each PDP gets the policies (§4, PO6)

- **Files:** YAML (block style), in `system/policies/`, under version control. For hashing, the loader converts the set to canonical JSON (RFC 8785).
- **How each node gets them:** Workers run as many short-lived isolates, so loading policies from the database on every decision would be slow. **My recommendation:** **bundle the policy set into the Worker at build time.** Every isolate has it in memory from the start, and no decision reads policies from the database.
- **The database's role:**
  - the immutable record of every set (its exact text, for auditors)
  - which set is active (`policy_set_activation`)
  - the foreign key from audit records.

  A deployment loads the bundled set (if new) and activates it.
- **A fail-closed check:** at startup, a Worker compares its bundled set's hash with the active one. If they differ, it refuses authorization decisions until they match. A stale Worker never decides with outdated policy.

Changing policy means deploying, which is what policy as code means anyway.

3. Combining: any unmet Deny gives Deny, then any unmet RequiresApproval gives RequiresApproval, then Allow; default deny stays in Access?
4. Examples as CI tests, plus a type check at load?
5. Policies bundled into the Worker at build time; the database keeps the immutable sets and the activation; Workers refuse decisions on a hash mismatch?

Recorded: PO4 (CEL, after a spike), and the PIP as the only source of attributes.

## Generalizing `RequiresApproval`: conditional outcomes with obligations

Good catch. Approval is one example of a broader idea: **the action may proceed, once something else happens.** Other future examples:
- step-up authentication (re-enter a credential)
- a second approval
- the customer's consent
- a waiting period.

Access-control standards call these **obligations** (XACML's term). So:

```yaml
unmet_outcome: Conditional
obligations:
  - obligation_kind: Approval
    fulfilled_by_permission: approval.decide
```

| | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `unmet_outcome` | `Deny` or `Conditional` |
| `obligations` | what must happen for a `Conditional` to proceed: one or more, **all** required |
| `obligation_kind` | a closed set: `Approval` today; `StepUpAuthentication`, `CustomerConsent` later |

**Your "predicate evaluated elsewhere" insight fits exactly.** An obligation is *how the requirement becomes true later*. So the requirement itself says what fulfilling it means:

```yaml
requires: context.duration_days <= 14 || context.approval_granted
```

- **At proposal time,** `approval_granted` is false. The requirement is unmet, so the outcome is Conditional and an approval is started.
- **When the approved change is applied,** the PDP re-evaluates (CM-9). The PIP now sets `approval_granted` to true from the decided `change_approval`, so the requirement **holds**, and the change is allowed.

There's no special case in the engine: an obligation is fulfilled, an attribute changes, and the same predicate passes.

**This ripples into AT-5:** the audit outcome `RequiresApproval` becomes **`Conditional`**, with the obligations recorded in `detail`. I'd amend AT-5. The change request's status stays `AwaitingApproval`: that's the specific obligation it's waiting on.

**`Met` → `true` in examples:** agreed. An example asserts **whether the policy is satisfied**: `then: true` (satisfied, or not applicable) or `then: false` (unmet, so its `unmet_outcome` applies). That tests the predicates directly.

## Actions and permissions in the glossary

Proposed entries:

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| **Action** | Something that can be attempted, named by an **action key** (`<resource>.<operation>`, or `feature.<feature_key>.<operation>`). Every audit record names one. |
| **Permission** | The right to perform one action, held through roles or assignments. **A permission's key is the key of the action it permits.** |
| **The relationship** | Every permission names an action. Not every action has a permission: session events (`session.sign-in`) are attempted by anyone and governed by authentication, not by roles. Authorizing an action means checking the permission with the same key. |

I'll add these to the glossary with the write-up.

## Testing (your PO3 and 4)

Agreed: the policy file isn't the place for tests. Where they go:

| Level | What it tests | Where |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Functional** (per subsystem) | each policy behaves as intended, given attributes | `system/test/functional/policy/`: data-driven Given/When/Then cases, one file per policy, run by Vitest |
| **End-to-end** | the whole path: browser → Worker → agent → database | `system/test/e2e/`: through the real UI and API, with the language model scripted |

Both levels go into the tester guide (`AI_AS_TESTER.md`), alongside the existing unit and verification levels.

**What the type check at load means:** three checks, run when the bundled policy set is loaded:
1. **Structure:** the file matches the policy schema (required fields, allowed values).
2. **Expression types:** every attribute used in `applies_when` and `requires` exists in the attribute schema, and comparisons make sense (no comparing a number with text). This is what CEL's type checker does.
3. **References:** every governed action key exists, and every obligation kind is known.

## The database: purpose, immutability, integrity

**Why policies are in the database at all, if files are the source:**
1. **Evidence:** an auditor reading a decision from two years ago gets **the exact policy text that decided it**, through the foreign key from the audit record. Files and git history aren't available to the running system or to auditors.
2. **Activation:** a record of which set was in force, when, and who activated it.
3. **The integrity reference** that Workers check at startup.

**Enforcing immutability:** the same as the audit. The application's database role has `INSERT` and `SELECT` only on the policy tables (CM-13).

**Integrity, as you described:** at startup, the Worker computes the hash of its bundled set and compares it with the set active in the database. If they differ, or the database can't be reached, it **refuses to make authorization decisions**: fail closed.

**Keyless hash or HMAC?** **Keyless SHA-256.** The version must be **reproducible by anyone** from the policy text: an auditor can hash the file and match it to the database. Tampering is covered differently than for the audit: the rows can't be changed (privileges), and `policy_set_version` *is* the hash of the content, so altered rows no longer match their own version.

## Your rulings

**1 — The structure of a finding.** Each handler in the chain adds findings:

```ts
type Finding = {
  readonly source: "account" | "access" | "entitlement" | "policy";
  readonly reasonCode: ReasonCode;                   // namespaced: "policy.grants-up-to-14-days"
  readonly outcome: "Allow" | "Deny" | "Conditional";
  readonly obligations: readonly Obligation[];       // only when Conditional
  readonly facts: Readonly<Record<string, AttributeValue>>;  // what an explanation cites
};

type Decision = {
  readonly outcome: "Allow" | "Deny" | "Conditional";   // the most restrictive finding
  readonly findings: readonly Finding[];
  readonly obligations: readonly Obligation[];          // all obligations, combined
  readonly policySetVersion: PolicySetVersion;
};
```

`Allow` findings exist too. For example, Access records which role granted the permission, which is what "explain why Jane *can* configure SSO" cites.

**3 — "Default deny stays in Access":** yes, in effect. The Access handler returns **Deny unless it finds a role or assignment granting the permission**. Nothing is allowed by omission: the allow comes only from an explicit grant. The policy handler can only add Deny or Conditional on top. So "default deny" is how the Access handler is built, not a setting someone could turn off.

**4 — How examples become tests:** the cases live in **test files**, not in the policy YAML. Each `system/test/functional/policy/<policy_key>.cases.yaml` holds Given/When/Then cases, and one Vitest suite loads every case file and runs each case against the real policy set and evaluator. Adding a policy means adding its case file. The test reviewer checks that every policy has one.

**5 — How new policies take effect:** **a deployment.** The set is bundled into the Worker. Workers have no restarts: a deployment replaces the code, and new isolates start with the new set. One detail matters during the rollout, because old and new versions run side by side for a short while:
1. **Load and activate** the new set (now two sets are active).
2. **Roll out** the Worker.
3. **End** the old set's activation once the rollout completes.

So "exactly one active set" becomes **"at most two, only during a rollout"**. Workers re-check their set every minute, so once the old set is ended, any lingering old isolate stops deciding. Each audit record names the set that actually decided.

5. Keyless SHA-256 for `policy_set_version`; insert-only privileges; Workers refuse decisions on a mismatch?
6. Rollout: at most two active sets during a deployment; Workers re-check every minute?
