## The project structure

I understand it as **subsystems as modules, each hexagonal inside**, plus shared ports and adapters, and one application that assembles everything. That's a modular monolith built from bounded contexts, and it's better than the layer-first layout in today's `AI_AS_ARCHITECT.md` (`domain/`, `application/`, `adapters/`): a subsystem's model, use cases and infrastructure stay together, and its boundary is visible. **I'd adopt it.**

How I read each part, with suggestions:

```text
entitlement-agent/
  tech-docs/
  typescript/
    system/
      port/                    shared ports: Clock, IdGenerator, UnitOfWork, LanguageModel, …
      adapter/                 shared adapters: PostgreSQL connection and unit of work, Workers AI, JWT, clock
      external/                ? (see question 1)
      subsystem/
        customer-identity/  internal-identity/  account/  access/  product/
        entitlement/  support/  attribution/  policy/
        authorization/         ← suggested: the decision chain itself (no tables)
          src/
            model/             pure rules, no I/O
            usecase/           use cases, plus the ports this subsystem needs
            infra/             this subsystem's adapters (its PostgreSQL repositories)
          tests/               unit and functional
      application/
        entitlement-agent/
          src/                 bootstrap (composition root), Worker entry, case agent, tools, HTTP endpoints, UI
          tests/               end-to-end, plus the slice verification tests
          policies/            ← suggested: the policy set (YAML) this application runs
```

**Suggestions and questions:**
1. **What goes in `external/`?** My guess is wrappers around third-party SDKs (the Agents SDK, Workflows), kept out of `adapter/`. If so, how is it different from `adapter/`? Please tell me what you have in mind.
2. **An `authorization` subsystem.** The decision chain (Chain of Responsibility) combines Account, Access, Entitlement and Policy. It doesn't belong *inside* any one of them. Since "authorization" is the decision's name (that's why we renamed Access), a small subsystem with no tables is its natural home. `policy` then owns only policy sets and their evaluation.
3. **Rules between subsystems:**
   - A subsystem uses another **only through that subsystem's public use cases or ports**: never its model internals, and **never its tables**. Each subsystem owns its tables and migrations.
   - Inside a subsystem: `model` ← `usecase` ← `infra`.
   - dependency-cruiser enforces both rules in the GREEN gate.
4. **Where the policy files live:** the policy *engine* is the `policy` subsystem, but *the policies themselves* are this application's configuration. So I'd put them under `application/entitlement-agent/policies/`.
5. **Slice verification tests** cross subsystems, so they sit with the application's end-to-end tests.

Once we agree, I'll rewrite `AI_AS_ARCHITECT.md` around this structure.

## The policy hash encoding

**base64url without padding.** A 32-byte SHA-256 becomes 43 characters. That matches how ids are shown (CM-14). I'd extend CM-14 with an amendment so the same rule covers hashes: binary values are stored as bytes and shown as unpadded base64url.

## Examples in policy files, like OpenAPI?

A useful comparison. In OpenAPI, examples are **documentation**: they show readers what a value looks like. The risk with documentation examples is that **they rot** when nothing runs them. So a hybrid:
- **The policy file may carry a few illustrative examples:** documentation for a reader of the policy.
- **The functional suite runs them too,** so they can't become wrong.
- **The exhaustive cases** (boundaries, every applicability combination) live in the test case files, as you approved.

The examples are part of the file, so editing one changes the set's hash and version. That's harmless: an honest new version.

**My recommendation: the hybrid.** Optional `examples` in policy files, executed. If you'd rather keep policy files strictly declarative, examples live only in tests.

## Re-checking the active set: polling, or a signal?

A stateless Worker isolate can't receive a push. There's no channel to call it on, and it has no background timer either. The options:

| Option | How | Fit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Lazy re-check** | When a decision is requested and the last check is more than 60 seconds old, read the active version (one small query). Otherwise use the cached result. | Simple. No timer; an idle isolate costs nothing. |
| A Durable Object as the signal | One object holds the active version; isolates ask it instead of PostgreSQL | Faster reads, one more component |
| KV | The active version in Workers KV | Eventually consistent (around a minute), so no better than a lazy re-check |

There's also a reason it rarely matters: **the deployment itself is the signal.** When a rollout completes, the platform retires the old version's isolates. After that, no old code is deciding, and the re-check is just a safety net for stragglers. **My recommendation: the lazy re-check**, against PostgreSQL, at most once a minute per isolate.

3. The actions and permissions glossary entries.
4. `Finding` and `Decision` as drafted.
5. Keyless SHA-256, insert-only privileges, fail closed on a mismatch.
6. At most two active sets during a rollout.

### Assistant

On the three points:

**1 — No `policy_set_id` or `policy_id`.** That's deliberate. A policy set is identified by its **content hash** (`policy_set_version`), the way a git commit is identified by its hash. A policy is identified by its set plus its key. Natural keys fit here for three reasons:
- **The identity *is* the content.** The same text always gets the same version, and an auditor can recompute it from the file. A surrogate id would be a second identity that says nothing.
- **Sets never change** (insert-only), so the usual reason for surrogate keys (natural keys that change) doesn't apply.
- **CM-14 covers entities with a lifecycle.** A policy set is immutable content. Its lifecycle is the **activation**, and `policy_set_activation` does get a UUIDv7 (`psa_…`).

If you'd prefer every table to have a UUID for uniformity, I'd add `policy_set_id` and `policy_id` and keep the version as a unique column. But that's two identities for one thing, so I'd keep the natural keys.

**2 — "Uploaded" or "loaded"?** Neither is ideal. "Uploaded" suggests a person sending a file from a client to a server, and nobody does that: the **deployment** inserts the bundled set. "Loaded" is vague (into memory? into the database?). What actually happens is that a set becomes **known** to the system of record. I'd suggest **`registered_at` / `registered_by`**: "the deployment registered policy set `9f2c…`". It reads clearly next to `activated_at` (registered first, then activated).

**3 — `scm_revision`:** agreed. SCM (source control management) is the established term and isn't tied to git, while "svc" is easily read as "service". Renaming it now:

## Agent report — 2026-10-25T01:42:51.417Z

**Report from an agent of this session:**


  # Design review of the Entitlement Agent (read-only; no files changed)
  
  I read the proposal, every data-model doc except the draft, all ADRs, production-readiness, AI_AS_ARCHITECT, the constitution §4, the Slice 1 verification spec and the ER diagrams (for columns and enums). Paths below are relative to `entitlement-agent/tech-docs/`. 35 items, most important first in each section.
  
  ## Must fix for the demo
  
  1. **Most Slice 2–3 steps have no action key, so they cannot be audited or authorized.**
     - `attribution.md` §2 and AT-5 say action keys are "every permission key, plus session events". The permission list in `access.md` §2 has only `case.open`.
     - Steps that need a key but have none: closing a case (S15), setting the case subject or contact, proposing, confirming and canceling a change request, and approval timeout (S26).
     - Contradiction: `change-request.apply` is held only by System, yet `support.md` §3 says the CSR is `applied_by` on a direct `Allow`.
     - Fix: add an action-key table per lifecycle step. Suggested additions: `case.close`, `case.identify-subject`, `case.set-contact`, `change-request.confirm`, `change-request.cancel`, `approval.expire` (System). Either let the CSR's apply record use the governing permission (`subscription.change-plan`, `Succeeded`), or allow action keys that are not permissions (system and lifecycle events), as `session.*` already are.
  
  2. **Re-evaluating an approved change is undefined.**
     - `support.md` §3 step 3 says "the principal is re-authorized with its current role". After approval the executor is `sys_approval_process`, which holds `change-request.apply`, not `grant.create`. `grants-up-to-14-days` governs `grant.create`, so evaluating the system principal never reaches it.
     - The `subject_account_version` check will often fail after up to 72 h: any other change, or the grant-expiry sweep if it bumps `version`, makes an approved change stale.
     - No status exists for "approved but no longer valid".
     - Fix: define the apply-after-approval decision as two checks.
       - Requester check: the original action, for the requester's *current* status and roles, with `context.approval_granted = true`.
       - System check: the system principal holds `change-request.apply`.
     - For the approval path, recompute the diff against current state instead of comparing versions. On failure, set `Refused` with reason codes (or add a status), and notify the case.
  
  3. **`contact-is-verified` makes the S16 contact row impossible.**
     - The policy is in the starting set (`policy.md` §5) and applies whenever `context.has_contact`. Contact verification is stretch S27, and its columns are "built with the contact-verification scenario" (`support.md` §2).
     - So every customer-requested change is denied with `policy.contact-is-verified` in addition to `access.contact-permission-not-held`. S16 expects only the latter.
     - Fix: leave this policy out of the set until S27, or build minimal verification (one method, `contact_verified_at`) in Slice 2.
  
  4. **Access periods do not fit plan changes.**
     - A `FreePlan` period is "usually open-ended" and "never ended early" (`entitlement.md` §1–2, EN-3).
     - Upgrade free → pro (S14, likely on Acme Labs or Initech): the open-ended `FreePlan` period keeps covering Pro forever, with no payment.
     - Downgrade to free: nothing adds a `FreePlan` period, so after the `Payment` period ends nothing works.
     - Fix: a period covers only plans compatible with its source (`FreePlan` covers only a free plan). Alternatively, the free plan needs no period, and `ChangePlan` to free adds an open-ended `FreePlan` period. Document upgrades within a `Payment` period as "billing out of scope" (item 15).
  
  5. **The optimistic concurrency check can race, and nobody owns the `version` bump.**
     - `support.md` §3 checks "current `version` equals `subject_account_version`" and later writes. Two confirmations of different requests can both pass.
     - Fix: `UPDATE account SET version = version + 1 WHERE account_id = $1 AND version = $2` as the first statement, or `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`.
     - `account.md` §2 says `version` "changes on every write that affects the account", but those writes happen in Entitlement. Decide who bumps it, and whether the grant-expiry sweep and the hold use case do (see item 34).
  
  6. **Supervisors have no way to see or decide approvals (S24, S25).**
     - There is no use case, endpoint or tool for a pending-approval inbox or for deciding, and no permission to read pending approvals. Approvals do not belong to the supervisor's case, so the case agent is the wrong place.
     - Fix: `ListPendingApprovals` (`approval.decide` holders) and `DecideApproval`, as HTTP endpoints plus a small UI panel. Keep them outside the LLM, like confirmation.
     - Also decide what closing a case does to an `AwaitingApproval` request. Today only `AwaitingConfirmation` is canceled (`support.md` §7). Suggest: cancel the approval and its process instance in the same transaction, and let the CSR withdraw a pending request.
  
  7. **Nothing defines how a case gets its subject and contact.**
     - `support.md` says the subject is "set once when identified", and `change_request` requires it. Nothing says how it is set, by whom, or with which audit record. S16's contact row needs a contact too.
     - If the LLM picks the subject from `findCustomerAccount`, an injected display name could steer it.
     - Fix: a use case `IdentifyCaseSubject` (authorized as `account.read` on the target, audited). The UI shows the subject and asks for confirmation, or at minimum displays it on every change-request card. Add `SetCaseContact` the same way.
  
  8. **The Enterprise read policy is bypassed through history and changes.**
     - `enterprise-accounts-need-level-2` governs only `account.read`. S20 history uses `audit.read`, so Alex (level 1) can read an Enterprise account's history. Proposing a change also needs a read (the diff) but is a different action.
     - Fix: govern `audit.read` and every change action as well, or state that every use case touching a subject also asks for `account.read` (the D3 rule from the Slice 1 spec, applied in all slices).
  
  9. **Absent attribute values are not defined for CEL.**
     - `downgrade-fits-seats` compares `resource.seats_in_use <= context.target_seat_quota`, but Enterprise seats are unlimited (null).
     - `resource.plan_key == 'enterprise'` has no plan to compare for Stark (canceled), accounts without a subscription, and `OrganizationMember` accounts.
     - PO-4 promises a type-checked closed schema, yet these values are undefined.
     - Fix: mark attributes optional in the schema, use `has()` or explicit defaults (unlimited as `target_seat_quota_unlimited: bool`), and add policy cases for them. Also define `target_seat_quota` as the target plan plus the add-ons that remain plus grants, which is what EN-8 and EN-4 imply.
  
  10. **Reads use the token's `roles` claim, which can stay stale for hours.**
      - CM-9 and Slice 1 D1 have reads use `roles` from the token. Renewal "keeps `auth_time`" (S04), but no rule says renewal re-derives roles or re-checks holds and identity status. A suspended or demoted employee could keep renewing reads for 8 h.
      - `principal.job_level` comes from the database (`policy.md` §4), so roles and attributes mix freshness.
      - Internal permission assignments are not roles, so they are invisible to token-based reads.
      - The performance argument no longer holds: every read already opens a transaction and takes the audit chain lock (AT-3, AT-4).
      - Fix: always load the principal from the database and keep `roles` in the token for display only. At minimum, make renewal re-derive everything and refuse renewal for suspended accounts.
  
  11. **The WebSocket and Agents SDK surface needs explicit rules.**
      - Agents SDK state can be synced to and written by clients (client `setState`, callable methods). The principal, case id and subject must never live in client-writable state. Reject client state updates (`validateStateChange` or equivalent) and expose no callable that changes state.
      - "A connection never outlives its token" (CM-9) needs a server-side close at `exp`.
      - On reassignment, the old assignee's open connection must be closed, or the assignment checked on each message.
  
  12. **Audits of refused or stale attempts get lost in the rollback.**
      - AT-3 says "commits with the action, or not at all". A confirmation refused as stale, Deny or a failed `test` rolls back, and its `Failed`/`Deny` record goes with it.
      - A stale request has no defined status or reason code: S16 says "re-proposed", but the old request stays `AwaitingConfirmation` forever.
      - Fix: roll back, then write the refusal record in a separate transaction. Move the stale request to `Canceled`, or add `Superseded`, with reason code `support.stale-proposal` (new namespace) or `entitlement.changed-since-proposal`.
  
  13. **The confirmation channel is not specified.**
      - The `state-change-needs-confirmation` guard is "owed" (constitution §4). Confirmation must be a client action the LLM cannot trigger, for example `POST /api/change-requests/:id/confirm` or a typed WebSocket frame that bypasses the model. The card must render the stored `entitlement_diff`, never model text.
      - Fix: specify this in the Slice 2 spec and make S18 its guard.
  
  ## Should fix / decide
  
  14. **Add a reason-code register.** Codes are scattered and several are missing:
      - stale proposal;
      - a grant that adds nothing (EN-4);
      - a grant on an account with no subscription or covering period;
      - `account.internal-identity-inactive` (missing from `entitlement.md` §3 step 1);
      - `session.*` values.
  
      Also, `account.no-active-membership` (Dan as principal) and `account.operational-suspension` (Umbrella as subject) do not say *whose* account: carry `facts.account_id` and the role (actor or subject).
  
  15. **CSR-initiated upgrades are free and unchecked.** Any level-1 CSR can upgrade any account to Enterprise with no contact and no approval. Only the seat check applies, and a `Payment` or `FreePlan` period then covers it. Decide on a policy: plan changes require `context.has_contact`, or upgrades above a tier rank need approval. Interviewers will probe this.
  
  16. **The EN-8 add-on rules are "implemented only if a scenario needs it", but the seed makes them necessary.** Acme has the `advanced-analytics` add-on on Business, so any upgrade of Acme to Enterprise (`IncludedByPlan`) or downgrade (`NotEligible`) needs them. Either pick S14 and S16 test accounts without add-ons, or build EN-8.
  
  17. **The rule register has inconsistencies** (`AI_CONSTITUTION.md` §4).
      - `deny-by-default` says "a request no policy permits is denied". That contradicts AC-1, PO-1 and AU-3 (policies never permit; the access handler denies by default). Its guard should be access-handler and PDP tests.
      - CM-5, the guiding principle, is still `Proposed`.
      - Rules worth adding: `audit-fails-closed`, `untrusted-text-is-wrapped`, `one-algorithm-one-building-block`.
  
  18. **Turn off Hyperdrive query caching.** Hyperdrive caches read queries by default. Holds, status, `version` and assignments must be fresh for decisions. Disable caching in the Hyperdrive configuration, or guarantee every decision read runs inside a transaction.
  
  19. **`change_request` is missing columns** (`support.er.puml`).
      - There is no `confirmed_by`, and no `ended_at`/`ended_by` for `Canceled`, `Refused` or `Expired`.
      - There is no snapshot of the deciding `policy_set_version` and reason codes, so a refusal cannot be explained without the audit.
      - `audit_chain_record.policy_set_version` is `text`, but the CM-14 amendment says hashes are stored as bytes.
  
  20. **`audit_participant` is "never the actor or the subject", so the contact disappears when the contact is the subject** (Jane asking about `acc_jane`). Allow `Contact` to equal the subject, or record it in `detail`.
  
  21. **One approval satisfies every obligation.** `approval_granted` is a single boolean, so one approval fulfils all `Conditional` obligations even if they name different `fulfilled_by_permission` values (PO-3). Add the invariant that the approver must hold every named permission, or key `approval_granted` by obligation.
  
  22. **`OrganizationMember` accounts reach a dead end.** The category is immutable and each customer has one account (AN-2, CM-6). An individual who leaves the organization is suspended forever and can never subscribe personally. The seed also gives these accounts the owner role on themselves, including `subscription.request-*`, on an account that cannot subscribe. Decide now, or put it in production-readiness explicitly.
  
  23. **Sign-in has a timing oracle and a CPU risk.**
      - S02 needs identical responses, but skipping PBKDF2 for unknown emails reveals which emails exist. Verify against a dummy PHC hash.
      - Check that PBKDF2 at 100k iterations fits Workers CPU limits (OWASP now recommends 600k; note it).
  
  24. **The UI needs output safety.**
      - Customer display names and LLM output are rendered in the chat. Render them escaped, use a strict Content Security Policy, and allow no remote images or auto-loaded links. A markdown image URL is a classic way for an injected prompt to exfiltrate data.
      - `proposal.md` §6 covers what the LLM *says*, not what the browser *does*.
  
  25. **Grant timing semantics are undefined.**
      - When an approval is involved, does a grant start at `applied_at`? Say so.
      - A grant lasting past the end of the covering period stops at the period's end (EN-4). The diff and explanation should say so.
  
  ## Production notes to add
  
  26. **The audit chain head lock is global and taken on every read.** Every chat tool call becomes a write transaction that serializes on `audit_chain_head` plus an HMAC. Asynchronous chaining is listed, but add the read-path cost explicitly: throughput limit, lock wait under load, and read-audit volume (sampling is not allowed, so consider batching or summarizing read records).
  
  27. **Session key management**: ES256 key rotation (`kid`), where the private key lives, and that sign-out leaves the token valid for up to 30 minutes, including open WebSockets.
  
  28. **LLM-written text stored as data**: the case summary (SU-7) and the persisted conversation are untrusted text that may reach future prompts or UIs. They also hold personal data in the agent's SQLite (retention is listed; injection persistence is not).
  
  29. **Approval operations**: the seed has one supervisor, so Sam's own over-limit requests can never be approved. Workflow-to-agent notification needs retries; there is no escalation or queue for supervisors.
  
  30. **Insider enumeration**: `FindCustomerAccount` `Ambiguous` candidates reveal that Enterprise accounts exist to level-1 CSRs (for example, searching by `pat@umbrella.com`). Bulk-lookup detection and rate limits are missing.
  
  31. **Migration ordering**: per-subsystem migrations with cross-subsystem foreign keys need a global order (or deferrable constraints). Seeding bypasses use cases and audit (database-level auditing is partly listed).
  
  ## Modularity assessment
  
  32. **The schema has cycles between subsystems.**
      - `entitlement_grant.change_request_id` has a foreign key to Support, while Support depends on Entitlement for diffs and applying.
      - `audit_chain_record` has foreign keys to `support_case`, `change_request` and `policy_set`, while every subsystem writes audit records.
      - Customer Identity's `*_by` columns reference `account`, while `customer_account` references `customer`.
      - Fix:
        - Declare a layer order: identities → account → access/product → entitlement → policy → authorization → support, with attribution as a sink.
        - Allow foreign keys only downward. `*_by` → `account` is the single sanctioned exception, because Account is the kernel.
        - Make audit's case, change-request and policy-set ids soft references, with no foreign key; audit must accept records regardless.
        - Move the grant↔request link into Support (the request records the grant it created) or keep it as an opaque id.
  
  33. **The Policy Information Point has no home, which invites a Policy → Support cycle.** The PIP needs Account, Identity, Entitlement (seats, plan) and Support (`approval_granted`, `contact_verified`, `requester_account_id`). Put it in `authorization`: it loads `principal` and `resource`, and the calling use case passes a typed, validated `context`. Then `policy` stays a pure evaluator with its set registry. Placing the decision chain in `authorization` is otherwise right.
  
  34. **Applying a change crosses four subsystems in one transaction.** Support (status), Entitlement (writes), Account (`version`) and Attribution (record) must commit together. Define a transaction-scoped `UnitOfWork` that subsystem use cases join, and mark which public use cases are "transaction participants". Consider moving the concurrency token to Entitlement (for example, `subscription.version` or an entitlement revision). Entitlement writes would then not have to update Account, and the hold use case would bump it only where it matters.
  
  35. **Policy test cases depend outward, and the split's cost should be acknowledged.**
      - `policy.md` §7 puts case files under `system/subsystem/policy/tests/…`, but the policies live in `application/entitlement-agent/resource/policies/`, so a subsystem's tests depend on the application. Move the cases next to the policies, or into the application's tests; the policy subsystem keeps evaluator unit tests on synthetic policies.
      - The 10-subsystem split is conceptually sound: clear table ownership, Account as the kernel, Authorization with no tables. For 2–4 days it is heavy: ten migration sets, ports and conformance suites. Keep it, but scaffold thin `infra` layers and build only the use cases the slices need.
  
  ## Overall assessment
  
  The design is coherent and unusually explicit: a single decision chain, policies in positive form, facts in time, a keyed audit chain, and a clear rule that the LLM proposes and the policy engine decides.
  
  Slice 1 is close to buildable. Slices 2–3 still need a few lifecycle definitions: action keys, approval re-evaluation, stale handling, the approval inbox, and how a case gets its subject and contact.
  
  Three real contradictions need rulings before Slice 2: `contact-is-verified` against the stretch S27, open-ended `FreePlan` periods against plan changes, and the Enterprise policy not covering `audit.read` and changes.
  
  The modules are well chosen. Remove the schema cycles (audit and grant foreign keys) and place the Policy Information Point in `authorization`.
  
  The biggest security points are reading the principal fresh (or re-checking it on renewal), keeping the Agents SDK state and confirmation channel away from the client and the LLM, safe rendering in the UI, and disabling Hyperdrive caching.

## Overall verdict

- **The Slice 1 spec holds up:** every expected result was recomputed from the fixture and the rules, and none is wrong. It has gaps, mostly missing audit expectations and a few undefined rules.
- **The design is coherent,** and the reviewers agree the subsystem split is sound.
- **What needs work:**
  - lifecycle steps for Slices 2–3 that were never specified
  - three real contradictions
  - some industry terms we stretched

## A. Decisions for you (design), with my recommendation

| # | Issue | Recommendation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| R1 | **Approving a change has no defined re-check.** After approval, the actor is `sys_approval_process`, which never reaches the `grant.create` policies. Also, the version check will often fail after up to 72 hours. | Applying an approved change runs **two checks**: (a) the requester's original action, with their *current* roles and `approval_granted = true`; (b) the system account holds `change-request.apply`. On the approval path, **recompute the diff** instead of comparing versions. If either check fails, the change becomes `Refused`, with reason codes, and the case is notified. |
| R2 | **`contact-is-verified` would block every customer-requested change,** because verification is only a stretch item (S27). | Leave that policy out of the starting set until S27 is built. |
| R3 | **An open-ended `FreePlan` period keeps covering after an upgrade to Pro,** and a downgrade to Free has no covering period. | A period covers only a compatible plan: `FreePlan` covers only a free plan. A change to a free plan adds a `FreePlan` period. For the demo, an upgrade's change request **includes the access period it adds** (`Trial` or `Courtesy`), since billing is out of scope. |
| R4 | **The Enterprise read policy is bypassed** through history (`audit.read`) and changes. | Make it a rule across all slices: **every use case that touches a subject account first asks for `account.read`**. One gate, no per-action duplication. |
| R5 | **Lifecycle steps have no action key,** so they can't be authorized or audited: closing a case, setting its subject or contact, confirming, canceling, approval timeout. | Allow **non-permission action keys** for lifecycle events (like `session.*`): `case.close`, `case.set-subject`, `case.set-contact`, `change-request.confirm`, `change-request.cancel`, `approval.expire`. A direct apply by the CSR is recorded under the governing permission (`subscription.change-plan`, `Succeeded`). |
| R6 | **Reads use the token's `roles` claim,** which can stay stale for hours (renewal never re-derives it). And CM-9's performance rationale no longer holds, because every read already opens a transaction to write its audit record. | **Load the principal fresh from the database on every decision.** The token carries identity only (`sub`, `auth_time`, `sid`). This would supersede CM-9 and D1's `roles` claim. I know you agreed to CM-9; the audit-on-every-read decision (AT-3) removed its benefit. |
| R7 | **`session_id = jti` breaks on renewal:** each token has its own `jti` (RFC 7519). | Add an **`sid` claim** (the OpenID Connect convention) that survives renewals; `session_id` = `sid`. |
| R8 | **We overclaim "non-repudiation".** An HMAC chain proves integrity, but whoever holds the key can still forge records. | Say **"tamper evidence"**. Keep non-repudiation for the signed `audit_checkpoint` (production). |
| R9 | **Stretched terms.** XACML **obligations** are duties carried out *with* a decision (log, notify), not prerequisites. And GoF **Chain of Responsibility** stops at the first handler that acts. | Rename obligation → **prerequisite** (`prerequisite_kind: Approval`). Describe AU-1 as **"a pipeline of handlers (a Chain of Responsibility variant where every handler runs)"**. |
| R10 | **A stale change request is never closed.** And a refusal's audit record is lost when the transaction rolls back. | A stale request ends as `Canceled`, with reason `support.stale-change-request` (a new namespace, `support.`). A refusal's audit record is written in its own transaction after the rollback. |
| R11 | **Supervisors have no way to see or decide approvals** (S24, S25). | `ListPendingApprovals` and `DecideApproval`: HTTP endpoints plus a small UI panel, outside the LLM like confirmation. Closing a case also cancels any `AwaitingApproval` request and its process instance. |
| R12 | **Nothing defines how a case gets its subject and contact.** If the LLM picks the subject, an injected display name could steer it. | Use cases `SetCaseSubject` (an `account.read` decision, audited, **confirmed in the UI**) and `SetCaseContact`. |
| R13 | **CSR-initiated upgrades are free and unchecked:** a Tier 1 CSR could upgrade anyone to Enterprise. | A policy: upgrades above `pro` without a contact need approval. Or: plan changes require a contact. Interviewers will probe this. |
| R14 | **The schema has cycles between subsystems:** Entitlement → Support (a grant references its change request), Attribution → Support and Policy. | A **layer order**: identities → account → access and product → entitlement → policy → authorization → support, with attribution as a sink. Foreign keys only point downward; `*_by` → `account` is the one exception (Account is the kernel). Audit references to cases, change requests and policy sets become **soft references** (no foreign key). The grant–request link moves to Support. |
| R15 | **The Policy Information Point has no home.** It would make Policy depend on Support. | **Put the PIP in `authorization`**, which loads `principal` and `resource`. The calling use case passes a typed `context`. `policy` stays a pure evaluator. |
| R16 | **Holds and grant revocation lack permissions.** No permission places or releases a hold. `grant.revoke` is used for expiry, which is a different thing: in IAM, revocation means ending something early. | Add `account.place-hold` and `account.release-hold` (designed, not built). Rename the system permission `grant.record-expiry`. Either give employees a designed `grant.revoke`, or drop `Revoked`. |
| R17 | **Seed accounts with add-ons make EN-8 necessary:** Acme's add-on is affected by any plan change. | Slice 2 tests use accounts without add-ons (Initech, Hooli). EN-8 stays deferred. |
| R18 | **The enumeration naming rule:** `contact_verification_method` doesn't end in a role word. | Rename to `contact_verification_kind`. |

## B. Mechanical fixes (no ruling needed). I'll apply these on your go

**Stale or wrong text:**
- **The sign-in sequence diagram** is fully pre-Account (the `csr` table, `csrRole`, plain email in the audit). Redraw it.
- **Support model:** the state machine still says `RequiresApproval`; "require approval" wording; the interface row labeled Customer Identity should be Account.
- **The CI-2 table** is broken by the amendment row.
- **"The proposal's entitlement diff"** should be "the change request's".
- **"Seats counted from memberships"** should be "from role assignments".
- **AC-1 and the Access model** still say "forbid, or require approval".
- **II-1** still says "two principal kinds"; it needs an amendment pointing to AN-4.
- **The audit `detail` sample** says `explainMemberAccess`; the tool is `explainAccess`.
- **The constitution's `deny-by-default` row** still says "no policy permits"; it should cite the access handler (AU-3).

**Wording and accuracy:**
- **Glossary pointers:** "principal", "action key" and "PDP" point to documents that don't define them.
- **CM-3:** the WebSocket upgrade *does* carry headers. The limit is that browsers can't add `Authorization` to it.
- **CM-9:** "role" should be plural.
- **PO-5:** "hash the file" should be "canonicalize and hash the set".
- **The action-key template** wording should cite AC-3's two patterns.
- **AC-6's title** contradicts its amendment.
- **Internal Identity's "not addressed"** sentence is garbled.
- **The CI-6 index title** doesn't match its heading.
- **The proposal** calls a grant a "7-day trial" (that collides with `Trial` periods) and says "beyond the CSR's authority" where it means "beyond a policy limit".

**Id prefixes:** `case_` and `cas_` break the three-letter pattern. Use `cse_` for cases and `asg_` for case assignments.

**Slice 1 spec gaps:**
- **Ambiguous lookups** return candidates with no `account.read` decision. That leaks Enterprise accounts to Alex. Each candidate should be authorized, and denied candidates left out.
- **Audit expectations are missing** for S09–S12.
- **The add-on remedy rule** should be defined in the Product model.
- **S04's absolute-lifetime row** can't tell the 8-hour cap from ordinary expiry. Give it a token that's still valid but past the cap.
- **An empty password** must go through the same verification and be audited.
- **Individuals' accounts need display names.**
- **Identity and customer statuses** should be stated as `Active`.
- **Stark's canceled subscription** should read "last plan business".
- **Smaller wording fixes:** S05's Given, S12's "add-ons none", deduplicated matches, the last-period facts, S03's "no audit record", and S03's "foreign key" row (rename it "unknown signing key").

**Gaps to fill:**
- **A reason-code register:** one list, with missing codes added (a grant that adds nothing, `account.internal-identity-inactive`, `session.*`).
- **`change_request` columns:** add `confirmed_by`, plus end timestamps for its final statuses.
- **`audit_participant`:** allow the contact to equal the subject.
- **`approval_granted`:** keyed per prerequisite.
- **Optimistic locking:** `UPDATE … WHERE version = $expected`, with ownership of the `version` bump defined.
- **Hyperdrive:** disable its query caching for decision reads.
- **Security rules:**
  - the Agents SDK state is never client-writable
  - the server closes the WebSocket at token expiry
  - the UI renders escaped output under a strict Content Security Policy, and loads no remote images (an exfiltration path for injected prompts)
  - sign-in verifies a dummy hash for unknown emails.
- **Policy test cases** move next to the policies, in the application's tests: a subsystem must not depend on the application.

**Production-readiness additions:**
- the cost of the audit-chain lock on every read
- session-key rotation (`kid`)
- LLM-written text stored as untrusted data
- approval escalation (the seed has one supervisor, so Sam's own requests can never be approved)
- insider enumeration and rate limits
- migration ordering across subsystems
- `OrganizationMember` accounts becoming a dead end after an individual leaves.

## How to proceed

Rule on **R1–R18**: one word per item is enough where you agree. I'll then apply your rulings together with **all of section B** in one pass, as ADRs, amendments and document updates.

### Assistant

You asked for a visual of where each concept comes into play, so I drew one first: an activity diagram of the authorization decision.

- tool `Write`: ../entitlement-agent/tech-docs/diagrams/authorization-decision.activity.puml

- tool `Bash`: Render the authorization decision activity diagram

- tool `Read`: ../entitlement-agent/tech-docs/diagrams/authorization-decision.png

- tool `Bash`: Remove the PNG used for visual check

### Assistant

The diagram is at `tech-docs/diagrams/authorization-decision.activity.puml` (rendered `.svg`). It walks one decision through the five steps and shows where each concept enters:
1. **Account holds**
2. **Roles → permissions** (Access)
3. **The contact's permission**
4. **Subscription → access period → plan, add-ons, grants → the feature** (Entitlement)
5. **Policies and their requirements**

Then the decision, then the audit record. A legend defines permission, entitlement, grant, policy and access period in one line each. Answers to the rest:

## R1: re-checking an approved change

Your understanding is right: **it re-evaluates the decision the way it was first made, but now with the approval in the context.** Between the request and the approval, up to 72 hours can pass, and either check can catch a change:

- **(a) Re-run the original decision for the requester,** with their *current* state and `approval_granted = true`. It catches anything that changed in between:
  - Alex was demoted or suspended
  - the subject account got a hold
  - the subscription lost coverage
  - a new policy now denies it (another policy, like the 90-day cap, still applies).

  So yes, it checks that **permissions and conditions still hold**, not just that the approval happened.
- **(b) The system account may still execute it:** `sys_approval_process` holds `change-request.apply` and isn't suspended. That catches a system identity that was disabled (a misbehaving process).
- **Also:** the entitlement diff is recomputed against the current state, so the record shows what was actually applied.

If either check fails, the change becomes `Refused` with its reason codes, and the case is told.

## R2 and R18: what "verification" means here, and a better word

**R2 explained:** the starting policy set includes `contact-is-verified`, which denies *any* change asked for by a contact unless the contact's verification is recorded. Recording verification is stretch scenario S27. So in Slice 2, every change asked for by a contact would be denied, which is why I suggested leaving that policy out until S27.

**On the word:** you're right. What a CSR does is **authentication**: checking the person against something already registered (a knowledge factor, a one-time code sent to a registered phone or email). "Identity verification" in industry usually means **identity proofing**, checking documents when someone first registers. Contact centers call this **caller authentication**, or customer authentication. So:

```text
support_case
  contact_authenticated_at
  contact_authentication_kind      KnowledgeFactor | OneTimeCode
  contact_authenticated_by
policy: contact-is-authenticated
```

This replaces "verification" everywhere, and it also settles R18 (`_kind` instead of `_method`).

## R3: what `FreePlan` is, and how periods are stored

**`FreePlan` is a value of an access period's `source`**: "this span is covered because the plan costs nothing". The table (Entitlement):

```text
subscription_access_period
  period_id         uuid
  subscription_id   → subscription
  source            FreePlan | Payment | Contract | Trial | Courtesy | Complimentary
  starts_at
  ends_at           empty = open-ended
  granted_by        → account
```

**How periods are managed:** rows are added by billing (`Payment`), by contracts (`Contract`), by trials, by a CSR (`Courtesy`), or by policy (`Complimentary`). A subscription works whenever any row covers the current instant.

**The problem:** Initech's Free subscription has an open-ended `FreePlan` row. After an upgrade to Pro, that row would keep covering Pro forever, for free. Two parts to the fix:
- **The catalog must know which plans are free.** That needs a small Product column, `plan.plan_category` (`Free` or `Paid`), since prices aren't modeled.
- **A `FreePlan` period covers only a `Free` plan.** On an upgrade, the `FreePlan` period **ends** (`ended_at`, `ended_by`, `end_reason = PlanChanged`, the same pattern as our other facts), and the change must add the new covering period in the same transaction. In the demo that's `Trial` or `Courtesy`; in production, billing's `Payment`. A downgrade to Free adds an open-ended `FreePlan` period.

## R4: `account.read` vs `audit.read`

Both are reads of the same subject account, at different depths:
- **`account.read`:** its **current state** (plan, entitlements, people, holds).
- **`audit.read`:** its **history** (audit records about it): more sensitive, and a separate permission.

**The relationship under R4:** every use case touching a subject asks for `account.read` first, as a gate. Reading history therefore needs **both**: `account.read` (so the Enterprise policy applies) and then `audit.read`. That closes the bypass without repeating the Enterprise rule on every action.

## R6: what's in the CSR's session token

Yes, it's the **CSR's session token** (the cookie). The lifetime part is already as you describe: short-lived (30 minutes), renewed on activity, capped at 8 hours. Your **explicit keep-alive endpoint** is a good addition, and it's actually needed: chat messages travel over the WebSocket, which can't set cookies, so the UI must call `POST /api/session/renew` to keep the session alive (CM-9 already says "the UI renews over HTTP").

R6 is about **what the token contains**, not how long it lives:
- **Today (D1, CM-9):** the token carries the CSR's `roles`, and reads trust them. If Alex is demoted or suspended, reads keep using the old roles until the next renewal, and nothing re-derives them on renewal either.
- **Proposed:** the token says only **who** (`sub` = the internal account, `sid`, `auth_time`). On every decision, the PDP loads current roles, holds and status from the database. The cost is close to nothing, because every decision already goes to the database to write its audit record.

You're right that it remains a bearer token either way. Binding the token to the client (DPoP, or mTLS-bound tokens) would be the production step, and I'll note it.

## R10: when a change request becomes stale

When the **subject account changes between proposal and confirmation**: another change applied, a grant created or expired, a hold placed. The `subject_account_version` no longer matches, or the diff's `test` operations fail. The request can't be applied as shown, so it's closed (`Canceled`, `support.stale-change-request`) and the assistant proposes a fresh one.

**The link to the audit chain** is indirect. A refused confirmation is a decision, and every decision is audited (AT-3). But the confirmation runs in a transaction that **rolls back** on refusal, and that rollback would erase the refusal's audit record with it. So the refusal is recorded in its own transaction, after the rollback.

## R12: the contact's authority

Yes, **both** are required:
1. **The contact is authenticated:** the columns above, with policy `contact-is-authenticated`.
2. **The contact holds a customer permission in the subject account** through a role assignment (SU-8), for example Jane, admin at Acme, holds `grant.request`.

One consequence: with R2, Slice 2 checks (2) but not (1). A contact who was only *identified* could ask for changes. The alternative is **minimal authentication in Slice 2**: the CSR records the method (a knowledge factor), and the policy is active from the start. Given the IAM audience, **I'd now recommend that instead of deferring**: the columns already exist, so it's one use case plus one policy.

## R14: the layer order, and whether it changes the schema

Your interpretation is exactly right:

```text
customer identity, internal identity      depend on no one
  → account                               depends on the identities
    → access, product                     depend on account
      → entitlement                       depends on account, product
        → policy                          a pure evaluator
          → authorization                 combines account, access, entitlement, policy
            → support                     uses authorization and entitlement
attribution                               a sink: everyone writes to it; it depends on no one's tables
```

It's both a rule and a **small schema change**. Foreign keys may only point **downward**. Three references point upward today:

| Today | Change |
| --- | --- |
| `entitlement_grant.change_request_id` → Support | **Remove it.** Support records the link instead: `change_request.created_grant_id` → `entitlement_grant` (downward) |
| `audit_chain_record` → `support_case`, `change_request`, `policy_set` | Keep the columns, **without foreign keys** (soft references). The audit must accept a record regardless of the state of other tables |
| `*_by` → `account` everywhere | **The one sanctioned exception:** Account is the kernel every subsystem attributes to |

## R15: PIP, PDP, PEP, PAP

**No: the policies are registered in Policy.** Authorization hosts the **decision** (PDP) and the **attribute loading** (PIP). Glossary entries I'd add:

| Term | Standard meaning | Subsystem in our design | Used by |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **PEP** (Policy Enforcement Point) | Intercepts a request, asks for a decision, enforces it | each subsystem's **use cases** | every use case, before reading or changing |
| **PDP** (Policy Decision Point) | Computes the decision | **authorization** (the decision pipeline) | the PEPs |
| **PIP** (Policy Information Point) | Supplies the attributes | **authorization** (loads principal and resource; the PEP passes the context) | the PDP |
| **PAP** (Policy Administration Point) | Where policies are authored and managed | the **policy files** under version control, plus **registration and activation** at deployment (Policy subsystem) | authors, the deployment |
| **PRP** (Policy Retrieval Point) | Where the PDP gets the active policies | the **policy set bundled into the Worker**, checked against the active set (Policy) | the PDP |

## R16: what `grant.revoke` would mean

Yes: **explicit, early revocation**. An employee ends a grant before its expiry, with `end_reason = Revoked`. Typical uses:
- the grant was given by mistake
- it's being abused
- the customer asked to stop it
- a security event.

It would be a change-request kind (`RevokeGrant`) with its own permission and audit record. I'd keep it **designed, not built**. The system's expiry recording becomes `grant.record-expiry`, so "revoke" keeps its industry meaning.

## Recorded

- **Agreed:** R5, R7, R8, R9, R11, R13 (the "O," taken as ok), R17.
- **R18:** `_kind`, plus "authentication" instead of "verification".

**Still to rule:**
1. **R1:** the two checks plus a recomputed diff?
2. **R2 and R12:** "contact authentication" as the term; build minimal authentication in Slice 2, rather than deferring the policy?
3. **R3:** `plan.plan_category` (`Free` or `Paid`); a `FreePlan` period covers only Free plans, and ends on an upgrade (`end_reason PlanChanged`)?
4. **R4:** the `account.read` gate before every subject-touching use case, `audit.read` included?
5. **R6:** an identity-only token; roles and holds loaded fresh per decision; `POST /api/session/renew` (this supersedes CM-9)?
6. **R10:** stale requests become `Canceled` with `support.stale-change-request`; refusals audited in their own transaction?
7. **R14:** the layer order, with the three reference changes?
8. **R15:** PIP and PDP in authorization; the PEP/PDP/PIP/PAP/PRP glossary entries?
9. **R16:** `grant.revoke` designed (explicit revocation); `grant.record-expiry` for the system?

### Assistant

## R3: plan vs access period, and whether to merge them

**The difference today.** There are two independent timelines on one subscription:
- **`subscription_plan`** answers ***which*** plan the account is on, from when to when (Free, then Pro from Oct 10).
- **`subscription_access_period`** answers ***whether*** the subscription is covered (paid, on trial, under contract, by courtesy) from when to when.

We designed it during Entitlement. The name `subscription_access_period` was yours, and so was choosing option (B), open-ended periods. They were kept separate because a plan can change mid-period, and coverage can lapse while the plan stays the same.

**Organization subscriptions.** They work exactly that way: **an organization's account is always `Standalone`** (AN-2, enforced by a check constraint), so Acme's subscription is simply the subscription on `acc_acme`. Only `OrganizationMember` accounts can't subscribe: they belong to individuals who use their organization's plan. Every organization can subscribe.

**Merging them: I think your instinct is better.** Two overlapping timelines are what caused the `FreePlan` confusion in the first place. One timeline of **terms**, each saying which plan and why it's covered, is clearer:

```text
subscription                      lifecycle: Active | Canceled
  subscription_id, account_id, subscription_status, started_at

subscription_term                 fact in time: one plan, one reason for coverage, one span
  term_id
  subscription_id   → subscription
  plan_key          → plan
  source            FreePlan | Payment | Contract | Trial | Courtesy | Complimentary
  starts_at
  ends_at           empty = open-ended
  ended_at, ended_by, end_reason   (PlanChanged | SubscriptionCanceled), when a term is cut short
  granted_by        → account
```

| Situation | Terms |
| --- | --- |
| Initech on Free | one term: `free`, `FreePlan`, open-ended |
| Upgrade to Pro on Oct 10 | the `FreePlan` term is cut short (`PlanChanged`); a new term starts: `pro`, `Trial` (demo) or `Payment` (production) |
| Monthly renewal | a new `pro` / `Payment` term, starting where the last one ends |
| Goodwill during a billing problem | a `pro` / `Courtesy` term, up to 14 days |
| Enterprise contract | one term: `enterprise`, `Contract`, open-ended |

**The rules:**
- **The subscription works when a term covers the current instant,** and **the current plan is that term's plan.** One question instead of two.
- **Terms never overlap,** enforced by a check in the change use case (and a verification test).
- **`FreePlan` terms only for free plans:** that still needs `plan.plan_category` (`Free` or `Paid`) on the catalog.
- **Add-ons and grants stay as they are.** Each is its own fact in time on the subscription.

**The trade-off:** a mid-month upgrade splits the current term in two. In production, billing would prorate (already on the production-readiness list).

This would **supersede EN-2 and EN-3** with one new ADR, and update the Entitlement diagram, model and the Slice 1 fixture: each organization gets terms instead of a plan plus periods.

## R4: why read history at all?

Because support often needs **what happened**, not just **what is**:
- *"Acme lost SSO yesterday. Why?"*
- *"Who upgraded Initech, and was it approved?"*

The answer comes from the audit records about that account. That's Slice 2's scenario **S20 (account history)**, and `audit.read` is its permission. It's a separate permission because history is more sensitive than current state: it shows who did what, including other employees.

If you'd rather not offer history, we drop S20 and `audit.read`. **I'd keep it:** it's one read use case, and it demonstrates attribution end to end, which matters for an IAM audience.

## R14: the references

**Entitlement ↔ Support:** yes. Support will point to Entitlement (`change_request.created_grant_id`), and the grant no longer points back.

**Why keep the audit's references to cases, change requests and policy sets without foreign keys:**
1. **Direction:** Attribution is the lowest layer, since everyone writes to it. A foreign key from the audit to Support makes Attribution's tables depend on Support's, so its migrations and tests can't stand alone.
2. **Different lifetimes:** audit records are kept longest. A foreign key would forbid ever archiving or moving the referenced tables (another database, a partitioned archive) while the audit lives on.
3. **The audit must never fail for reasons outside itself:** a fail-closed audit (AT-3) shouldn't be blocked by a referential check on another subsystem's table.
4. **Nothing is lost in practice:** the referenced ids are immutable and never deleted (CM-7), so a soft reference can't go stale.

**The `*_by` exception, clarified.** You're right that almost every `*_by` column points *down* to `account`, which is fine. The exception is **one place: Customer Identity's `organization_membership.ended_by`**. Account depends on Customer Identity (`customer_account` → `customer`), and that column makes Customer Identity point *up* to Account: a cycle.

**Better than an exception:** make that one column a **soft reference** too. Then the rule has **no exceptions**: foreign keys only point downward, and any column that has to point upward is a soft reference.

## R15: the PEP

Exactly. **Enforcement points are decentralized:** each use case enforces at the boundary of what it protects, because only it knows the action, the subject and the context. That's the standard shape: one PDP, many PEPs. Consistency comes from three things:
- **One shared building block:** every use case runs through the same template (Template Method: authorize, then execute, then audit), so no use case can skip the decision.
- **A rule in the register,** `llm-never-authorizes`, guarded by an architecture test that every use case calls the PDP.
- **The decision itself** stays centralized in authorization.

The PEP/PDP/PIP/PAP/PRP table goes into the glossary, as you asked.

## Recorded

- "Authentication" replaces "verification"
- R6: an identity-only token, the principal loaded fresh, a renewal endpoint
- R10
- R12: minimal contact authentication in Slice 2
- R16
- the R15 table into the glossary.

**To rule:**
1. **R3:** merge plan and coverage into `subscription_term` (superseding EN-2 and EN-3), with `plan.plan_category`?
2. **R4:** keep history (S20, `audit.read`) behind the `account.read` gate?
3. **R14:** foreign keys only downward; the audit references and `organization_membership.ended_by` become soft references, with no exceptions?
4. **R1** (the two checks plus a recomputed diff) is still open from my last message.

### Assistant

## R3: refining terms

You've found a real inconsistency, and it makes the model cleaner.

**The subscription ends: move `ended_at` to it.** Agreed. It also corrects something: `Canceled` is final (subscribing again creates a new subscription), so by **CM-11** a subscription is a **fact in time**, not a lifecycle. It should have timestamps, not a status:

```text
subscription
  subscription_id, account_id
  started_at
  ended_at, ended_by, end_reason     (Canceled | CustomerRemoved); empty while in force
```

`subscription_status` goes away; "in force" means `ended_at` is empty. One clarification: **unpaid isn't an end.** When payment stops, coverage lapses (no term covers now), but the subscription stays in force until someone cancels it. Billing would decide when that happens.

**No overlapping terms:** agreed. Terms are sequential, with gaps allowed. A term cut short says so with `ended_at`, `ended_by` and `end_reason`.

**`ends_at`:** your reading is exactly right, so I'd rename it to say so:

| Column | Meaning | Changes? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `scheduled_end_at` | when the term is **planned** to end | **never** (immutable) |
| `ended_at` | when it **actually** ended, if earlier | set once, if cut short |

Can `scheduled_end_at` be **never empty**? For most sources, yes: `Payment`, `Trial` and `Courtesy` always have a planned end, and a check constraint can require it. But `FreePlan`, `Contract` and `Complimentary` are often genuinely open-ended. Representing that without an empty value needs either PostgreSQL's `'infinity'` (PostgreSQL-specific, which conflicts with your portability concern) or a fake far-future date (misleading). **My recommendation:** empty is allowed **only** for those three sources (a check constraint), and means "no planned end".

**The active term** is the one where `starts_at ≤ now` and now is before its effective end: `ended_at` if set, otherwise `scheduled_end_at`, otherwise no end. With no overlaps, there's at most one. "No overlaps" spans rows, so (CM-13) it's guarded by the term use case and a verification test. PostgreSQL's exclusion constraints could enforce it in the database, but they're PostgreSQL-specific.

**`granted_by`** was meant as **who created the term record**, not who pays. **The payer is always the subject account's customer**, and payment itself is out of scope. Who creates terms:

| Source | Created by |
| --- | --- |
| `Payment` | the billing integration: a **system account** (a `sys_billing` identity in production; seeded in the demo) |
| `Courtesy`, `Trial`, plan changes | the **employee** whose change request was applied, or `sys_approval_process` after an approval |
| `FreePlan`, `Contract`, `Complimentary` | an employee, a system account, or seeding |

So it's always an **internal account**, and system accounts are included. Customers never create terms directly; they ask, and an employee acts. I'd rename it to **`created_by`**, since "granted" belongs to entitlement grants.

## R4

Recorded: history stays (S20, `audit.read`, behind the `account.read` gate).

## R14: is Attribution like Identity?

**In one way, yes:** both **depend on no one**. They differ in direction:
- **Identity is a foundation:** others build on it and point to it.
- **Attribution is a sink:** others write *into* it, and it points out only softly.

Both are safe ends of the dependency graph. I'll write the layer order, the downward-only rule and the soft references into an ADR.

## `organization_membership.ended_by`: who ended it?

**Potentially any kind of account:**
- an **employee** removing someone on request
- an organization's **owner** removing a member, through the product
- a **system** process, for example deprovisioning.

So the identity-id options you list would need a qualifier column, because several kinds of identity are possible. That reintroduces what accounts were meant to unify.

Your "does it belong elsewhere?" instinct points to the cleanest answer: **drop `ended_by` from the membership altogether.** Every change is audited (AT-3), so *who* ended a membership is already recorded: the audit record for that action names the actor, with the membership as its subject. Customer Identity then keeps **only identity facts**: no `*_by` columns, no reference upward, no soft reference to explain.

| Option | Cost |
| --- | --- |
| Keep `ended_by` as a soft reference to `account` | a reference that's checked by nothing |
| Identity id plus a qualifier column | two columns, and it breaks "attribution names accounts" |
| **Drop it; the audit records who** | none: the answer is one query on the audit chain |

**My recommendation: drop it.** The same principle would apply to any future identity-level change.

## To rule

1. **R3:** a subscription as a fact in time (`ended_at`, `ended_by`, `end_reason`; no status); `subscription_term` with no overlaps, an immutable `scheduled_end_at` (empty only for `FreePlan`, `Contract` and `Complimentary`), `ended_at` when cut short, and `created_by` (internal accounts); `plan.plan_category`. This supersedes EN-1, EN-2 and EN-3.
2. **R14:** the layer order, foreign keys only downward, soft references for the audit's links, and **dropping `organization_membership.ended_by`** (the audit records who).
3. **R1** (still open): applying an approved change re-runs the requester's original decision with `approval_granted`, checks that the system account may execute, and recomputes the diff.

## New and superseded ADRs

| Record | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| **CM-15** | Foreign keys point down the layer order; upward links are soft references. No exceptions: `organization_membership.ended_by` is dropped, and the audit records who ended a membership |
| **CM-16** *(supersedes CM-9)* | The session token carries identity only (`sub`, `sid`, `auth_time`). The principal is loaded fresh for every decision. `POST /api/session/renew` keeps a session alive |
| **EN-9** *(supersedes EN-1)* | A subscription is a fact in time (`ended_at`, `ended_by`, `end_reason`) |
| **EN-10** *(supersedes EN-2, EN-3)* | `subscription_term`: one timeline of plan and coverage. Terms never overlap; `scheduled_end_at` is immutable; `ended_at` when cut short; `created_by` is an internal account |
| **PR-9, PR-10** | `plan_category` (`Free` or `Paid`); the remedy rule |
| **SU-9 to SU-13** | Lifecycle use cases with action keys (subject confirmed in the UI); contact authentication; re-running the decision when an approved change is applied; the approvals panel outside the chat; stale requests canceled, refusals audited in their own transaction |
| **AU-4, AU-5** | The `account.read` gate on every subject-touching use case; the PDP and PIP in Authorization, with use cases as PEPs |

There are 20 dated amendments, including:
- **wording:** a pipeline of handlers; tamper evidence rather than non-repudiation; prerequisites rather than obligations
- **`sid` replaces `jti`** for grouping a session's records
- **new action keys** for lifecycle events, and a new `support.` reason namespace
- **new permissions:** `grant.record-expiry`, plus the designed `grant.revoke`, `account.place-hold` and `account.release-hold`
- **retitled records:** AC-6 and PO-3 now match their amended meaning, with every link updated.

## Models, diagrams and other documents

- **Diagrams:**
  - Entitlement is reworked around `subscription_term`.
  - Product, Customer Identity, Support, Attribution (soft references) and Policy (`policy_prerequisite`) are updated.
  - **The sign-in sequence is redrawn** for the current design.
  - The authorization activity diagram uses terms instead of periods.
- **Models:**
  - **Entitlement and Support are rewritten.** Support now covers the life of a case and of a change request, approvals and the version check.
  - **Policy** has the new starting set, including `contact-is-authenticated` and `upgrade-beyond-pro-needs-contact-or-approval`, plus optional attributes tested with `has()`.
  - **Attribution** has a **reason-code register** (15 codes).
  - **Account** has `Principal` and the session rules.
  - **Access, Product and both identity models** are updated.
- **The proposal:** the glossary gains the **PEP/PDP/PIP/PAP/PRP table** you asked for, and the pointers are fixed. Slice 2 gains contact authentication (S27 moved in), stale requests (S16a) and upgrade approval (S16b).
- **The Slice 1 spec** is rewritten:
  - tokens carry identity only
  - terms replace periods
  - lookup candidates are each authorized (Alex no longer sees Umbrella)
  - audit expectations on every scenario
  - display names, a fixed S04 row, and the remaining reviewer gaps.
- **Constitution and guides:**
  - The rule register gets a corrected `deny-by-default` row and three new rules: `audit-fails-closed`, `foreign-keys-point-downward`, `principal-loaded-fresh`.
  - The architecture guide has the layer order, the PDP and PIP placement, and the unit of work.
  - The developer guide has security rules: no client-writable agent state, the WebSocket closed at token expiry, escaped output under a strict Content Security Policy, confirmations the model can't trigger, no Hyperdrive caching for decisions, and a dummy hash at sign-in.
- **Production readiness** gained ten items, among them bound tokens (DPoP), key rotation, approval escalation, insider enumeration, the audit cost on the read path, migration ordering, and `OrganizationMember` accounts after someone leaves.
